# EPQ Research Compendium: Multilateral Cooperation and Chokepoint Disruption

20 October 2026 · Manuela López-Henares · Live version: https://claude.ai/code/artifact/502ddf95-6214-4efa-8ccf-8c8bfaf06e76

## Headline findings

Multilateral cooperation was not the main reason disruption risk changed in any of the three case studies. It softened severity at the margin, and how well it worked depended on the type of threat.

| Case study | Risk (CCRI, 1–5), earlier → later | Cooperation index (0–20) | Cooperation's share of the change | Verdict |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Strait of Hormuz (Tanker War 1984–88 vs 2026 closure) | 3.49 → 3.75 (rose) | 9 → 12 | 5.7%, the smallest of nine causes | Minor reason |
| Suez / Red Sea (1967–75 closure vs 2023–26 Houthi crisis) | 4.22 → 3.30 (fell) | 5 → 13 | 8.5%, partly negative | Minor reason |
| Strait of Malacca (2000–05 peak vs 2008) | 2.37 → 1.53 (fell) | 6 → 13 | 15.8–23.7% | Major reason among several |

- **Hormuz:** risk rose by 7% even though cooperation increased. Iran's own decision to close the strait, the concentration of about 20 million barrels a day through it, and the change in the US role mattered more. The IEA's 400-million-barrel stock release and the JMIC information centre softened the rise.
- **Suez / Red Sea:** risk fell because a non-state group cannot shut a canal the way Egypt did in 1967, so the type of threat explains most of the change. The January 2024 strikes on the Houthis were followed by a tripling of war-risk premiums, so some cooperation made things worse.
- **Malacca:** the joint patrols tie with the 2004 tsunami as the largest cause of the fall in attacks. The steepest fall (75 to 16 attacks, 2000–02) came before any joint patrol. Cooperation becomes the main reason only if each navy's patrols in its own waters count as cooperation.

**Thesis this evidence supports:** cooperation works best against small-scale criminal threats, where it supports national enforcement. It works least against states (Iran, Egypt) and armed groups (the Houthis), whose decisions drive both likelihood and severity.

## Method

Risk is measured with the Cooperation-Adjusted Chokepoint Risk Index (CCRI), built on the structure of the EU's INFORM Risk Index, and causes are tested with process tracing.

```latex
R = \left(L \times S \times K\right)^{1/3}, \qquad K = 5 - \frac{C}{5}, \qquad 1 \le R \le 5
```

| Term | Meaning | Indicators (each scored 1–5 on fixed bands) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L | Likelihood of disruption | Incidents per year; war-risk insurance premium (% of hull value) |
| S | Severity of disruption | Fall in transits; length of disruption; Brent price rise; freight-rate rise |
| C | Multilateral cooperation index (0–20) | Formal frameworks, joint operations, information sharing, response speed, enforcement (each 0–4) |
| K | Lack of coping capacity | 5 with no cooperation, falling to 1 at full cooperation |
| R | Risk | 1.0–1.9 Low, 2.0–2.9 Moderate, 3.0–3.9 High, 4.0–5.0 Very high |

**Why this equation:** risk as likelihood × consequence follows ISO 31000. The geometric mean with a coping-capacity term follows INFORM (Marin-Ferrer, Vernaccini and Poljansek, 2017). Banded scores and a sensitivity test follow the OECD/JRC (2008) handbook on composite indicators, and averaging several indicators answers Cox's (2008) critique of plain risk matrices. The simpler version 1, R = L × S on a 1–25 scale, gives the same verdicts.

**Separating cooperation's effect:** the counterfactual keeps the later L and S but uses the earlier K, which shows what risk would have been without the extra cooperation. The K term assumes that cooperation lowers risk, so it gives the best case for cooperation, not proof.

**Testing causes (process tracing):** each possible cause is scored 0–2 on four tests (George and Bennett, 2005): change, timing, mechanism and counterfactual. A cause's share is its score divided by the total. Cooperation is the main reason only if its share is the largest and at least 1.5 times the next.

**Grading sources:** every source has an Admiralty Code grade (NATO AJP-2.1). The letter rates the producer: A completely reliable, B usually reliable, C fairly reliable, D not usually reliable, E unreliable, F cannot be judged. The number rates the content: 1 confirmed, 2 probably true, 3 possibly true, 4 doubtful, 5 improbable, 6 cannot be judged.

## Results

Risk rose at Hormuz and fell at Suez and Malacca. In every case, the counterfactual shows that extra cooperation lowered risk by no more than 0.61 points.

| Case | Earlier R | Later R | Later R if cooperation had not increased |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Hormuz: Tanker War → 2026 | 3.49 | 3.75 | 4.02 |
| Suez/Red Sea: 1967–75 → 2023–26 | 4.22 | 3.30 | 3.91 |
| Malacca: peak 2000–05 → 2008 | 2.37 | 1.53 | 1.79 |
| Malacca: 2025 → 2026 | 1.71 | 1.59 | 1.59 |

| Case and period | L | S | C | K |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Hormuz: Tanker War 1984–88 | 5.00 | 2.67 | 9 | 3.20 |
| Hormuz: 2026 closure | 4.50 | 4.50 | 12 | 2.60 |
| Suez: 1967–75 closure | 5.00 | 3.75 | 5 | 4.00 |
| Red Sea: 2023–26 Houthi crisis | 4.00 | 3.75 | 13 | 2.40 |
| Malacca: peak 2000–05 | 3.50 | 1.00 | 6 | 3.80 |
| Malacca: 2008 | 1.50 | 1.00 | 13 | 2.40 |
| Malacca: 2025 | 2.50 | 1.00 | 15 | 2.00 |
| Malacca: 2026 (Jan–Sep) | 2.00 | 1.00 | 15 | 2.00 |

| Case | Largest causes (share) | Cooperation's share |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hormuz | Type of threat 15.1%; trade concentration 13.2%; US unilateral role 13.2% | 5.7% |
| Suez / Red Sea | Type of threat 17.0%; connected crises 17.0%; technology 14.9% | 8.5% (sign mixed) |
| Malacca, peak → 2008 | Joint patrols 15.8% = 2004 tsunami 15.8%; national enforcement, Aceh peace, economy, Lloyd's listing 13.2% each | 15.8–23.7% |
| Malacca, 2025 → 2026 | Indonesian police enforcement 36.4%; ReCAAP information sharing 27.3% | 13.6% (patrols) + 27.3% (information) |

| Sensitivity test | Result | Verdict changes? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Version 1 equation (R = L × S) | Same direction in every case | No |
| Suez 1967 likelihood scored 4 instead of 5 | Risk falls by 0.61 instead of 0.92 | No |
| Drop the 2026 Hormuz freight score | Later risk 3.70 instead of 3.75 | No |
| Malacca Strait alone, without the Singapore Strait | 2025 and 2026 risk 1.26 | No |
| Count national patrols as cooperation at Malacca | Cooperation share 36.8%, 2.3 times the next cause | **Yes** |

## Sources: the links in your original plan

16 of your 34 links are graded A1–B2, good enough for figures. Replace the D-graded reposts with the originals, and open the F- and 6-graded links before you use them.

### Hormuz

- **Kharroubi, E. (2026). Maritime chokepoints and the global economy: evidence from the Strait of Hormuz.** BIS Working Papers No. 1378. [link](https://www.bis.org/publications/working-paper-1378-maritime-chokepoints-and-the-global-economy-evidence-strait-hormuz) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Uses ship-tracking data to show that Hormuz disruption pushes up energy and fertiliser prices, weakens world industry and raises consumer prices after about 4–6 months, for up to a year. Reliability: A respected central-bank research body, but it is a working paper that has not been peer-reviewed and was seen only through search snippets.

- **Hellenic Shipping News (n.d.). [Title not confirmed].** Hellenic Shipping News Worldwide. [link](https://www.hellenicshippingnews.com/?p=1131337) — **Grade D6**. Summary: Content unknown, because this post could not be found in searches. Reliability: A Greek shipping site that mostly reposts other outlets' reports, and this post was never seen, so it cannot be trusted yet. Fix: open it, check it is the post you meant, and cite the original source it reposts.

- **Ministry of Defense of Japan (2026). Joint statement on the Multinational Military Mission for the Strait of Hormuz.** MOD Japan. [link](https://www.mod.go.jp/j/approach/exchange/area/2026/pdf/20260514_gbr-j_b.pdf) — **Grade B2**. Summary: About 25 countries, led by the UK and France, commit to diplomatic, economic and military steps to keep shipping moving through Hormuz. Reliability: An official government document, so reliable for what was agreed, but it shows intentions, not whether the mission worked. Fix: this is a multinational statement (12 May 2026), not a Japan–UK meeting.

- **International Energy Agency (2026). IEA Member countries to carry out largest ever oil stock release amid market disruptions from Middle East conflict.** IEA press release. [link](https://www.iea.org/news/iea-member-countries-to-carry-out-largest-ever-oil-stock-release-amid-market-disruptions-from-middle-east-conflict/) — **Grade A1**. Summary: On 11 March 2026 all 32 IEA members agreed to release 400 million barrels of emergency oil, the largest release ever, because Hormuz exports had fallen below 10% of normal. Reliability: The official announcement from the body that took the decision, widely confirmed, though it is written to calm markets and does not show the effect.

- **Gulf News (2026). Hormuz ship crossings jump 44%: marine traffic tracker.** Gulf News. Summary: Daily crossings rose from 9 to 13 in mid-August 2026, compared with about 100–140 a day before the war. Reliability: A UAE newspaper passing on commercial tracking data, and reports disagree on whether the data came from MarineTraffic or Kpler. Fix: 13 crossings is still about 90% below normal, so do not call it a recovery; check the exact date.

- **Talmadge, C. (2008). Closing Time: Assessing the Iranian Threat to the Strait of Hormuz.** International Security, 33(1). Summary: Argues Iran probably could not keep the strait fully closed for long, but mines, missiles and small boats could cause a long, costly fight. Reliability: Peer-reviewed article in a top security journal and free to read, though it was written in 2008 and takes a US point of view.

- **O'Neil, W.D. and Talmadge, C. (2008/09). Correspondence: Costs and Difficulties of Blocking the Strait of Hormuz.** International Security, 33(3). Summary: O'Neil argues Talmadge should have given more weight to the costs Iran itself would face from closing the strait, and she replies. ### Suez and Red Sea

- **Wikipedia (n.d.). 1974 Suez Canal Clearance Operation.** Wikipedia. [link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Suez_Canal_Clearance_Operation) — **Grade D2**. Summary: Outlines the US-led mine and wreck clearance (Nimbus Star, Nimbus Moon, Nimrod Spar) that let the canal reopen on 5 June 1975 after eight years shut. Reliability: Anyone can edit it and there is no named author, though its facts match US Navy sources. Fix: use for background only; cite links 23 and 27 in the essay.

- **Boyd, J.H., Jr. (1976). Nimrod Spar: Clearing the Suez Canal.** U.S. Naval Institute Proceedings. [link](https://www.usni.org/magazines/proceedings/1976/february/nimrod-spar-clearing-suez-canal) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The US Navy officer who ran the job describes removing ten wrecks from the canal, paid for by the US. Reliability: A first-hand account by the person in charge, so it may praise the operation and show a US view; the web text may have scanning errors. Fix: date it February 1976 (not January).

- **Feyrer, J. (2021). Distance, trade, and income – The 1967 to 1975 closing of the Suez Canal as a natural experiment.** Journal of Development Economics, 153. [link](https://ideas.repec.org/p/red/sed011/1438.html) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Uses the 1967–75 closure to show that distance cuts trade about half as much as older estimates said, and that trade really does raise income. Reliability: A peer-reviewed economics study by a Dartmouth economist, though only the abstract was seen. Fix: the link is the 2011 working paper; cite the 2021 journal version (DOI 10.1016/j.jdeveco.2021.102708).

- **Feyrer, J. (2009). 1967–75 Suez Canal closure: Lessons for trade and the trade-income link.** VoxEU/CEPR. [link](https://cepr.org/voxeu/columns/1967-75-suez-canal-closure-lessons-trade-and-trade-income-link) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A short, readable version of Feyrer's research: a 10% fall in sea distance goes with about a 5% rise in trade, and each extra dollar of trade adds about 25 cents of income. Reliability: Written by the researcher himself, but not peer-reviewed and not independent of his paper. Fix: use this clean URL without "index%2ephp".

- **Notteboom, T., Haralambides, H. and Cullinane, K. (2024). The Red Sea Crisis: ramifications for vessel operations, shipping networks, and maritime supply chains.** Maritime Economics & Logistics, 26(1). [link](https://marineinfo.org/doc/publication/391353) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Leading port economists explain how Houthi attacks forced ships around the Cape of Good Hope and how that changed shipping routes and supply chains. Reliability: Top experts in a leading journal, but it is an editorial, so probably not fully refereed, and the full text was not seen. Fix: cite via the DOI 10.1057/s41278-024-00287-z.

- **Commander Task Force 65 (1975). CTF Sixty-Five Final Report: Suez Canal Clearance Operations Nimbus Star, Nimbus Moon, Nimrod Spar.** DTIC AD-A010 261. [link](https://apps.dtic.mil/sti/pdfs/ADA010261.pdf) — **Grade B1**. Summary: The US Navy's official report on clearing the canal from 11 April to 15 December 1974, mainly using helicopters to sweep for mines. Reliability: The official record by the team that did the work, so authoritative, but it gives a US view and says little about British, French or Egyptian roles.

- **UNCTAD (2024). Navigating Troubled Waters: Impact to Global Trade of Disruption of Shipping Routes in the Red Sea, Black Sea and Panama Canal.** UNCTAD Rapid Assessment. [link](https://unctad.org/publication/navigating-troubled-waters-impact-global-trade-disruption-shipping-routes-red-sea-black) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Suez Canal transits fell 42% from their peak and weekly container-ship transits fell 67% in early 2024. Reliability: A UN body publishing its own data, though it is a short early-2024 snapshot, not a yearly figure. Fix: check the 82% container figure in the PDF; it appears only in other coverage.

- **Container News (2026). EUNAVFOR ASPIDES warns of rising Red Sea shipping threat.** Container News. Reliability: Trade press repeating an official warning, with the exact date unconfirmed. Fix: cite the ASPIDES advisory (mscio.eu) directly.

- **Energy Intelligence (2024). [Title not confirmed].** Energy Intelligence. [link](https://www.energyintel.com/0000018d-8566-d705-a7af-877f0be90000) — **Grade C6**. ### Malacca

- **Raymond, C.Z. (2009). Piracy and Armed Robbery in the Malacca Strait: A Problem Solved?** Naval War College Review, 62(3). [link](https://www.jstor.org/stable/26397033) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Asks whether the sharp fall in Malacca piracy after 2004–05 means the problem was solved by joint patrols and information sharing. Summary: ReCAAP counted 80 incidents in January–June 2025 against 21 a year earlier, mostly petty thefts. Reliability: A Malaysian newspaper repeating a Guardian report, but the figures are confirmed by ReCAAP and Singapore. Fix: cite ReCAAP directly, and note incidents fell to 28 in late 2025 after arrests.


## Sources: Strait of Hormuz

405 sources on the Tanker War (1980–88) and the 2026 closure. Iranian state and IRGC-linked outlets (Press TV, Tasnim, IRNA, Mehr) are graded D: use them only to show how Iran framed the crisis. Most Gulf papers are government-aligned and graded C.

### Peer-reviewed and academic

- **Lott, A. and Kawagishi, S. (2022). The legal regime of the Strait of Hormuz and attacks against oil tankers.** *Ocean Development & International Law* 53(2–3). [link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/00908320.2022.2096158) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Explains Hormuz transit-passage rules and the 2019 tanker attacks, arguing states never proved Iran laid the 2019 mines. Reliability: Specialist peer-reviewed law journal, written before 2026; its verdict on Iran is disputed by US/UK governments, and only the abstract was seen.
- **Richer, C. (2024). "No Sense in Military Terms": Reconsidering the Surprising Use of Force in a Gray Zone Conflict.** *Journal of Military and Strategic Studies* 23(3). [link](https://jmss.org/article/view/76863) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Argues US officials misjudged how far the 1987–88 Tanker War could escalate; the first escorted tanker, *Bridgeton*, hit a mine. Reliability: Peer-reviewed open-access Canadian journal, but US-centred, and its conclusion was seen only in a search summary.
- **Shepard, J.U. and Pratson, L.F. (2020). Maritime piracy in the Strait of Hormuz and implications of energy export security.** *Energy Policy* 140. [link](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S030142152030135X) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Models piracy and harassment as a "soft restriction" on Gulf exports, showing some exporters are hit harder than others depending on their fuel and risk sensitivity. Reliability: Duke University authors in a leading energy journal (DOI confirmed), but it is a model of a rare threat type, not observed 2026 data.
- **Lassoued [initial to check] (2026). Maritime chokepoint insecurity and systemic trade vulnerability: a transportation security assessment of the Strait of Hormuz.** *Journal of Transportation Security* 19(1). [link](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s12198-026-00345-y) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Treats Hormuz as a problem of keeping a transport corridor secure, not just an oil-price shock, covering likelihood as well as severity. Reliability: Peer-reviewed Springer journal, but the author is unconfirmed and it was written before outcomes were known; abstract only.
- **Meza, A., Ari, I., Al Sada, M. and Koç, M. (2026). Implications of interrupting the Hormuz Strait in the LNG trade.** *Journal of Transportation Security*. [link](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s12198-026-00350-1) — **Grade A2**. Summary: A simulation finds a one-month closure could be absorbed via Pacific suppliers, but over six months leaves LNG demand unmet. Reliability: Peer-reviewed, but from a Qatari state-funded university writing about Qatar's own export route, and based on a model, not real 2026 data.
- **Navias, M.S. and Hooton, E.R. (1996). Tanker Wars: The Assault on Merchant Shipping during the Iran–Iraq Conflict, 1980–1988.** I.B. Tauris (review in *Iranian Studies*). [link](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/iranian-studies/article/abs/tanker-wars-the-assault-on-merchant-shipping-during-the-iraniraq-conflict-19801988-martin-s-navias-and-e-r-horton-london-and-new-york-ib-tauris-publishers-1996-ix-244-pp-including-appendix-notes-index-of-ships-and-general-index-cloth/555128C86253A41B2A6257F098EC20C2) — **Grade A2**. Summary: The standard scholarly history of the Tanker War, with an index of ships attacked; Iraq began attacks in 1981 and Iran retaliated from 1984, and over 400 seafarers died. Reliability: Academic book reviewed in journals, but dated (1996), needs library access, and may duplicate plan link 8.
- **Walker, G.K. (2000). The Tanker War, 1980–88: Law and Policy.** US Naval War College, *International Law Studies* vol. 74. [link](https://digital-commons.usnwc.edu/ils/vol74/iss1/) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Covers the law of neutral shipping, reflagging, convoys and UN Resolution 598 during the Tanker War, with a free full text. Reliability: Authoritative legal study, but published by a US Navy institution, so it takes a US legal viewpoint.
- **López Martín, A.G. (n.d., c. 2012–13). El "cierre" del estrecho de Ormuz: un análisis desde el Derecho internacional.** Dialnet / Ágora. [link](https://agora.edu.es/descarga/articulo/4371328.pdf) — **Grade A2**. Summary: A Spanish legal article asking whether Iran may lawfully close Hormuz or charge fees, or justify closure as a reprisal for sanctions. Reliability: Peer-reviewed article by a Complutense law professor, but probably written over a decade before the 2026 war and the date is unconfirmed.
- **[Authors to check] (2026). The price of passage: the legality of Iranian transit charges in the Strait of Hormuz under the Law of the Sea.** *Australian Journal of Maritime & Ocean Affairs* (to confirm). [link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/18366503.2026.2701606) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Covers Iran's toll law and permits after the late-April 2026 partial reopening, arguing no state may charge ships simply for passing through. Reliability: Peer-reviewed publisher, but search could not find the article or authors, so its existence is unconfirmed.
- **[Authors to check] (2026). Geopolitical disruptions in the Middle East and global supply chain resilience.** *Maritime Economics & Logistics*. [link](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1057/s41278-026-00366-3) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Studies how Middle East disruption hits supply chains and how firms can limit damage; a tracker counted 138 ships a day on 1 March 2026 and 12 on 2 April. Reliability: Leading journal in its field, but authors unknown and only a snippet seen; the 138→12 figure is from an unnamed tracker.
- **[Authors to check] (2012). The effect of transnational threats on the security of Persian Gulf maritime petroleum transportation.** *Journal of Transportation Security*. [link](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s12198-012-0090-y) — **Grade A6**. Summary: Looks at how terrorism, piracy and other non-state threats affect Gulf oil tankers, useful background on types of threat. Reliability: Peer-reviewed Springer journal, but only the title was seen and it is 14 years old, so its content cannot be judged.
- **Saidi, M., Esmaeildoost, F., Khankan, R., Masarani, H. and Toyasaki, F. (2026). Logistical agility at the Strait of Hormuz [full title to check].** *Transport Policy*. [link](https://trid.trb.org/View/2694716) — **Grade A6**. Summary: Looks at how logistics systems adapt to disruption at Hormuz, which supports supply-chain design as an alternative to naval cooperation. Reliability: Strong Elsevier journal, but only a bibliographic record was seen and the title is incomplete.
- **Mobley, R.A. (2022). Deterrence Without Escalation: Fresh Insights into U.S. Decisionmaking During Operation Earnest Will.** *Joint Force Quarterly* 106 (NDU Press). [link](https://ndupress.ndu.edu/Media/News/News-Article-View/Article/3106465/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Uses declassified documents to show US planners sent no minesweepers ahead, so *Bridgeton* was mined, and struggled to read Iran and avoid escalation. Reliability: US defence-university journal by a former US intelligence analyst, so it reflects a US view with no Iranian sources.
- **Mobley, R.A. (2021). London and Washington [full title to check] on the 1987 reflagging.** *Naval War College Review* 74(2). [link](https://digital-commons.usnwc.edu/nwc-review/vol74/iss2/9) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Uses declassified British records to show London had doubts about America's 1987 reflagging and escorts, so the "coalition" was not united. Reliability: Archive-based but published by the US Navy's college for officers and only editor-reviewed, so expect some bias towards US naval policy.
- **Mobley, R.A. (2016). Fighting Iran: intelligence support during Operation Earnest Will, 1987–88.** *Studies in Intelligence* 60(3), CIA. [link](https://www.cia.gov/resources/csi/static/Fighting-Iran.pdf) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Describes how intelligence and information sharing supported the 1987–88 escort operation, which can be compared with modern ship-tracking tools. Reliability: Official CIA in-house journal that passed CIA review, so it is pro-US and somewhat self-congratulatory.
- **Robert S. Strauss Center, University of Texas (n.d.). Strait of Hormuz – Tanker War.** Strauss Center. [link](https://www.strausscenter.org/strait-of-hormuz-tanker-war/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Says the Tanker War cut commercial shipping by 25% and raised crude prices early on, but at its peak disrupted no more than about 2% of ships in the Gulf. Reliability: Neutral university research summary, but undated, and its price claim is disputed by other sources.
- **American Society of International Law (2026). Charges for passage in the Strait of Hormuz.** *ASIL Insights* 30(10). [link](https://asil.org/insights/volume-30-issue-10/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Explains that the law gives no power to charge ships just for passing through a strait; charges are allowed only for specific services. Reliability: Editorially reviewed briefing by a leading US law society, short and aimed at a US audience; matches other legal sources.
- **Lott, A. (2026). The legality of Iran's closure of the Strait of Hormuz.** *EJIL: Talk!* [link](https://www.ejiltalk.org/the-legality-of-irans-closure-of-the-strait-of-hormuz/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Notes that the US, Israel and Iran are all non-parties to UNCLOS, and sets out Iran's argument that only suspendable innocent passage applies against the view that transit passage cannot be suspended. Reliability: Expert blog by a straits specialist, not peer-reviewed, and by the same author as the 2022 ODIL article.
- **Völkerrechtsblog (2026). Iran's Legal Strategy in Hormuz: Law of the Sea Considerations and Rules of Naval Warfare.** *Völkerrechtsblog*, 16 April. [link](https://voelkerrechtsblog.org/irans-legal-strategy-in-hormuz/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Sets out the strongest legal defence of Iran: it signed but never ratified UNCLOS and objected to transit passage becoming customary law. Reliability: German academic law blog with no named author and no peer review, but useful for presenting Iran's view fairly.
- **Verfassungsblog (2026). More than a Trade Route: Hormuz.** *Verfassungsblog*, 21 May. [link](https://verfassungsblog.de/more-than-a-trade-route-hormuz/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Says the strait normally carries 80–130 ships a day and about 20% of world oil and LNG, but traffic fell below 10 a day after the war began. Reliability: European academic legal blog, author not shown and not refereed; snippet only.
- **Libman, L. (2026). The U.S.-Iran MOU and the Future of Maritime Shipping Through the Strait of Hormuz.** *Lawfare*, 13 July. [link](https://www.lawfaremedia.org/article/the-u.s.-iran-mou-and-the-future-of-maritime-shipping-through-the-strait-of-hormuz) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Calls Article 5 of the June 2026 MoU "vague and unsatisfactory", for example on when 60 days of free passage start, and predicted another crisis. Reliability: Legal analysis by a former Israeli military lawyer, so flag an Israeli perspective; snippet only.
- **The Conversation (2026). Despite Its Best Efforts, Iran Won't Be Able to Toll the Strait of Hormuz.** *The Conversation*. [link](https://theconversation.com/despite-its-best-efforts-iran-wont-be-able-to-toll-the-strait-of-hormuz-heres-why-286241) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Argues Hormuz is too large to police like Suez or Panama, so a permanent toll is unenforceable, and Gulf states warn of a dangerous precedent. Reliability: Academic commentary written for the public, not peer-reviewed; pair it with the opposing Asia Times view.
- **The Conversation (2026). What Will It Take to Get Ships Going Through the Strait of Hormuz Again?** *The Conversation*. [link](https://theconversation.com/what-will-it-take-to-get-ships-going-through-the-strait-of-hormuz-again-280275) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Proposes limited escorts first, then a wider presence for surveillance, information sharing and rapid response, using the 2019 IMSC as a model. Reliability: Academic commentary, author not shown, not peer-reviewed; snippet only.
- **The Conversation (2026). How the Law of Naval Warfare Applies to the Strait of Hormuz.** *The Conversation*. [link](https://theconversation.com/how-the-law-of-naval-warfare-applies-to-the-strait-of-hormuz-278653) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Explains that merchant ships escorted by a warring state's navy can become lawful targets, a legal reason many states refused to join US convoys. Reliability: Academic commentary, not refereed; snippet only.
- **The Conversation (2026). The Strait of Hormuz Is Reopening, but Global Shipping Won't Return to Normal for Months.** *The Conversation*. [link](https://theconversation.com/the-strait-of-hormuz-is-reopening-but-global-shipping-wont-return-to-normal-for-months-285313) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Insurance costs, mine clearance and container backlogs keep disruption going after a political deal, so the disruption lasts longer than the fighting. Reliability: Academic commentary, author not shown; snippet only.
- **Zatarain, L.A. (2010). America's First Clash with Iran: The Tanker War, 1987–88.** Casemate. [link](https://search.worldcat.org/oclc/635489155) — **Grade C2**. Summary: A detailed 425-page narrative of Operation Earnest Will, from the USS *Stark* and *Bridgeton* mining to Praying Mantis and the *Vincennes* shoot-down. Reliability: Well-reviewed popular history using Pentagon documents and interviews, but not academic and written from a US viewpoint.
- **Kudrin, B. (2026). The Strait of Hormuz and the Limits of Maritime Law.** *Lawfare*, 23 March. [link](https://www.lawfaremedia.org/article/the-strait-of-hormuz-and-the-limits-of-maritime-law) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Argues international law gives weak protection to shipping once a coastal state is at war and claims economic warfare as self-defence. Reliability: US legal-policy outlet; the author's credentials were not shown and only a snippet was seen.
- **The Conversation (2026). How Might the Strait of Hormuz Be Reopened? Here Are 3 Scenarios.** *The Conversation*. [link](https://theconversation.com/how-might-the-strait-of-hormuz-be-reopened-here-are-3-scenarios-279840) — **Grade C3**. Summary: One scenario is a UN-mandated naval coalition, although Russia and China vetoed a reopening resolution in April 2026. Reliability: Academic commentary; the scenarios are speculative and only a snippet was seen.
- **Opinio Juris (2026). The closure of the Strait of Hormuz by Iran: considerations over the law of the sea and law of naval warfare.** *Opinio Juris*, 6 May. [link](http://opiniojuris.org/2026/05/06/the-closure-of-the-strait-of-hormuz-by-iran-considerations-over-the-law-of-the-sea-and-law-of-naval-warfare/) — **Grade C6**. Summary: A legal commentary on whether Iran's closure fits the law of the sea and the law of naval warfare; no figures or argument were visible. Reliability: Leading international-law blog, but no author or content was seen, so it cannot be judged.
- **Yousef, M. (2026). Does Iran Possess the Right to Close the Strait of Hormuz under International Law?** *internationallaw.blog*, 6 April. [link](https://internationallaw.blog/2026/04/06/does-iran-possess-the-right-to-close-the-strait-of-hormuz-under-international-law/) — **Grade C6**. Summary: Tests Iran's claimed right to close the strait against UNCLOS and customary law; only the headline and byline were visible. Reliability: Blog by a Turkish PhD student, not peer-reviewed, and content unseen.

### Reports, think tanks and official documents

- **International Energy Agency (2026). Oil Market Report, April, May and August 2026.** IEA. [link](https://www.iea.org/reports/oil-market-report-may-2026) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Loadings through the strait fell to about 3.8 mb/d in April from over 20 mb/d in February; lost Gulf output passed 1 billion barrels by May. Reliability: Intergovernmental energy agency defending its own stock release; partly paywalled, and its May forecast of resumption proved wrong.
- **World Bank (2026). Commodity Markets Outlook, April 2026.** World Bank (seen via Kitco). [link](https://www.kitco.com/news/off-the-wire/2026-04-28/world-bank-forecasts-24-surge-energy-prices-2026-due-middle-east-war) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Calls the disruption the largest oil supply shock on record (about 10 mb/d at the peak) and forecast Brent at $86 for 2026, or $115 in a worse case. Reliability: Official international lender, but seen only through secondary coverage and its baseline (normal traffic by October) was wrong.
- **OECD (2026). OECD Economic Outlook, Interim Report, March 2026.** OECD (mirror copy). [link](https://academicnewsletter.sufe.edu.cn/info/462159) — **Grade A2**. Summary: G20 inflation for 2026 was revised up 1.2 points to 4.0% because Hormuz shipments stopped; prolonged disruption would add about 0.9 points more. Reliability: Official forecast from an intergovernmental body, seen via a mirror and a summary, and possibly revised later.
- **UNCTAD (2026). Strait of Hormuz Disruptions: Growth and Financial Implications.** UN Trade and Development, April. [link](https://unctad.org/system/files/official-document/osginf2026d2_en.pdf) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Describes the strait as practically closed and expects slower world trade and growth in 2026. Reliability: Official UN agency report, but written in April before the June re-closure and only the summary was seen.
- **U.S. Energy Information Administration (2026). Today in Energy: Brent's first-quarter rise.** EIA. [link](https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=67424) — **Grade A2** (graded here). Summary: Brent rose from $61 at the start of 2026 to $118 at the end of the first quarter, the largest inflation-adjusted quarterly rise since 1988. Reliability: US government statistics agency giving its own price data; seen only as a search snippet, but consistent with World Bank figures.
- **World Bank Open Data blog (2026). Strait of Hormuz disruption sends oil prices surging.** World Bank. [link](https://blogs.worldbank.org/en/opendata/strait-of-hormuz-disruption-sends-oil-prices-surging) — **Grade A2** (graded here). Summary: Brent rose about 65% (+$46) in March 2026, its biggest monthly rise on record, while global supply fell by 10.1 mb/d. Reliability: Official World Bank data blog; snippet only, but the figures match EIA and IEA data.
- **Childs, N. (2026). Navigating the Strait of Hormuz.** IISS *Survival Online*, June. [link](https://www.iiss.org/online-analysis/survival-online/2026/06/navigating-the-strait-of-hormuz/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Argues there is "no plausible military option" to reopen the strait: narrow waters cancel US advantages and allied navies lack ships, so they will join only after a ceasefire. Reliability: Respected London think tank, but it took large Bahraini funding in the past, and this is opinion written mid-crisis.
- **IISS (2026). A bad peace: the Arab Gulf states and the US–Iran memorandum of understanding.** IISS Online Analysis, June. [link](https://www.iiss.org/online-analysis/online-analysis/2026/06/a-bad-peace-the-arab-gulf-states-and-the-usiran-memorandum-of-understanding/) — **Grade B2** (graded here). Summary: Looks at the 17 June 2026 Islamabad MoU and how Gulf states other than Oman resisted Iranian transit fees. Reliability: Top think tank with past Gulf-state funding; seen only as a search snippet.
- **IISS (2026). Steel Traps: America's Fraught Quest for Maritime Dominance.** IISS *Survival Online*, September. [link](https://www.iiss.org/online-analysis/survival-online/2026/09/steel-traps-americas-fraught-quest-for-maritime-dominance/) — **Grade B2** (graded here). Summary: Says the US Navy cannot yet keep Hormuz open "at an acceptable cost" and calls the result "sea denial via reciprocal blockade". Reliability: Top think tank analysis (past Bahraini funding); opinion seen only as a search snippet.
- **Brookings Institution (2026). From chokepoint to crisis: the Strait of Hormuz and global oil markets.** Brookings. [link](https://www.brookings.edu/articles/from-chokepoint-to-crisis-the-strait-of-hormuz-and-global-oil-markets/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Oil passed $100 in March and reached $118 by 29 April; the US, though a net exporter, still faced the same global price shock. Reliability: Centrist US think tank with past Qatari funding; the author is unknown and figures came from a blended search summary.
- **Atlantic Council (2026). The crisis in the Strait of Hormuz is a heavy blow to the deteriorating maritime order.** Atlantic Council issue brief. [link](https://www.atlanticcouncil.org/in-depth-research-reports/issue-brief/the-crisis-in-the-strait-of-hormuz-is-a-heavy-blow-to-the-deteriorating-maritime-order/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Treats the closure as part of a wider breakdown of maritime rules, noting the US blockade from 13 April and Iran's fee-paying lane from 5 May. Reliability: Pro-NATO US think tank funded partly by governments, defence firms and Gulf states, with an advocacy tone.
- **CSIS (2026). No One, Not Even Beijing, Is Getting Through the Strait of Hormuz (and related LNG pieces).** CSIS. [link](https://www.csis.org/analysis/no-one-not-even-beijing-getting-through-strait-hormuz) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Shows even China could not get its ships through, so good relations with Iran did not protect trade; related pieces cover Qatari LNG cut off from Asia. Reliability: Major US think tank part-funded by the US government, defence firms and Gulf states; authors not shown.
- **Hidalgo Pérez, M. (2026). Vulnerabilidades sistémicas ante la disrupción del estrecho de Ormuz.** *Comentario Elcano*, Real Instituto Elcano, 11 March. [link](https://www.realinstitutoelcano.org/comentarios/vulnerabilidades-sistemicas-ante-la-disrupcion-del-estrecho-de-ormuz/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Spain looks safe on crude (8.98% dependence) but its plastics and rubber sector is 18.78% exposed through other countries' products; about 20% of world oil and LNG uses Hormuz. Reliability: Spain's leading think tank, part-funded by firms including Repsol, and partly promoting its own index.
- **Burguete, V. (2026). Irán y el estrecho de Ormuz: en la antesala de una crisis energética.** CIDOB, March. [link](https://www.cidob.org/publicaciones/iran-estrecho-ormuz-antesala-crisis-energetica) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The size of the crisis depends on how long fighting lasts and how much infrastructure is hit; Brent was $80 and LNG prices up 50% at publication. Reliability: Barcelona think tank partly funded by public bodies; written early in the crisis.
- **Hidalgo García, M. del M. (2026). El bloqueo del estrecho de Ormuz y los fertilizantes: ¿conflicto de hoy y hambre para mañana?** IEEE / CESEDEN. [link](https://www.defensa.gob.es/ceseden/-/ieee/2026-estrecho-ormuz-fertilizantes-analisis) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The blockade also cuts fertiliser and raw-material exports, which threatens food price rises in the medium term. Reliability: Spanish Ministry of Defence research institute, so it speaks from inside government; exact date not shown.
- **Universitat de València / Valenciaport (2026). Informe internacional valenciaport – mayo 2026.** Valenciaport. [link](https://www.uv.es/porteconomy/publicaciones/informes-internacionales/2026/valenciaport-Informe-mayo-2026.pdf) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A port-economics report centred on Hormuz: about 30% of internationally traded oil and a fifth of traded gas pass the 21-nautical-mile strait. Reliability: University port chair writing for port stakeholders; its 30% figure measures seaborne trade, not consumption.
- **Bordoff, J. and O'Sullivan, M.L. (2026a). The Iran Shock and the Dangerous Allure of Energy Autarky.** *Foreign Affairs* 105(3). [link](https://www.foreignaffairs.com/iran/iran-shock) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Calls Iran's near-closure the largest energy disruption in history; oil rose 55% in three weeks, and the authors warn self-sufficiency drives weaken the market that cushions shocks. Reliability: Essay by two former senior US officials with a pro-market view; paywalled and seen as a snippet.
- **Bordoff, J. and O'Sullivan, M.L. (2026b). The Long Shadow of the Iran Shock.** *Foreign Affairs*, 14 August. [link](https://www.foreignaffairs.com/iran/long-shadow-iran-shock) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Prices stayed lower than feared because the market was oversupplied and Saudi and UAE pipelines bypassed the strait, not because of naval cooperation. Reliability: Respected energy experts with a US liberal-internationalist view; an argument, so check the figures it cites.
- **Congressional Research Service (2026). Report R45281 (Iran conflict background).** US Congress. [link](https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/R45281) — **Grade B2** (graded here). Summary: Confirms US and Israeli strikes on Iran began on 28 February 2026 and a US–Iran ceasefire came on 7 April. Reliability: Non-partisan research service for the US Congress, reliable on US actions; used only for dates and seen as a snippet.
- **Federal Reserve Bank of Dallas (2026). Working Paper 2609 (closure of the Strait of Hormuz).** Dallas Fed, April. [link](https://www.dallasfed.org/~/media/documents/research/papers/2026/wp2609.pdf) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Models a closure removing nearly 20% of world oil: WTI averages about $110, $132 or $167 after one, two or three quarters of outage. Reliability: Central-bank economists, not peer-reviewed; these are early scenarios, and search summaries gave conflicting figures.
- **Kiel Institute for the World Economy (2026). The Cost of Closing the Strait of Hormuz.** Kiel Policy Brief 206, March. [link](https://www.kielinstitut.de/fileadmin/Dateiverwaltung/IfW-Publications/fis-import/01b7c020-27e6-4096-8cc5-e037738d2058-KPB_206.pdf) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Estimates world oil prices up about 12% and an EU welfare loss of about 0.4%, far below the Dallas Fed's estimate. Reliability: Top publicly funded German think tank, but a quick pre-outcome scenario.
- **Oxford Institute for Energy Studies (2026). Modelling the Impact of the Strait of Hormuz Closure.** OIES Energy Comment, March. [link](https://www.oxfordenergy.org/wpcms/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/Comment-Modelling-the-Impact-of-the-Strait-of-Hormuz-Closure.pdf) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Focuses on gas, with oil scenarios at $125 or $150 a barrel. Reliability: Independent but partly industry-funded institute; early scenarios, not outcomes.
- **National Institute for Defense Studies, Japan (2026). NIDS Commentary No. 427.** NIDS. [link](https://www.nids.mod.go.jp/english/publication/commentary/pdf/commentary427e.pdf) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Argues the special circumstances of the war make it hard to call Iran's de facto closure simply a breach of transit passage, a contrast with Western views. Reliability: Research body of Japan's Ministry of Defense, so shaped by the institution; snippet only.
- **RUSI and War on the Rocks (2026). The Atlantic Brief: the Strait of Hormuz and the future of free navigation (webinar).** RUSI / WOTR, 31 July. [link](https://warontherocks.com/2026/07/the-atlantic-brief-the-strait-of-hormuz-and-the-future-of-free-navigation/) — **Grade B3**. Summary: An expert panel on free navigation after the war, the fragile ceasefire, the re-blocking of Hormuz and Houthi threats to Saudi shipping. Reliability: UK/US defence-establishment speakers; spoken opinion, possibly members-only, and not watched.
- **Council on Foreign Relations (2026). The Strait of Hormuz: A U.S.-Iran Maritime Flash Point.** CFR.org. [link](https://www.cfr.org/articles/strait-hormuz-us-iran-maritime-flash-point) — **Grade B3**. Summary: After the 28 February strikes tanker traffic fell about 70% and Brent peaked "above $138"; the IEA released 400m barrels and the US blockaded Iran from 12 April. Reliability: US establishment think-tank explainer; its Brent peak is higher than other sources give.
- **Council on Foreign Relations (2026). Trump's Project Freedom Isn't Going to Open the Strait of Hormuz.** CFR.org. [link](https://www.cfr.org/articles/trumps-project-freedom-isnt-going-to-open-the-strait-of-hormuz) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Argues a US-led escort scheme cannot restore normal traffic alone; only two Project Freedom transits happened before a pause on 6 May. Reliability: Opinion critical of the Trump administration, author not shown.
- **Council on Foreign Relations (2026). Strait-jacket: Global Energy Flows and the War with Iran.** CFR.org. [link](https://www.cfr.org/articles/strait-jacket-global-energy-flows-the-war-with-iran) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Argues there was "no viable export route" for most Gulf barrels while the strait was shut. Reliability: US think-tank analysis, author not shown; snippet only.
- **Panda, J. (2026). China's Free Ride at Hormuz.** Institute for Security and Development Policy (first in *The Diplomat*), 23 March. [link](https://www.isdp.eu/?p=41951) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Argues China is "a principal beneficiary of the status quo" that pays little for Gulf security, and that Chinese-linked Iranian cargoes kept moving. Reliability: Reputable Swedish institute, but the author is known to be China-sceptical and the cargo claims are unverified.
- **Real Instituto Elcano (2026). Boletín/Newsletter 346-2026: El estrecho de Ormuz.** Elcano. [link](https://www.realinstitutoelcano.org/newsletters/boletin-newsletter-346-2026-el-estrecho-de-ormuz/) — **Grade B6**. Summary: A March 2026 roundup of Elcano work on Hormuz, led by the Hidalgo Pérez commentary. Reliability: Think-tank promotional roundup with no original content; cite the underlying papers instead.
- **Real Instituto Elcano (2026). Novedades en la RED nº 974 – julio de 2026: Verano en el estrecho de Ormuz.** Elcano library. [link](https://www.realinstitutoelcano.org/newsletters/novedades-en-la-red/974-julio-de-2026-verano-en-el-estrecho-de-ormuz/) — **Grade B6**. Summary: A July 2026 reading list on Hormuz after the re-closure. Reliability: Library bulletin; only the page menu was indexed, so its content is unknown and it is not evidence.
- **Arteaga, F. and Escribano, G. (2026). Los mercados energéticos ante los últimos peldaños de la escalada en Oriente Medio.** Real Instituto Elcano, 4 March. [link](https://www.realinstitutoelcano.org/) — **Grade B6**. Summary: Covers energy markets in the first week of the 2026 war; only the title and date were seen. Reliability: Respected think tank, but the exact URL and content were not seen, so it cannot be judged.
- **Brew, G. (2026). Iran's New Oil Weapon.** *Foreign Affairs*, 6 May. [link](https://www.foreignaffairs.com/iran/irans-new-oil-weapon) — **Grade B6**. Summary: Argues Iran has turned control of Hormuz into a lasting "oil weapon" for leverage rather than a one-off blockade. Reliability: Essay by an Iran and energy analyst; the full argument was not visible, so read it before quoting.
- **Howden Re (2026). Strait of Hormuz report, March 2026.** Howden Re. [link](https://www.howdenre.com/sites/howdenre.howdenprod.com/files/2026-03/HowdenRe_Strait_of_Hormuz_report_March272026.pdf) — **Grade C1** (graded here). Summary: Says Gulf war-risk cover had reached about 5% of a ship's value by 19 March, roughly five times early-war levels. Reliability: Reinsurance broker research with a commercial interest in high premiums, but its figure matches Bloomberg's report of the Lloyd's chief.
- **Naval History (2025). The Tanker War.** US Naval Institute, June. [link](https://www.usni.org/magazines/naval-history/2025/june/tanker-war) — **Grade C1**. Summary: In the first Earnest Will convoy *Bridgeton* hit an Iranian mine, and the escorts had no plan for mine strikes or night-time Iranian minelaying. Reliability: US naval professional magazine with a US Navy viewpoint; its facts are well confirmed elsewhere.
- **Samaan, J.-L. (2026). The Missing Navies: The Hormuz Crisis and the Limits of America's Indo-Pacific Partnerships.** *War on the Rocks*, 13 May. [link](https://warontherocks.com/the-missing-navies-the-hormuz-crisis-and-the-limits-of-americas-indo-pacific-partnerships/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Calls the closure "fundamentally an Asian problem", yet Asian states mostly refused to send ships; Seoul only agreed to "review" joining after a Korean ship was fired on. Reliability: Expert analysis by a Singapore-based scholar for a US policy audience; snippet only.
- **Schroden, J. (2025). A Strait Comparison: Lessons from the Dardanelles for a Strait of Hormuz Closure.** *War on the Rocks*, 30 June. [link](https://warontherocks.com/2025/06/a-strait-comparison-lessons-from-the-dardanelles-for-a-strait-of-hormuz-closure/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Uses the failed 1915 Dardanelles attack to show Iran would struggle to close Hormuz fully, but mines plus shore fire could still make it unusable. Reliability: Expert from a US-Navy-funded research tradition; written before the 2026 war.
- **Sun, Y. (2026). How Does the Iran War Affect China's Energy Security?** *War on the Rocks*, 17 March. [link](https://warontherocks.com/2026/03/how-does-the-iran-war-affect-chinas-energy-security/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Says headlines exaggerate China's weakness: it is about 85% energy self-sufficient overall, though it imports most of its oil. Reliability: Mainstream US think-tank analyst giving a Chinese-perspective reading; snippet only.
- **CIMSEC (2026). The Price of Doubt: Sea Control in the Strait of Hormuz.** CIMSEC. [link](https://cimsec.org/the-price-of-doubt-sea-control-in-the-strait-of-hormuz/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Argues Earnest Will worked because the US government took on the tankers' risk, and 2026 shipping recovered only when financial risk became manageable, so escorts need insurance guarantees. Reliability: Naval-affairs forum analysis, author not checked; links both Hormuz periods directly.
- **US Naval Institute Proceedings (2026). The Crisis in Mine Countermeasures.** *Proceedings*, April. [link](https://www.usni.org/magazines/proceedings/2026/april/crisis-mine-countermeasures) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Argues Western navies lack enough minehunters, a weakness exposed by Iran's mining of Hormuz. Reliability: US naval professional journal, not peer-reviewed, with a pro-navy readership.
- **Govaerts, B. and Burke, S. (2026). The Iran War Could Trigger a Global Food Crisis.** *Project Syndicate*, March. [link](https://www.project-syndicate.org/commentary/strait-of-hormuz-closure-not-just-an-oil-problem-by-bram-govaerts-and-sharon-burke-2026-03) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Hormuz is "not just an oil problem": blocked Gulf fertiliser and sulphur exports threaten harvests, and 40–50% of seaborne urea starts in the Middle East. Reliability: Opinion by a food-research director and a former US defence official; take figures from data sources.
- **Deutsche Bank España (2026). PERSPECTIVES: Conflicto de Ormuz, retos para Europa.** Deutsche Bank CIO. [link](https://www.deutsche-bank.es/dam/spain/pdf/PERSPECTIVES-Special-Conflicto-de-Ormuz-retos-para-Europa.pdf) — **Grade C2** (graded here). Summary: About 12% of EU oil imports and 14% of EU LNG imports pass through Hormuz, while under 10% of Spain's oil imports come from the Middle East. Reliability: Bank research written for investor clients; seen only as a snippet, and its Spain figure fits official CORES data.
- **Raman, S. [attribution unconfirmed] (2026). The Toll Booth at the Throat of World Trade.** *War on the Rocks*. [link](https://warontherocks.com/the-toll-booth-at-the-throat-of-world-trade/) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Iran's "Persian Gulf Strait Authority" from 18 May 2026 issued transit permits with fees up to $2 million a voyage, designed to sit outside the US-dollar system; the US sanctioned it on 27 May. Reliability: US sanctions-law perspective; the byline and date are unconfirmed.
- **Guerin, E. (2026). Europe Is Losing the Energy-Security Battle to China.** *Project Syndicate*, May. [link](https://www.project-syndicate.org/commentary/rapid-electrification-is-key-to-energy-security-by-emmanuel-guerin-2026-05) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Uses the Hormuz shock to argue that fast electrification, not naval protection of oil routes, is Europe's real path to energy security. Reliability: Pro-energy-transition opinion piece; snippet only.
- **Carvalho, L. (2026). The Hormuz Crisis and the Fate of the Global South.** *Project Syndicate*, April. [link](https://www.project-syndicate.org/commentary/global-south-hormuz-crisis-opportunity-to-accelerate-economic-transition-by-laura-carvalho-2026-04) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Argues developing countries should treat the shock as a reason to speed up the energy transition and cut fuel-import exposure. Reliability: Opinion by a Brazilian economist, a Latin American voice; no data seen.
- **Morsy, H. (2026). Is the Global Financial System Making the Hormuz Shock Worse?** *Project Syndicate*, May. [link](https://www.project-syndicate.org/commentary/global-financial-system-making-hormuz-shock-worse-by-hanan-morsy-2026-05) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Argues capital flight and costly borrowing amplify the shock for African and other poorer importers, whose currencies were hit hard. Reliability: Opinion by a UN Economic Commission for Africa economist; snippet only.
- **Hernandez Diaz, D., Di Fiori, L. and Devesa, T. (2026). Energy Security in an Age of Disruption.** *Project Syndicate*, September. [link](https://www.project-syndicate.org/commentary/how-countries-and-companies-can-prepare-for-the-next-energy-shock-by-diego-hernandez-diaz-et-al-2026-09) — **Grade C3**. Summary: More than one in five barrels of seaborne oil in Q2 2026 took a new route; stockpiles and rerouting, not reopening the strait, did most of the work. Reliability: Opinion with advice for governments and firms; figures from PS coverage, not checked.
- **Revista General de Marina (2026). Geoestrategia del estrecho de Ormuz.** Spanish Navy, June issue. [link](https://armada.defensa.gob.es/archivo/rgm/2026/06/rgmjun2026_Parte03.pdf) — **Grade C3**. Summary: A Spanish naval article stressing how much oil and gas is concentrated in the strait and calling it a critical node of instability. Reliability: Official Spanish Navy journal, not peer-reviewed and largely descriptive; snippet only.
- **Frieda, G. (2026). Why This Energy Shock Is Different.** *Project Syndicate*, July. [link](https://www.project-syndicate.org/commentary/iran-war-energy-shock-requires-new-policy-toolkit-by-gene-frieda-2026-07) — **Grade C4**. Summary: Argues the 2026 shock needs a new policy toolkit; it says Brent settled around $79 after the July ceasefire collapse, well below an April peak near $120. Reliability: Opinion by a macro strategist; its $79 figure conflicts with other sources showing oil above $100.
- **Deutsche Bank España (2026). PERSPECTIVES Special: La crisis de Ormuz. Camino incierto incluso para EEUU.** Deutsche Bank, March. [link](https://www.deutsche-bank.es/dam/spain/pdf/PERSPECTIVES-Special-La-crisis-de-Ormuz-Camino-incierto-incluso-para-EEUU.pdf) — **Grade C6**. Summary: A note on the market effects of the Hormuz shock, including for the US; no figures were visible. Reliability: Commercial bank research meant to inform and keep investor clients; content unseen.
- **Pensamiento Conjunto (2026). La guerra entre EE. UU., Israel e Irán de 2026.** Peruvian Joint Command journal, 30 June. [link](https://www.pensamientoconjunto.com.pe/index.php/PC/article/view/188) — **Grade C6**. Summary: An operational analysis of the 2026 war, including the fight over Hormuz, from a Latin American military view. Reliability: Military journal essay, author not shown and content not seen.
- **Institute for China-America Studies (2026). Why has Trump's Proposal for an "Escort Coalition" in the Strait of Hormuz been Slow to Materialize?** ICAS, 19 March. [link](https://chinaus-icas.org/?p=62867) — **Grade D2**. Summary: Allies held back because US officials said escorts would come only after air superiority, and no one would commit while fighting continued. Reliability: Washington think tank linked to a Chinese state institute, so it gives a China-leaning view; only the opening was seen.

### Primary sources (resolutions, statements)

- **UN Security Council (1987). Resolution 598.** UN Digital Library, 20 July 1987. [link](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/137345) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The binding Iran–Iraq ceasefire resolution, passed 15–0, which deplores attacks on neutral shipping; Iran accepted it only in July 1988 and the ceasefire began on 20 August 1988. Reliability: Original UN legal text reflecting great-power agreement; it shows diplomacy helped end the Tanker War but not how much.
- **UN Security Council (2026). Resolution 2817.** Security Council Report, 11 March 2026. [link](https://www.securitycouncilreport.org/un-documents/document/s-res-2817.php) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Passed 13–0 with China and Russia abstaining; it condemns Iran's moves to close Hormuz and says blocking lawful transit passage threatens peace. Reliability: Original UN text drafted by Bahrain for the Gulf states, who were parties to the crisis; declaratory, and it did not reopen the strait.
- **Lloyd's Market Association Joint War Committee (2026). JWLA-033 listed areas circular.** LMA, 3 March 2026. [link](https://lmalloyds.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/03/JWLA-033_Iran.pdf) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Widened the high-risk zone across the Gulf, Gulf of Oman and Red Sea and added Bahrain, Djibouti, Kuwait, Oman and Qatar, so ships there must pay extra war-risk premiums. Reliability: Original insurance-market document, confirmed by Gard, though the market profits from wide listings; it gives no price levels.
- **UN News (2026). "No precedent" for seafarers caught in war zone in post-WW2 era.** UN News, March. [link](https://news.un.org/en/story/2026/03/1167224) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The IMO warned that about 20,000 seafarers were trapped in the Gulf, a scale not seen since 1945. Reliability: UN official news reporting the IMO's own estimate, confirmed by other outlets; the UN also campaigns for seafarers.
- **UN News (2026). Strait of Hormuz: UN evacuates 2,500 seafarers before attack freezes rescue operation.** UN News, June. [link](https://news.un.org/en/story/2026/06/1167818) — **Grade A1**. Summary: An IMO-led evacuation got 115 ships and about 2,500 crew out before pausing; by June at least 14 seafarers were dead and over 40 ships attacked. Reliability: UN official news using IMO figures, confirmed by *The National*; counts rise over time, so cite the date.
- **UN News (2026). Stranded seafarers remain trapped as Hormuz shipping stalls.** UN News, July. [link](https://news.un.org/en/story/2026/07/1168004) — **Grade A1**. Summary: After fighting resumed in mid-July the IMO found "very few ships, if any" crossing, and some owners abandoned crews without pay. Reliability: UN official news, consistent with other data, but its wording is descriptive rather than a count.
- **International Maritime Organization (n.d.). Middle East / Strait of Hormuz hot-topic page.** IMO. [link](https://www.imo.org/en/mediacentre/hottopics/pages/middle-east-strait-of-hormuz.aspx) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Says Iran and Oman proposed the Hormuz traffic separation scheme, adopted by the IMO in 1968; in 2026 the central lanes were unusable because of mines. Reliability: Official IMO page that presents its own role positively; the 1968 date conflicts with other findings, so check it.
- **UN Human Rights Office (OHCHR) (2026). Stranded Hormuz seafarers abandoned for months at sea (press briefing).** OHCHR, July. [link](https://www.ohchr.org/en/press-briefing-notes/2026/07/stranded-hormuz-seafarers-abandoned-months-sea) — **Grade A2** (graded here). Summary: Said some shipowners had abandoned crews trapped by the Hormuz crisis without pay or a way home. Reliability: Official UN statement with a human-rights advocacy aim; seen only as a snippet, but consistent with IMO reports.
- **Governments of Greece and the UAE, with Seatrade report (2026). Statements on the vetoed Hormuz draft resolution.** Greek Mission to the UN, 7 April 2026. [link](https://greeceforunsc.mfa.gr/wp-content/uploads/2026/04/GRC_Res.-on-the-Strait-of-Hormuz-EoV-April-7th.pdf) — **Grade B1**. Summary: A softened draft "encouraging" defensive escorts, with no force authorised, was vetoed by China and Russia (11–2–2). Reliability: Governments explaining and defending their own votes, but the veto is confirmed by independent reports; also get the UN meeting record.
- **Joint Maritime Information Center (2025–26). JMIC Advisory Notes and threat levels.** JMIC / UKMTO (via Seatrade). [link](https://www.seatrade-maritime.com/security/jmic-raises-strait-of-hormuz-threat-level-to-severe) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The Hormuz threat level went "critical" in March 2026, "substantial" on 17 June after the MoU and "severe" on 7 July. Reliability: Warnings from a US-led coalition body that is itself a party to the conflict; criteria not published in detail.
- **Hellenic Shipping News (2026). 80-country joint statement on reopening the Strait of Hormuz.** Hellenic Shipping News. [link](https://www.hellenicshippingnews.com/?p=1149783) — **Grade D3**. Summary: About 80 countries called for Resolution 2817 to be carried out and opposed tolls, showing broad but non-binding cooperation. Reliability: A repost about the statement, not the statement itself, and sources disagree on its date.

### Historical news, 1980–88

- **UPI (1987). The Pentagon raised the toll today to 37 sailors.** UPI Archives, 19 May. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/05/19/The-Pentagon-raised-the-toll-today-to-37-sailors/3451548395200/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Two Iraqi Exocet missiles from a Mirage F-1 killed 37 sailors on USS *Stark* on 17 May 1987, the attack that led to US escorts; the US accepted Saddam Hussein's apology. Reliability: Wire report written at the time using Pentagon figures; the death toll is confirmed by many sources.
- **UPI (1987). Iraq apologized for its mistaken attack; Stark survivors describe the hit.** UPI Archives, 18 May. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/05/18/Iraq-apologized-Monday-for-its-mistaken-attack-on-the/2828548308800/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Iraq called the *Stark* attack mistaken identity and apologised; survivors said a missile struck below the bridge near crew quarters. Reliability: Same-week wire reports; Iraq's version of the pilot's motives is its own claim.
- **UPI (1987). The Navy relieved the skipper of the USS Stark.** UPI Archives, 19 June. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/06/19/The-Navy-relieved-the-skipper-of-the-USS-Stark/9837551073600/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: The captain was relieved after the inquiry; he and an officer were later reprimanded and left the Navy without a court-martial. Reliability: Wire report from the time based on US Navy statements.
- **UPI (1987). Kuwaiti tankers reflagged with Stars and Stripes.** UPI Archives, 21 July. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/07/21/Kuwaiti-tankers-reflagged-with-Stars-and-Stripes/5770553838400/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: *Bridgeton* and *Gas Prince* became the first of 11 Kuwaiti tankers put under the US flag; Reagan cited freedom of navigation while critics feared being dragged into the war. Reliability: Wire report written at the time, widely confirmed.
- **UPI (1987). First Earnest Will convoy reports.** UPI Archives, 22–23 July. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/07/23/US-warships-escorted-two-reflagged-Kuwaiti-tankers-up-the/9564554011200/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: USS *Kidd*, *Fox* and *Crommelin* escorted the first reflagged tankers; Earnest Will ran from July 1987 to September 1988, the largest convoy operation since WWII. Reliability: Wire reports from the time; early details came from the US Navy.
- **UPI (1987). The supertanker Bridgeton hit an underwater mine.** UPI Archives, 24 July. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/07/24/The-supertanker-Bridgeton-hit-an-underwater-mine-Friday-morning/4397554097600/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: The 401,382-ton *Bridgeton* hit a mine about 18 miles west of Farsi Island on the first convoy; none of the 26 aboard were hurt and it reached Kuwait. Reliability: Same-day wire report, widely confirmed, though blaming Iran was a Pentagon claim at the time.
- **UPI (1987). Britain, France sending mine sweepers to Persian, Oman gulfs.** UPI Archives, 11 August. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/08/11/Britain-France-sending-mine-sweepers-to-Persian-Oman-gulfs/5251555652800/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: After the *Bridgeton* mining, Britain sent four minesweepers and France joined; Italy, Belgium and the Netherlands followed, coordinated loosely through the Western European Union. Reliability: Wire report of government announcements, not results; confirmed by later analysis.
- **UPI (1987). A missile believed to be an Iranian-fired Silkworm hit the Sea Isle City.** UPI Archives, 16 October. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/10/16/A-missile-believed-to-be-an-Iranian-fired-Silkworm-slammed/3846561355200/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: A Silkworm missile hit the reflagged tanker in Kuwaiti waters, injuring 18 including its American master, showing convoys did not stop all attacks. Reliability: Wire report confirmed by the Washington Post; Iranian blame was a US claim, later widely accepted.
- **UPI (1988). The U.S. Navy located and destroyed two mines linked to Iran.** UPI Archives, 15 April. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1988/04/15/The-US-Navy-located-and-destroyed-two-mines-linked/6846577080000/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: USS *Samuel B. Roberts* hit an Iranian mine on 14 April 1988, injuring 10, and the Navy then destroyed two more mines about 55 miles off Bahrain. Reliability: Wire report from the time; the link to Iran was a US Navy claim, later widely accepted.
- **UPI (1988). US searches for missing helicopter; Reagan calls Persian Gulf actions a "measured response".** UPI Archives, 19 and 23 April. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1988/04/23/Reagan-calls-Persian-Gulf-actions-measured-response/9721577771200/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: A Cobra helicopter with two crew was lost; Reagan called the strikes a "measured response" to Iran's renewed mine-laying and said the incident was closed. Reliability: Wire reports confirmed by the Washington Post; the quotes are official US framing, not proof of success.
- **UPI (1988). Reagan said downing of Iranian airliner was "understandable accident".** UPI Archives, 4 July. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1988/07/04/President-Reagan-said-downing-of-Iranian-airliner-was-understandable-accident/3452583992000/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: USS *Vincennes* shot down Iran Air Flight 655 on 3 July 1988, killing 290; the crew mistook the Airbus A300 for an F-14. Reliability: Wire report from the next day reflecting the US official line; the snippet wrongly said A320.
- **UPI (1988). Iran demands apology; Security Council to meet on Iran Air downing.** UPI Archives, 5 and 12 July. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1988/07/05/Iran-demands-apology-from-US-for-airliner-downing/3787584078400/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Iran called the shoot-down "premeditated" and said the plane was climbing and sending its civilian code; the UN Security Council then met. Reliability: Wire reports from the time; Iran's claims are reported as claims.
- **UPI (1988). Iran accepts unconditional cease-fire.** UPI Archives, 18 July. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1988/07/18/Iran-accepts-unconditional-cease-fire/6562585201600) — **Grade B1**. Summary: A letter from President Khamenei accepted UN Resolution 598, a year after it passed, which ended the Tanker War through diplomacy. Reliability: Wire report from the time; the letter was dated 17 July and announced 18 July.
- **Washington Post (1988). U.S. to end "convoy" role in the Gulf.** *The Washington Post*, 26 September. [link](https://www.washingtonpost.com/archive/politics/1988/09/26/us-to-end-convoy-role-in-the-gulf/db6ddca8-b769-49e8-9f33-3cc8f6df6cb8/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: After the August 1988 ceasefire, formal convoys ended and became looser "accompaniment"; the disruption ended through politics, not only naval action. Reliability: Paper of record reporting at the time, from a US perspective.
- **UPI (1984). The Iran-Iraq war in the Persian Gulf has cost marine insurers.** UPI Archives, 9 March. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1984/03/09/The-Iran-Iraq-war-in-the-Persian-Gulf-has-cost/3441276045960/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Lloyd's Shipping Information Services estimated over $500 million in losses to marine insurers since September 1980. Reliability: Wire report from the time, but the only source for this figure, which is a total loss, not a premium rate.
- **UPI (1984). Two cargo ships, one British and one Turkish, attacked.** UPI Archives, 7 March. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1984/03/07/Two-cargo-ships-one-British-and-one-Turkish/3365447483600) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Early 1984 attacks on neutral merchant ships during Iraq's declared blockade of Iranian ports, marking the start of the Tanker War. Reliability: Wire report written at the time; details may be incomplete.
- **UPI (1984). Attack on Persian Gulf tanker "dangerous escalation".** UPI Archives, 15 May. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1984/05/15/Attack-on-Persian-Gulf-tanker-dangerous-escalation/6583453441600/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Lloyd's doubled Kharg Island war-risk cover from 0.75% to 1.5% of hull value, and after a Kuwaiti tanker was hit rates reached 3% of ship value plus 2% of cargo. Reliability: Wire report from the time; excellent insurance evidence, but check which May story gives each figure.
- **UPI (1984). A U.S.-built Iranian warplane fired at least five missiles at a tanker.** UPI Archives, 16 May. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1984/05/16/A-US-built-Iranian-warplane-fired-at-least-five-missiles/2004052345288) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Insurers sought "massive increases in premiums", and Saudi fighters scrambled but broke off the chase. Reliability: Wire report from the time.
- **UPI (1984). Kuwait's oil minister criticized the United States.** UPI Archives, 21 May. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1984/05/21/Kuwaits-oil-minister-criticized-the-United-States-today-for/8218453960000) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Kuwait criticised the US response to tanker attacks, early evidence of Gulf states seeking outside protection that led to reflagging. Reliability: Wire report from the time.
- **UPI (1984). Iranian warplanes attacked and crippled a Liberian-registered tanker.** UPI Archives, 24 May. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1984/05/24/Iranian-warplanes-attacked-and-crippled-a-Liberian-registered-tanker-Thursday/5251454219200/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Iran's attacks spread to neutral tankers in 1984. Reliability: Wire report from the time.
- **Michigan Daily / UPI (1984). Sinking of the Fidelity near Kharg Island.** *Michigan Daily*, May. [link](https://digital.bentley.umich.edu/midaily/mdp.39015071755370/132) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The bulk carrier *Fidelity* sank near Kharg, the first sinking since Iraqi planes hit the 357,000-ton *Safina al Arab* on 25 April; it was unclear whose jets did it. Reliability: Faithful print reprint of UPI wire copy; exact date not shown.
- **UPI (1984). Here is a chronology of Persian Gulf tanker attacks.** UPI Archives, 24 June. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1984/06/24/Here-is-a-chronology-of-Persian-Gulf-tanker-attacks/6779456897600/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A list of 1984 attacks so far; Iraqi claims were usually unconfirmed, and the only confirmed strike that day hit the *Alexander the Great* at Kharg. Reliability: Wire chronology compiled at the time; Iraqi claims were often exaggerated.
- **UPI (1984). Iraqi missile hits Swiss-owned tanker.** UPI Archives, 27 June. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1984/06/27/Iraqi-missile-hits-Swiss-owned-tanker/5958457156800/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The *Tiburon* was hit leaving Kharg and eight crew died, the largest loss of life in the Tanker War so far. Reliability: Wire report from the time.
- **UPI (1984). British supertanker hit by missiles.** UPI Archives, 10 July. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1984/07/10/British-supertanker-hit-by-missiles/1953458280000/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: More than 40 neutral ships had been hit by mid-July 1984, most of them by Iraq. Reliability: Wire report from the time.
- **UPI (1984). Iraq claims strike on Kharg Island facility.** UPI Archives, 20 September. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1984/09/20/Iraq-claims-strike-on-Kharg-Island-facility/6424464500800/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Lloyd's of London had confirmed 50 strikes on Gulf shipping so far in 1984. Reliability: Wire report citing Lloyd's, a strong industry source; Iraq's strike claim is its own.
- **UPI (1987). Stark radar failed; Stark-class frigate flunks Navy Exocet test.** UPI Archives, 19 and 27 May. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/05/19/The-USS-Starks-sophisticated-radar-system-failed-to-pick/3677548395200/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The *Stark*'s radar failed to pick up the threat and the ship never fired at the jet or its missiles, a technology lesson. Reliability: Wire reports from the time based on US official sources.
- **UPI (1987). U.S. officials say Navy forces have the ability to deal with mines.** UPI Archives, 27 July. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/07/27/US-officials-say-Navy-forces-have-the-ability-to/2431554356800) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Divers found a 30-by-10-foot hole in *Bridgeton*; the Navy was "forced to hide its ships behind the wounded Bridgeton" because it had no minesweepers ready. Reliability: Wire report based on official US sources.
- **UPI (1987). The reflagged Kuwaiti tanker Gas Prince and its escort.** UPI Archives, 2 August. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/08/02/The-reflagged-Kuwaiti-tanker-Gas-Prince-and-its-escort/2432554875200) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A follow-up on the first convoy's progress. Reliability: Wire report from the time; little detail in the snippet.
- **UPI (1987). Iranian speedboats attacked a Kuwaiti freighter.** UPI Archives, 31 August. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/08/31/Iranian-speedboats-Monday-attacked-a-Kuwaiti-freighter-in-the/2888557380800/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Iranian small-boat "swarm" attacks, a type of threat also seen in 2026. Reliability: Wire report from the time.
- **UPI (1987). Four U.S. Navy destroyers demolished an armed Iranian oil platform.** UPI Archives, 19 October. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/10/19/Four-US-Navy-destroyers-demolished-an-armed-Iranian-oil/2384561614400/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: US retaliation (Operation Nimble Archer) against the Rashadat (Rostam) platform after the Silkworm attack. Reliability: Wire report from the time; check the platform name on the page.
- **UPI (1987). 22nd convoy; Bridgeton returns to the gulf.** UPI Archives, 28 and 30 December. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/12/30/Bridgeton-enters-gulf-area-where-it-hit-mine-five-months-ago/8297567838800/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The 22nd US-escorted convoy since July 1987, with the repaired *Bridgeton* leading the last one of the year. Reliability: Wire reports from the time.
- **UPI (1987). Mines paralyze shipping in Gulf of Oman port.** UPI Archives, 12 and 15 August. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/08/12/Mines-paralyze-shipping-in-Gulf-of-Oman-port/5320555739200/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Mines found off Fujairah, outside Hormuz, halted port traffic, showing mines spread disruption beyond the strait. Reliability: Wire reports from the time.
- **UPI (1987). Four Royal Navy minesweepers left their Scottish base.** UPI Archives, 17 and 24 August. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/08/17/Four-Royal-Navy-minesweepers-left-their-Scottish-base-today/1809556171200/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The British ships left about three weeks after the *Bridgeton* strike, coordinating with the US. Reliability: Wire reports from the time.
- **UPI (1987). Sea Stallion minesweeper helicopters expand operations.** UPI Archives, 13 and 23 September. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/09/13/The-Navys-Sea-Stallion-minesweeper-helicopters-are-expanding-operations/3099558504000) — **Grade B2**. Summary: US RH-53 minesweeping helicopters, the same type used at Suez in 1974, searched the Gulf for mines. Reliability: Wire reports from the time.
- **UPI (1987). British mine sweepers began operations in the Persian Gulf.** UPI Archives, 28 September. [link](https://www.upi.com/amp/Archives/1987/09/28/British-mine-sweepers-began-operations-in-the-Persian-Gulf/2503559800000/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Royal Navy minesweepers started work inside the Gulf. Reliability: Wire report from the time.
- **UPI (1989). Four U.S. warships withdraw from Persian Gulf.** UPI Archives, 2 April 1989. [link](https://www.upi.com/amp/Archives/1989/04/02/Four-US-warships-withdraw-from-Persian-Gulf/9819607496400) — **Grade B2**. Summary: European minesweepers had gone home after clearing "only a few dozen mines", suggesting presence mattered more than mines removed. Reliability: Wire report written just after the operation, with a retrospective judgement.
- **UPI (1988). Iraq renews Persian Gulf attacks.** UPI Archives, 11 January. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1988/01/11/Iraq-renews-Persian-Gulf-attacks/7010568875600/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Tanker attacks went on despite the convoys. Reliability: Wire report from the time.
- **UPI (1988). U.S. forces destroyed two Iranian oil platforms.** UPI Archives, 18 April. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1988/04/18/US-forces-destroyed-two-Iranian-oil-platforms-in-the/6703577339200/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: In Operation Praying Mantis, four days after the mining, the US destroyed the Sassan and Sirri platforms and sank or crippled up to six Iranian vessels, including *Sahand*. Reliability: Same-day wire report; early Pentagon loss figures vary.
- **Washington Post (1988). The day in detail: what happened where.** *The Washington Post*, 19 April. [link](https://www.washingtonpost.com/archive/politics/1988/04/19/the-day-in-detail-what-happened-where/ca524ecc-94c6-4efc-a32a-05b9b95e69f1/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A step-by-step account of the 18 April battle, in which the frigates *Sahand* and *Sabalan* were crippled or hit. Reliability: Paper of record based on US briefings; its Iranian loss figures differ from UPI's.
- **UPI (1988). Iraqi jets attacked four tankers anchored at an Iranian terminal.** UPI Archives, 14 May. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1988/05/14/Iraqi-jets-attacked-four-tankers-anchored-at-an-Iranian/9675579585600/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Iraqi attacks continued after Praying Mantis, showing US protection covered only US-flagged ships. Reliability: Wire report from the time.
- **UPI (1988). Chronology of Persian Gulf War (three parts).** UPI Archives, 7–9 August. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1988/08/07/Chronology-of-Persian-Gulf-War1987/1822586929600/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Day-by-day timelines from 1980 to 1988; the ceasefire took effect on 20 August 1988 with UN observers. Reliability: Wire chronology compiled at the time; earlier errors may carry over, so check key dates.
- **UPI (1988). Iran-Iraq peace talks recess after 20 days.** UPI Archives, 13 September. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1988/09/13/Iran-Iraq-peace-talks-recess-after-20-days/6007590126400/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Geneva talks made "very little if any progress", so the ceasefire held without a full peace. Reliability: Wire report quoting the UN mediator.
- **Washington Post (1987). Kuwait rejects U.S. offer of Gulf tanker escorts.** *The Washington Post*, 28 March. [link](https://www.washingtonpost.com/archive/politics/1987/03/28/kuwait-rejects-us-offer-of-gulf-tanker-escorts/2d0b325d-845a-43f0-8d7c-3bd110d6186c/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Kuwait at first refused US escorts to avoid more superpower presence, after a 7 March offer to reflag 11 tankers. Reliability: Paper of record using diplomatic sources; a snapshot, since Kuwait later accepted.
