- **Washington Post (1987). Panel faults officers of USS Stark.** *The Washington Post*, 14 June. [link](https://www.washingtonpost.com/archive/politics/1987/06/14/panel-faults-officers-of-uss-stark/b199a8e6-8552-4494-9089-167e78a9605b/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A House committee said *Stark*'s officers reacted too late to the Iraqi jet. Reliability: Paper of record reporting preliminary findings with a political framing.
- **UPI (1987). Nunn: Reflagging Kuwaiti tankers risky, unpredictable.** UPI Archives, 29 June. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/06/29/Nunn-Reflagging-Kuwaiti-tankers-risky-unpredictable/9769551937600/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The Senate Armed Services chairman warned reflagging was risky, showing US policy was debated and was a US–Kuwait deal, not a multilateral one. Reliability: Wire report of one partly partisan politician's view.
- **Washington Post (1987). Escorted ships slip into Persian Gulf.** *The Washington Post*, 9 August. [link](https://www.washingtonpost.com/archive/politics/1987/08/09/escorted-ships-slip-into-persian-gulf/af70e933-e9d7-4e36-8e37-dd35937dd6da/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A later US-escorted convoy passed Hormuz into the Gulf, showing traffic kept moving. Reliability: Paper of record; little detail in the snippet.
- **UPI (1987). Once obscure port caught up in Iran-Iraq war.** UPI Archives, 17 August. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/08/17/Once-obscure-port-caught-up-in-Iran-Iraq-war/3779556171200/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Lloyd's began charging war-risk premiums at Fujairah, outside the strait, after nearby mines, so the risk zone was spreading. Reliability: Wire report; no premium rate given.
- **UPI (1987). Tanker sinks in Persian Gulf.** UPI Archives, 10 December. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/12/10/Tanker-sinks-in-Persian-Gulf/8340566110800/) — **Grade B3**. Summary: The *Norman Atlantic*, insured for $7.5m, sank; related coverage cited 447 ships attacked since 1981, 178 in 1987. Reliability: Wire report, but no source was found for the 447/178 counts and other Lloyd's totals differ.
- **UPI (1984). Saudi F-15s shot down Iranian fighter-bombers; count corrected to one.** UPI Archives, 5 and 12 June. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1984/06/05/Saudi-Arabian-fighters-shot-down-two-Iranian-fighter-bombers-in/7626455256000/) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Saudi jets guided by US AWACS engaged Iranian aircraft; a week later the Pentagon cut the count from two to one. Reliability: The two wire reports contradict each other, showing why early claims need checking.
- **UPI (1987). Diplomats say attack on U.S. ship was tragic accident.** UPI Archives, 17 May. [link](https://www.upi.com/Archives/1987/05/17/Diplomats-say-attack-on-US-ship-was-tragic-accident/1013548222400/) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Same-day report on the Iraqi attack on *Stark* about 85 miles north-east of Bahrain; early reports said 28 dead, later 37. Reliability: Written on the day, so casualty figures were early and wrong.
- **Christian Science Monitor (1987). Stark's captain says Iraqi missile spotted "seconds" before hit.** *CSM*, 21 May. [link](https://www.csmonitor.com/1987/0521/ocrew.html) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Captain Glenn Brindel said the crew detected the missile only seconds before impact, a sign of detection gaps. Reliability: National daily quoting one captain who had a personal stake, as he was later relieved.
- **Christian Science Monitor (1987). Gulf fleet navigates sea of problems.** *CSM*, 29 July. [link](https://www.csmonitor.com/1987/0729/onavy.html) — **Grade C2**. Summary: US warships were more at risk from mines than tankers; with little US mine-clearing capacity, the convoy slowed below 9 knots. Reliability: National daily analysis written days after *Bridgeton*, before the outcome.
- **Christian Science Monitor (1984). News In Brief.** *CSM*, 25 June and 27 August. [link](https://www.csmonitor.com/1984/0625/062528.html) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Short items on 1984 Gulf tanker strikes, useful for counting incidents. Reliability: Reputable daily, but only brief notes.
- **MERIP (1988). NATO goes to the Persian Gulf.** *Middle East Report*, December. [link](https://merip.org/1988/11/nato-goes-to-the-persian-gulf/) — **Grade C2** (graded here). Summary: Explains that the European minesweeping effort was coordinated loosely through the Western European Union rather than NATO. Reliability: Analysis magazine with a critical, left-leaning view of Western policy; seen only as a snippet, but consistent with UPI reports.

### News and trade press, 2026

- **Bloomberg (2026). IEA confirms huge release of emergency oil stockpiles to calm prices.** Bloomberg, 11 March. [link](https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-03-11/iea-confirms-huge-release-of-emergency-oil-stockpiles) — **Grade B1**. Summary: The IEA's 32 members agreed a record 400m-barrel release, a third of their 1.2bn barrels of stocks, as Hormuz exports fell below 10% of pre-war levels. Reliability: Major news agency, figures confirmed by CNBC and the IEA itself, but paywalled; cite the IEA release for the numbers.
- **CNBC (2026). Brent oil closes at $100 after Iran's new supreme leader says Strait of Hormuz must remain closed.** CNBC, 12 March. [link](https://www.cnbc.com/2026/03/12/oil-prices-jump-iea-record-reserve-release-markets-doubt-relief.html) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Brent closed above $100 for the first time since August 2022, a day after the IEA release, as markets doubted stocks could replace a closed strait. Reliability: Major financial broadcaster; consistent with EIA and World Bank data, though daily prices are noisy.
- **Reuters via gCaptain (2026). Shipping slows after Iran says it has again shut the Strait of Hormuz.** gCaptain (Reuters), 22 June. [link](https://gcaptain.com/shipping-slows-after-iran-says-it-has-again-shut-the-strait-of-hormuz/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: The IRGC re-closed the strait on 21 June; Kpler counted 5 transits (down from 26), Windward 12 (from 35), while CENTCOM claimed 55. Reliability: Reuters wire copy reposted by trade press; CENTCOM's figure is a claim by a party to the war.
- **Lloyd's List (2026). Crude tanker rates in unchartered territory; VLCC index tops $420K.** Lloyd's List, c. 2 March. [link](https://www.lloydslist.com/LL1156492/Crude-tanker-rates-in-unchartered-territory-VLCC-index-tops-420K) — **Grade B1**. Summary: The Gulf–China supertanker index (TD3C) hit a record $423,736 a day as attacks and insurers pulling cover effectively closed the strait. Reliability: The leading shipping paper of record reporting Baltic Exchange data, which are modelled assessments, not deals actually paid.
- **Gard (2026). Escalating conflict in Middle East threatens Gulf shipping.** Gard P&I club, early March. [link](https://www.gard.no/en/insights/escalating-israel-iran-conflict-threatens-gulf-shipping/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Explains the JWLA-033 listed-area expansion (five countries added) and says Hormuz was declared a High Risk Area on 2 March 2026. Reliability: Briefing from a major ship insurer to its members, confirmed by the JWC circular; insurers can gain when risk is stressed.
- **Insurance Journal / Bloomberg (2026). Lloyd's CEO says it's critical Mideast war cover stays available.** Insurance Journal, 19 March. [link](https://www.insurancejournal.com/news/international/2026/03/19/862608.htm) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Lloyd's chief Patrick Tiernan said cover must stay on offer even though it cost about 5% of a ship's value, roughly five times early-war levels. Reliability: Bloomberg agency copy, confirmed by Howden Re; Lloyd's has a commercial interest in the message.
- **S&P Global (2026). Middle East shipping insurance costs rise on Hormuz risks: Marsh.** S&P Global, 22 July. [link](https://www.spglobal.com/energy/en/news-research/latest-news/shipping/072226-middle-east-shipping-insurance-costs-rise-on-hormuz-risks-marsh) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Marsh said Hormuz additional premiums jumped again to 7.5–10% of hull value, up from 1–3% weeks earlier, with global war-hull capacity only $2.5–3bn. Reliability: Commodity news firm quoting a broker that profits from high premiums; the figure is confirmed by *The National*.
- **Lloyd's List Intelligence (2026). Strait of Hormuz Brief: 29 July 2026.** Lloyd's List Intelligence. [link](https://www.lloydslistintelligence.com/resources/blog/strait-of-hormuz-brief-29-july-2026) — **Grade B1**. Summary: A regular data brief reporting hull war quotes back at 7.5–10% of hull value, the peak-crisis level. Reliability: Commercial shipping-data firm selling its products; figures match Marsh and S&P.
- **Washington Post (2026). Iranian attacks sink two ships in Strait of Hormuz.** *The Washington Post*, 14 October. [link](https://www.washingtonpost.com/world/2026/10/14/iranian-attack-sinks-container-ship-strait-hormuz/) — **Grade B1**. Summary: October was one of the most violent months of the war; the Singapore-flagged *GFS Pride* sank and its 21 crew were saved. Reliability: US paper of record, with the sinking confirmed by several outlets.
- **CNBC (2026). China calls U.S. blockade of the Strait of Hormuz "dangerous and irresponsible".** CNBC, 14 April. [link](https://www.cnbc.com/2026/04/14/china-us-strait-of-hormuz-war-donald-trump-oil-energy-crisis-conflict-middle-east-iran.html) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Beijing condemned the US blockade of Iranian ports and called a full ceasefire the only way to ease the strait. Reliability: Major US broadcaster reporting China's official statement, confirmed by other outlets.
- **CNBC (2026). Container ship sinks in Strait of Hormuz as Iran steps up attacks.** CNBC, 14 October. [link](https://www.cnbc.com/2026/10/14/container-ship-sinks-strait-hormuz-as-iran-war-attacks.html) — **Grade B1**. Summary: The IMO counted the third ship sunk in the war; Hormuz exports averaged 8.5 mb/d in the week to 12 October, about 40% below normal (Kpler). Reliability: Major US broadcaster citing IMO and Kpler, consistent with other reports.
- **S&P Global (2026). Marine insurers seek solutions as Gulf shipping threats evolve.** S&P Global, 6 March. [link](https://www.spglobal.com/energy/en/news-research/latest-news/refined-products/030626-marine-insurers-seek-solutions-as-gulf-shipping-threats-evolve) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Major insurers ended Gulf war cover from 5 March and transits fell from about 135 a day in February to under 10 in early March. Reliability: Commodity news firm, partly paywalled; the transit figures came from a summary of several S&P articles.
- **Carrier Management (2026). Marine insurers and Gulf shipping (report).** Carrier Management, 5 March. [link](https://www.carriermanagement.com/news/2026/03/05/285346.htm) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The Lloyd's Market Association said cover was available, and shipowners said crew safety, not price, decided whether they sailed. Reliability: Insurance trade press; the LMA has a commercial interest in saying cover was on offer.
- **Lloyd's List (2026). Gulf war risk premiums topping double-digit millions of dollars per trip.** Lloyd's List, c. 11 March. [link](https://www.lloydslist.com/LL1156586/Gulf-war-risk-premiums-topping-double-digit-millions-of-dollars-per-trip) — **Grade B2**. Summary: War cover was quoted at 7.5% of hull value, against 0.15–0.25% before the war, so over $10m for one supertanker trip; the 1980s rate was about 5%. Reliability: Shipping paper of record quoting brokers, who gain when premiums rise.
- **Rigzone / Bloomberg (2026). Treasury Sec says Hormuz insurance program to start soon.** Rigzone, 27 March. [link](https://www.rigzone.com/news/wire/treasury_sec_says_hormuz_insurance_program_to_start_soon-27-mar-2026-183317-article/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The US Treasury said a state-backed Hormuz insurance scheme would launch soon, but analysts said Iranian strikes, not a lack of insurance, were the real brake. Reliability: Bloomberg wire copy reporting an official US statement.
- **gCaptain / Bloomberg (2026). U.S. counts far more Hormuz ship transits than commercial tracking data shows.** gCaptain, 5 June. [link](https://gcaptain.com/u-s-counts-far-more-hormuz-ship-transits-than-commercial-tracking-data-shows/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: CENTCOM counted nearly 1,000 commercial transits in about two months, but ship-tracking data found just over 650 since 8 April, probably because ships sailed "dark". Reliability: Bloomberg copy; a key warning that counters give very different numbers.
- **Insurance Journal (2026). Some war insurers advise shipowners to pause Hormuz voyages after attacks.** Insurance Journal, 8 July. [link](https://www.insurancejournal.com/news/international/2026/07/08/876716.htm) — **Grade B2**. Summary: After renewed tanker attacks and the ceasefire breakdown, some underwriters told clients to stop Hormuz voyages and the IMO said to avoid sailings. Reliability: Agency-style copy based on unnamed sources.
- **Insurance Journal / Rigzone (Bloomberg) (2026). Hormuz ship insurance demand drops.** Insurance Journal, 9 July. [link](https://www.insurancejournal.com/news/international/2026/07/09/876855.htm) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Quote requests fell as owners stopped planning voyages; Marsh put cover at 2–6% of vessel value, about $6m for a $100m tanker. Reliability: Bloomberg copy quoting a broker with a commercial interest.
- **Lloyd's List (2026). Hormuz war risk premium surge confirmed.** Lloyd's List, c. 10 July. [link](https://www.lloydslist.com/LL1157799/Hormuz-war-risk-premium-surge-confirmed) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The largest broker said Hormuz hull war premiums had settled at "mid-single-digit" percentages of ship value, a "new normal". Reliability: Shipping paper of record quoting a broker; paywalled.
- **OE Digital / Reuters (Kpler data) (2026). Strait of Hormuz commodity vessel transits at three-month low.** Offshore Engineer, 24–25 August. [link](https://www.oedigital.com/news/542400-strait-of-hormuz-commodity-vessel-transits-at-three-month-low) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Only two commodity ships entered the Gulf on 24 August against a 10-day average of 14; oil flow was about 5 mb/d against about 20 mb/d pre-war. Reliability: Reuters copy using commercial tracking data; Iran's "45 blacklisted tankers" is its own claim.
- **Lloyd's List (2026). VLCC market hits historic high in latest phase of Hormuz crisis.** Lloyd's List, c. 8–9 September. [link](https://www.lloydslist.com/LL1158394/VLCC-market-hits-historic-high-in-latest-phase-of-Hormuz-crisis) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The Gulf–China tanker index set a record $759,969 a day on 8 September, 26% above the March peak. Reliability: Shipping paper of record reporting Baltic Exchange modelled assessments, not fixtures paid.
- **gCaptain / Reuters (2026). Strait of Hormuz ship crossings remain in single digits, data shows.** gCaptain, c. 16 September. [link](https://gcaptain.com/strait-of-hormuz-ship-crossings-remain-in-single-digits-data-shows/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Four transits on 16 September, down from seven the day before, against a 10-day average of about 15–18. Reliability: Reuters copy based on commercial tracking data.
- **Argus Media (2026). UAE speeds up Fujairah crude export capacity expansion.** Argus, 2026. [link](https://www.argusmedia.com/en/news-and-insights/latest-market-news/2827434-uae-speeds-up-fujairah-crude-export-capacity-expansion) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The Habshan–Fujairah pipeline, rated about 1.5 mb/d, has run at 1.7–1.8 mb/d; a second line of 1.5 mb/d is due in 2027. Reliability: Major energy price-reporting agency; partly paywalled and the date is unconfirmed.
- **Foreign Policy (2026). Oil Is Leaving the Strait of Hormuz Again, But Gasoline and Diesel Prices Will Remain High.** *Foreign Policy*, 1 October. [link](https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/01/oil-strait-hormuz-iran-trump-war-gulf-gas-diesel-prices/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Weekly crude flows are near pre-war levels via a costly US-guarded southern route, but about 3 mb/d of refined products are still missing (Kpler). Reliability: Respected US magazine news-analysis; snippet only.
- **Foreign Policy (2026). Trump's Iran Deal Leaves Hormuz Less Than Open.** *Foreign Policy*, 18 June. [link](https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/06/18/iran-deal-mou-hormuz-shipping-oil/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The June MoU left shipping terms vague, so traffic recovered only partly. Reliability: Respected US magazine news-analysis; snippet only.
- **Foreign Policy (2026). Iran Puts "Toll Booth" in Strait of Hormuz, Charging Fees for Ships' Safe Passage.** *Foreign Policy*, 26 March. [link](https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/03/26/iran-strait-hormuz-war-tolls-shipping/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The IRGC routed ships near Iran's coast and charged "safe passage" fees; the IMO condemned tolls by either side. Reliability: Respected US magazine news-analysis; snippet only.
- **Reuters via Al-Monitor (2026). Analysis: Hormuz Closure Divides the Fortunes of Middle Eastern Oil States.** Al-Monitor, April. [link](https://www.al-monitor.com/originals/2026/04/analysis-hormuz-closure-divides-fortunes-middle-eastern-oil-states) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Iraq's and Kuwait's export revenues fell about three-quarters year-on-year in March, while Saudi Arabia (7 mb/d East–West pipeline), the UAE and Oman had bypass routes. Reliability: Reuters wire analysis; strong Gulf data, snippet only.
- **Reuters via Al-Monitor (2026). Gulf Countries' Plans to Bypass Hormuz Still Far Off, Experts Warn.** Al-Monitor, May. [link](https://www.al-monitor.com/originals/2026/05/gulf-countries-plans-bypass-hormuz-still-far-experts-warn) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Pipelines cover only a fraction of pre-war exports, a trans-Arabian gas pipeline never progressed, and GCC rail is delayed. Reliability: Reuters wire analysis quoting experts; snippet only.
- **Lloyd's List (2026). Two Chinese tankers exit Hormuz as US and Iran trade threats.** Lloyd's List, 2026. [link](https://www.lloydslist.com/LL1157224/Two-Chinese-tankers-exit-Hormuz-as-US-and-Iran-trade-threats) — **Grade B2**. Summary: COSCO's *Yuan Gui Yang* and another tanker used an Iran-approved corridor south of Larak Island after 10 Chinese-owned ships got out the week before. Reliability: Specialist, highly reliable shipping paper; paywalled.
- **CNN (2026). Visualizing shipping through the Strait of Hormuz since war began.** CNN, 29 April. [link](https://www.cnn.com/2026/04/29/world/iran-war-gulf-hormuz-shipping-maps-intl-vis) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Maps showing the collapse in tanker traffic after the war began. Reliability: Major US broadcaster's data graphic; check the underlying tracker.
- **CNN (2026). Iran has lost considerable leverage in the Strait of Hormuz.** CNN, 29 September. [link](https://www.cnn.com/2026/09/29/economy/oil-strait-of-hormuz-iran) — **Grade B2**. Summary: US-guided "dark" transits and the blockade weakened Iran's grip; Kpler showed Hormuz crude exports back at pre-war levels in September. Reliability: Major US broadcaster; analysis with a US-leaning framing.
- **CNN (2026). After more oil left the Gulf, Iran tries to regain its stranglehold on shipping.** CNN, 11 October. [link](https://www.cnn.com/2026/10/11/middleeast/iran-hormuz-oil-analysis-intl) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Six ships were struck in one day, the most since the war began. Reliability: Major US broadcaster; snippet only.
- **CNN (2026). How the US-Iran war upended two critical global trade routes.** CNN, 17 October. [link](https://www.cnn.com/2026/10/17/economy/us-iran-war-shipping-trade-hormuz-bab-el-mandab-strait-intl-vis) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A visual explainer on how the war disrupted both Hormuz and Bab el-Mandeb. Reliability: Major US broadcaster's graphic; no figures seen.
- **Washington Post / AP (2026). Iran increases threat to Strait of Hormuz shipping, and other Middle East developments.** *The Washington Post*, 13 October. [link](https://www.washingtonpost.com/national/2026/10/13/saudi-arabia-yemen-houthis-october-13-2026/67e392ca-c6cf-11f1-aefb-abe4112d087e_story.html) — **Grade B2**. Summary: UKMTO reported 12 attacks in a week; Kpler flows averaged 13.1 mb/d against 17.1 mb/d pre-war, with oil above $100. Reliability: AP wire roundup in a paper of record.
- **Washington Post / AP (2026). Oil tanker arrives in South Korea after passing through the Strait of Hormuz in mid-April.** *The Washington Post*, 8 May. [link](https://www.washingtonpost.com/business/2026/05/08/south-korea-oil-tanker-iran-hormuz/876bef2c-4a9a-11f1-a119-857cd2bf4fd4_story.html) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A Korea-bound tanker that crossed Hormuz in mid-April reached South Korea. Reliability: AP wire copy; little detail in the snippet.
- **Lloyd's List (2026). Tanker rates, already higher than most imagined possible, just spiked again.** Lloyd's List, late September/October. [link](https://www.lloydslist.com/LL1158620/Tanker-rates-already-higher-than-most-imagined-possible-just-spiked-again) — **Grade B3**. Summary: The Gulf–China tanker index hit another record of $1.33m a day, up 10% in a week. Reliability: Shipping paper of record, but the date is unclear and the figure stands alone.
- **S&P Global (2026). War cover available for Hormuz trades but transit challenges remain: insurers.** S&P Global, 23 June. [link](https://www.spglobal.com/energy/en/news-research/latest-news/shipping/062326-war-cover-available-for-hormuz-trades-but-transit-challenges-remain-insurers) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Cover could be bought again after the June deal; Marsh put the premium at about 3–4% of hull value against 0.25% pre-war. Reliability: Commodity news firm, but the figure came from a summary of several S&P articles.
- **CNN (2026). 60 targets struck, 18M barrels of oil protected: the US military's record day in the Strait of Hormuz.** CNN, 2 September. [link](https://www.cnn.com/2026/09/02/politics/latest-us-strikes-against-iran-marked-a-shift-in-strategy) — **Grade B3**. Summary: US forces shifted to actively guiding tankers through and striking Iranian targets. Reliability: Major broadcaster, but the headline figures come from the US military, a party to the war.
- **Insurance Journal / Reuters (2026). Maritime insurance premiums surge as Iran conflict widens.** Insurance Journal, 6 March. [link](https://www.insurancejournal.com/news/international/2026/03/06/860842.htm) — **Grade B6**. Summary: War-risk premiums jumped as the conflict spread to Gulf states, while insurers cancelled Gulf cover from 5 March. Reliability: Reuters copy, but no figure was visible in the snippet.
- **S&P Global (2026). War risk insurance cost off highs but still elevated in Persian Gulf.** S&P Global, 30 March. [link](https://www.spglobal.com/energy/en/news-research/latest-news/shipping/033026-war-risk-insurance-cost-off-highs-but-still-elevated-in-persian-gulf) — **Grade B6**. Summary: By late March premiums had eased from the mid-March peak but stayed far above pre-war levels. Reliability: Commodity news firm; no figure was visible, so check the article.
- **S&P Global (2026). Hormuz insurance costs deter clean tankers more than crude carriers: brokerage.** S&P Global, 16 June. [link](https://www.spglobal.com/energy/en/news-research/refined-products/061626-hormuz-insurance-costs-deter-clean-tankers-more-than-crude-carriers-brokerage) — **Grade B6**. Summary: Product tankers earn less per voyage than crude carriers, so the same war premium deters them more. Reliability: Commodity news firm quoting a broker; no figure visible.
- **Foreign Policy (2026). Navigating the Strait of Hormuz Is Dangerous but Vital.** *Foreign Policy*, 9 March. [link](https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/03/09/hormuz-strait-insurance-seafarers/) — **Grade B6**. Summary: Appears to cover insurers and seafarers; a companion piece said the strait was "more dangerous than it's been for decades". Reliability: Respected magazine, but this article's content could not be confirmed.
- **Insurance Journal (2026). Chubb outlines structure of $20B Gulf reinsurance facility, now including liability cover.** Insurance Journal, 23 March. [link](https://www.insurancejournal.com/news/international/2026/03/23/863026.htm) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The US government (DFC)-backed $20bn facility led by Chubb aimed to get insured ships moving again; liability cover was added after Moody's criticism. Reliability: Insurance trade press staff story.
- **Insurance Journal (2026). US doubles Hormuz reinsurance guarantees to $40 billion with new partners.** Insurance Journal, 6 April. [link](https://www.insurancejournal.com/news/international/2026/04/06/864586.htm) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The facility was doubled to $40bn as AIG and Berkshire Hathaway joined Chubb, a large state–market cooperation scheme. Reliability: Insurance trade press staff story.
- **Insurance Journal (2026). Chubb leads new Lloyd's war risk consortium for Hormuz shipping.** Insurance Journal, 19 June. [link](https://www.insurancejournal.com/news/international/2026/06/19/874528.htm) — **Grade C2**. Summary: After the 18 June deal a Chubb-led consortium offered up to $200m each for hull, P&I and cargo on Hormuz voyages. Reliability: Insurance trade press staff story.
- **gCaptain (2026). By the Numbers: U.S. Claims Control of Hormuz as Ship Attacks Hit New High.** gCaptain, 15 October. [link](https://gcaptain.com/by-the-numbers-u-s-claims-control-of-hormuz-as-ship-attacks-hit-new-high/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: CENTCOM claimed 121 escorted transits on 11–14 October (about 30 a day, 22% of pre-war), but JMIC rated the threat SEVERE and *GFS Pride* sank. Reliability: Trade press weighing US military claims against JMIC data.
- **Forces News (2026). Does Royal Navy's Strait of Hormuz deployment mark new era of maritime warfare?** Forces News (BFBS), 2026. [link](https://www.forcesnews.com/services/navy/does-royal-navy-strait-hormuz-deployment-mark-new-era-maritime-warfare) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The UK sent mine-hunting drones and RFA *Lyme Bay*, which left Gibraltar in late May with over 100 specialists, for the France–UK mission. Reliability: Funded through the UK defence ministry's broadcasting service, so supportive of the military.
- **Maritime Executive (2026). Insurance and naval escorts may not be enough to reboot Gulf shipping.** *The Maritime Executive*, early March. [link](https://maritime-executive.com/article/insurance-and-naval-escorts-may-not-be-enough-to-reboot-gulf-shipping) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Argued an escort mission looked unlikely to succeed given the fighting and lack of ships, citing the Red Sea where escorts did not bring traffic back. Reliability: Good trade press, but an early forecast that events may have overtaken.
- **Syndicated wire story on wwbl.com (2026). Strait of Hormuz crisis traps 6,000 sailors as attacks on ships mount, UN maritime chief says.** wwbl.com, 18 October. [link](https://www.wwbl.com/2026/10/18/strait-of-hormuz-crisis-traps-6000-sailors-as-attacks-on-ships-mount-un-maritime-chief-says/) — **Grade D2**. Summary: The IMO head described a "double blockade" by Iran and the US; about 6,000 seafarers were trapped and 24 ships attacked in two weeks. Reliability: Unattributed repost of wire copy (probably AP) on a local radio site; 6,000 is confirmed, the 24 is not.
- **Capital News Point (2026). Iran's IRGC Navy declares Strait of Hormuz closed again, warns global shipping.** Capital News Point, 17 October. [link](https://english.capitalnewspoint.com/2026/10/17/irans-irgc-navy-declares-strait-of-hormuz-closed-again-warns-global-shipping/) — **Grade F3**. Summary: Reports an October IRGC closure; PortWatch showed 2 transits on 11 October against about 85 a day pre-crisis. Reliability: Unknown outlet whose owners could not be found, and no independent report of this closure was found; replace it.

### Regional press (Iran and the Gulf)

- **Al Jazeera (2026). Iran-Iraq Tanker War redux? Why the Strait of Hormuz crisis is different.** Al Jazeera (Qatar), 24 April. [link](https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2026/4/24/iran-iraq-tanker-war-redux-why-the-strait-of-hormuz-crisis-is-different) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Compares Earnest Will with 2026: escorts did not stop losses then, and the 1987–88 ratio suggests about eight warships to escort 11 merchant ships. Reliability: Qatari state-funded broadcaster, but this analysis is not about Qatar's own policy; snippet only.
- **Al Jazeera (2026). "De-escalation": Europe hesitates as US pushes for Hormuz naval mission.** Al Jazeera, 7 August. [link](https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2026/8/7/european-caution-slowing-us-push-for-hormuz-naval-coalition) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The UK–France mission looked stalled, as Europeans would not join a war they did not start without clear Gulf backing. Reliability: Qatari state-funded broadcaster, consistent with other reports of the mission's delay.
- **Al Jazeera (2026). Saudi, UAE, Iraq: Can three pipelines help oil escape Strait of Hormuz?** Al Jazeera, 27 March. [link](https://www.aljazeera.com/economy/2026/3/27/saudi-uae-iraq-can-three-pipelines-help-oil-escape-strait-of-hormuz) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Gulf bypass pipelines can carry about 9 mb/d against about 20 mb/d through Hormuz; Fujairah exports rose to 1.62 mb/d in March (Kpler). Reliability: Qatari state-funded broadcaster, but data-led using a commercial tracker; capacity estimates vary.
- **The National (2026). Shipping insurance surges again as attacks intensify over Strait of Hormuz.** *The National* (UAE), 17 July. [link](https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/2026/07/17/war-risk-shipping-premium-surges-again-as-tensions-escalate-at-strait-of-hormuz/) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Marsh said war-risk premiums jumped from 1–3% of hull value to 7.5–10%, against about 0.15–0.25% before the war. Reliability: Abu Dhabi royal-owned paper, but the Marsh figures are confirmed by S&P Global; Marsh profits from high premiums.
- **Al Jazeera (2026). How a 95 percent drop in Hormuz traffic changed global shipping.** Al Jazeera, 27 August. [link](https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2026/8/27/how-a-95-percent-drop-in-hormuz-traffic-changed-global-shipping) — **Grade C1**. Summary: About 5 vessels a day crossed from 15 July to 23 August, a drop of about 95% from pre-war levels. Reliability: Qatari state-funded broadcaster, but the figure matches IMO, PortWatch and Reuters; name the tracker.
- **Al Jazeera (2026). US and Iran both claim control of Strait of Hormuz amid attacks on vessels.** Al Jazeera, 17 October. [link](https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2026/10/17/us-and-iran-both-claim-control-of-strait-of-hormuz-amid-attacks-on-vessels) — **Grade C1**. Summary: UKMTO reported at least 12 ships attacked in the week to 16 October while both sides claimed control. Reliability: Qatari state-funded broadcaster; the attack count matches AP.
- **The National (2026). Iran says Strait of Hormuz will not reopen until seven conditions are met.** *The National*, 4 October. [link](https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/gulf/2026/10/04/iran-says-strait-of-hormuz-will-not-reopen-until-its-conditions-are-met/) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Iran's lead negotiator tied reopening to seven conditions based on the June framework; Iran–Oman routes did not mean the strait was open. Reliability: UAE royal-owned paper following the government line, but the conditions are confirmed by many outlets.
- **Press TV (2026). Ship transits through Hormuz Strait at lowest in over two months, shipping data shows.** Press TV (Iran), 8 October. [link](https://www.presstv.co.uk/Detail/2026/10/08/777877/Hormuz-Strait-transits-lowest) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Using Kpler data, ship numbers hit a two-month low while crude flow was about 10.1 mb/d, roughly 74% of pre-war. Reliability: Iranian state broadcaster, but here relaying Western commercial data confirmed by Reuters.
- **Iran International (2026). Hormuz traffic falls to lowest in more than two months.** Iran International (London), 8 October. [link](https://www.iranintl.com/en/202610085164) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Seven commodity ships crossed one Tuesday and crude flows were about 74% of pre-war (Kpler); war-risk cover reached up to $20m for some tankers. Reliability: Iranian opposition channel with reported Saudi-linked funding, but figures match Reuters.
- **Saudi Gazette (2026). Saudi Arabia shuts East-West oil pipeline after multiple attacks.** *Saudi Gazette*, c. 13 September. [link](https://saudigazette.com.sa/article/664495/saudi-arabia/saudi-arabia-shuts-east-west-oil-pipeline-after-multiple-attacks) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Drone attacks, reportedly from Iraqi territory, forced a "precautionary" shutdown of the pipeline that bypasses Hormuz. Reliability: Pro-government Saudi paper, but the shutdown is confirmed by Arab News and Al Jazeera.
- **The National (2020). Iraq-Iran war: a legacy of carnage that shaped the Middle East for 40 years.** *The National*, 2020. [link](https://www.thenationalnews.com/world/iraq-iran-war-a-legacy-of-carnage-that-shaped-the-middle-east-for-40-years-1.1081287) — **Grade C1**. Summary: A retrospective noting more than 450 ships from 15 countries were attacked, about two-thirds by Iraq, and that Kuwait's request led to US reflagging. Reliability: UAE royal-owned paper; the figures are consistent with other histories.
- **Arab News (2025–26). Analysis: What happens if Iran closes the Strait of Hormuz.** *Arab News* (Saudi Arabia). [link](https://www.arabnews.com/middle-east/analysis-what-happens-if-iran-closes-the-strait-of-hormuz-2604618) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Uses the Tanker War as background, recalling the USS *Stark* (37 dead) and a reflagged tanker mined despite US protection. Reliability: Saudi state-linked group; the historical facts are well confirmed, but the date is unconfirmed.
- **Al Jazeera (2026). IEA announces release of 400 million barrels of oil. But is it enough?** Al Jazeera, 13 March. [link](https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2026/3/13/iea-announces-release-of-400-million-barrels-of-oil-but-is-it-enough) — **Grade C2**. Summary: An explainer comparing the record release with the volume lost each day through Hormuz. Reliability: Qatari state-funded broadcaster, and Qatar's LNG goes through Hormuz; no original data.
- **Al Jazeera (2026). Iran, Oman agree on temporary Hormuz route: what we know.** Al Jazeera, 26 August. [link](https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2026/8/26/iran-oman-agree-on-temporary-hormuz-route-what-we-know) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Iran and Oman agreed a temporary bilateral routing arrangement, regional cooperation outside the Western coalition. Reliability: Qatari state-funded explainer; the terms come from the governments involved.
- **Al Jazeera (2026). US says it's clearing Hormuz traffic: why are oil futures beyond $100?** Al Jazeera, 14 September. [link](https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2026/9/14/us-says-its-clearing-hormuz-traffic-why-are-oil-futures-beyond-100) — **Grade C2**. Summary: US claims of escorting ships did not calm markets, and oil futures stayed above $100. Reliability: Qatari state-funded explainer relying partly on US wartime claims.
- **The National (2026). French warships "ready to go now" for Gulf deployment, says admiral.** *The National*, 6 October. [link](https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2026/10/06/french-warships-ready-to-go-now-for-gulf-deployment-says-admiral/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Eight months into the crisis the European mission was still waiting for a US–Iran deal, against about three weeks for minesweepers in 1987. Reliability: UAE state-linked paper quoting a French official presenting his navy well.
- **The National (2026). UK and France to launch mission to secure Strait of Hormuz within days.** *The National*, 6 July. [link](https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/07/06/uk-and-france-to-launch-mission-to-secure-strait-of-hormuz-within-days/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Oman agreed to host the mission's hub at Duqm to clear an estimated 80 mines; the ceasefire collapsed a day later and the mission did not launch. Reliability: UAE royal-owned paper; the mine estimate comes from unnamed reports.
- **The National (2026). UAE's West-East pipeline expansion to become operational in 2027.** *The National*, 15 May. [link](https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/energy/2026/05/15/uaes-west-east-pipeline-expansion-to-become-operational-in-2027-doubling-oil-export-capacity/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: ADNOC's second pipeline beside the 1.5–1.8 mb/d Habshan–Fujairah line would roughly double exports from outside the strait in 2027. Reliability: UAE royal-owned paper promoting a national project.
- **The National (2020). How the Iran-Iraq war shaped oil markets regionally and globally.** *The National*, 2020. [link](https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/how-the-iran-iraq-war-shaped-oil-markets-regionally-and-globally-1.1084279) — **Grade C2**. Summary: A retrospective saying oil flow through Hormuz was never "seriously disrupted" during the Tanker War. Reliability: UAE royal-owned paper; the claim conflicts with some other sources.
- **Gulf News (2026). The Tanker War returns: Why Hormuz risks repeating its dangerous past.** *Gulf News* (UAE), c. March. [link](https://gulfnews.com/world/mena/the-tanker-war-returns-why-hormuz-risks-repeating-its-dangerous-past-1.500483407) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Compares 2026 with the 1980s, warning of mines, misfires and fast escalation in shipping lanes only about 2 miles wide. Reliability: Private but pro-government UAE paper; analysis by a staff writer.
- **Gulf News (2026). Iran declares Hormuz Strait "closed" to all ships, oil tankers.** *Gulf News*, 2026. [link](https://gulfnews.com/world/mena/iran-declares-hormuz-strait-closed-to-all-ships-oil-tankers-1.500570341) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Reports the IRGC Navy ban on all ships; CENTCOM said traffic continued on the southern route under a "severe" JMIC threat rating. Reliability: Pro-government UAE paper; the date is unconfirmed.
- **Gulf News (2026). India pushes diplomacy to reopen Hormuz Strait as Trump urges naval escort.** *Gulf News*, March. [link](https://gulfnews.com/world/mena/india-pushes-diplomacy-to-reopen-hormuz-strait-as-trump-urges-naval-escort-1.500476043) — **Grade C2**. Summary: A senior Indian official said direct talks with Iran were the best way to restart shipping. Reliability: Pro-government UAE paper under press controls; snippet only.
- **Khaleej Times (2026). UAE seeks clarity on terms of US-Iran truce (live blog).** *Khaleej Times* (UAE), c. 8–9 April. [link](https://www.khaleejtimes.com/world/mena/iran-us-israel-ceasefire-day-1-live-updates) — **Grade C2**. Summary: On day one of the Pakistan-brokered truce, the UAE said Hormuz access must be guaranteed and could not be a "bargaining chip". Reliability: Private, pro-government UAE paper's live blog.
- **Khaleej Times (2026). UAE's Adnoc confirms two vessels attacked while transiting Hormuz on August 13.** *Khaleej Times*, c. 14 August. [link](https://www.khaleejtimes.com/business/energy/uae-adnoc-says-two-vessels-attacked-transiting-strait-of-hormuz-august-13) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The second attack on ADNOC ships in a week, with no injuries; the paper counted 16–17 ships attacked since the war began. Reliability: Pro-government UAE paper whose own counts differ.
- **Arab News (2026). Region needs new trade routes with or without Hormuz, UNDP's Al-Dardari tells Arab News.** *Arab News*, c. mid-October. [link](https://www.arabnews.com/middle-east/region-need-new-trade-routes-with-or-without-hormuz-undps-abdallah-al-dardari-tells-arab-news-3006469) — **Grade C2**. Summary: A UN official says the region needs new trade routes, as the strait is still effectively closed six months on. Reliability: Saudi state-linked paper quoting a UN official.
- **Arab News (2026). Saudi Arabia restarts East-West oil pipeline to resume exports from Yanbu.** *Arab News*, c. 22 September. [link](https://www.arabnews.com/saudi-arabia/saudi-arabia-restarts-east-west-oil-pipeline-to-resume-exports-from-yanbu-sources-say-3002654) — **Grade C2**. Summary: After drone attacks shut it on 13 September, the pipeline restarted at reduced rates, later reaching 5.8 mb/d. Reliability: Saudi state-linked paper relaying Reuters sources.
- **Arab News (2026). Two ships sink near Hormuz after being hit by projectiles.** *Arab News*, c. 14–15 October. [link](https://www.arabnews.com/middle-east/two-ships-sink-near-hormuz-after-being-hit-by-projectiles-3006316) — **Grade C2**. Summary: A container ship and a bulk carrier sank; 24 ships were hit in the two weeks to 11 October (FT data). Reliability: Saudi state-linked paper; the sinkings are confirmed by US outlets.
- **Saudi Gazette (2026). Saudi Arabia plans to raise East-West Pipeline capacity to 10 million barrels per day.** *Saudi Gazette*, c. October. [link](https://saudigazette.com.sa/article/665347/saudi-arabia/saudi-arabia-plans-to-expand-east-west-pipeline-capacity-to-10-million-barrels-daily) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The energy minister says the kingdom is considering expanding the bypass pipeline to about 10 mb/d. Reliability: Pro-government Saudi paper reporting a minister's plan.
- **Saudi Gazette (2026). Saudi Arabia rejects weaponizing vital waterways for pressure, threats.** *Saudi Gazette*, c. Sep–Oct. [link](https://saudigazette.com.sa/article/664975/saudi-arabia/saudi-arabia-rejects-weaponizing-vital-waterways-for-pressure-threats) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The Saudi Cabinet called for the strait to return to pre-28 February conditions, with no fees or restrictions. Reliability: Pro-government paper reporting the official position.
- **Asharq Al-Awsat (2026). Iran says Strait of Hormuz will remain closed until its conditions are met.** *Asharq Al-Awsat*, c. October. [link](https://english.aawsat.com/world/5320357-iran-says-strait-hormuz-will-remain-closed-until-its-conditions-are-met) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Reports Iran's conditions; related coverage says ships switching off tracking would be treated as "hostile" and Brent touched $110. Reliability: Saudi state-linked paper reporting Iranian statements.
- **Asharq Al-Awsat (2026). French FM: US's Hormuz coalition "not in competition" with France, UK-led bid.** *Asharq Al-Awsat*, c. Apr–May. [link](https://english.aawsat.com/world/5268451-french-fm-uss-hormuz-coalition-not-competition-france-uk-led-bid) — **Grade C2**. Summary: France called the US "Maritime Freedom Construct" a complement to the UK–France mission, not a rival. Reliability: Saudi state-linked paper quoting the French minister.
- **The Peninsula (2026). Qatar LNG capabilities could return to normal within couple of weeks of Hormuz reopening.** *The Peninsula* (Qatar), 20 September. [link](https://thepeninsulaqatar.com/article/20/09/2026/qatar-lng-capabilities-could-return-to-normal-within-couple-of-weeks-of-hormuz-reopening:-energy-minister) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Repairing two damaged LNG trains will take three years, but normal operations could resume within weeks of reopening; LNG cannot go by pipeline. ### World press

- **CNN en Español (2026). El petróleo se dispara por encima de los US$ 100 tras el cierre del estrecho de Ormuz.** CNN en Español, 12 March. [link](https://cnnespanol.cnn.com/2026/03/12/economia/petroleo-superar-ususd-100-trax) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Brent rose to around $100 after Iran said the strait stays shut; a 18 March companion piece has useful maps and charts. Reliability: Spanish-language arm of a major US broadcaster; the price is confirmed by other sources.
- **Ara (2026). España rechaza prestar ayuda a los EUA por la guerra contra Irán: no usará las bases de Rota y Morón.** *Ara*, March. [link](https://es.ara.cat/politica/espana-rechaza-prestar-ayuda-eua-guerra-iran-no-usara-bases-rota-moron_1_5665730.html) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Spain vetoed US use of Rota and Morón for strikes on Iran, saying bases serve only actions fitting the UN Charter; Trump threatened to suspend trade. Reliability: Catalan centre-left paper, confirmed by BBC Mundo.

## Sources: Suez Canal and Red Sea

283 sources on the 1967–75 closure, the 1974 clearance, the 2023–26 Houthi crisis, the naval missions and Somali piracy. State-run outlets such as Saba (Houthi) and Ahram (Egypt) are graded D on topics touching their own government: use them only to show how each side framed events.

### Peer-reviewed and academic

- **Treves, T. (2009). Piracy, law of the sea, and use of force: developments off the coast of Somalia.** *European Journal of International Law* 20(2). [link](https://doi.org/10.1093/ejil/chp027) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Explains how UN Resolution 1816 (2008) let navies enter Somali waters, and why navies were wary of detaining pirates, which led to transfer deals with Kenya. Reliability: A former law-of-the-sea judge in a top law journal, so very authoritative, but from 2009, missing later prosecutions.
- **Guilfoyle, D. (2008). Piracy off Somalia: UN Security Council Resolution 1816 and IMO regional counter-piracy efforts.** *International and Comparative Law Quarterly* 57(3). [link](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/international-and-comparative-law-quarterly/article/abs/ii-piracy-off-somalia-un-security-council-resolution-1816-and-imo-regional-counterpiracy-efforts/A7C877DE295732AD8D439457F9AA4978) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Covers Resolution 1816, the IMO-led regional cooperation that became the Djibouti Code of Conduct, and the rule that navies needed the Somali government's consent to enter its waters. Reliability: A UCL international lawyer in a leading journal, but written in 2008 before outcomes were known; DOI not confirmed.
- **Yap, W.Y. and Yang, D. (2024). Geopolitical tension and shipping network disruption: analysis of the Red Sea crisis on container port calls.** *Journal of Transport Geography* 121. [link](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jtrangeo.2024.104004) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Carriers switched to the Cape and added ships to keep port calls steady; daily Suez transits fell from about 75 to about 30 (December 2023–December 2024). Reliability: PolyU researchers in a strong journal; the 75→30 figure is snippet-only and covers year one.
- **Bedoya-Maya, F. et al. (2025). Container port competitiveness amid disruptions: insights from the European maritime network during the Red Sea crisis.** *Journal of Transport Geography* 128. [link](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jtrangeo.2025.104304) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Transport-chain costs to Europe rose 3–13% depending on the port; hubs near Gibraltar coped best, while feeder ports near Suez lost connections. Reliability: A strong University of Antwerp port-economics team with a free PDF and a confirmed abstract, but it covers only the first half of 2024.
- **[Authors to check] (2026). Mediterranean container port reconfiguration under geopolitical disruption: an AIS-based multi-scale analysis.** *Maritime Economics & Logistics*. [link](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1057/s41278-026-00356-5) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Ship-tracking data over 240 days show capacity, especially the biggest ships, shifting to western Mediterranean ports, so the crisis moved trade around as well as cutting it. Reliability: The leading journal in its field, but the authors are unconfirmed and only a snippet was seen.
- **Bensassi, S. and Martínez-Zarzoso, I. (2012). How costly is modern maritime piracy to the international community?** *Review of International Economics* 20(5). [link](https://doi.org/10.1111/roie.12000) — **Grade A2**. Summary: A gravity model of Europe–Asia trade (1999–2008) finds ten extra hijackings go with about an 11% fall in exports, costing about US$24.5 billion a year. Reliability: Peer-reviewed work by trade economists (one Spanish), but the data stop in 2008 and the cost figure conflicts with another estimate of US$18 billion.
- **Shortland, A. and Vothknecht, M. (2011). Combating "maritime terrorism" off the coast of Somalia.** *European Journal of Political Economy* 27(S1). [link](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ejpoleco.2011.03.004) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Finds the 2008–10 Gulf of Aden naval mission, including EU Atalanta, was only a partial success: attacks stayed high and moved out of the guarded corridor into the open ocean. Reliability: DIW Berlin and King's College economists in a peer-reviewed journal, but written before piracy collapsed in 2012.
- **Percy, S. and Shortland, A. (2013). The business of piracy in Somalia.** *Journal of Strategic Studies* 36(4). [link](https://doi.org/10.1080/01402390.2012.750242) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Argues Somali piracy is a business run from land that even gains from local stability, so naval patrols alone cannot end it. Reliability: Oxford and King's College academics in a leading strategic-studies journal, though the Somali case may not transfer to state threats.
- **Bueger, C. (2013). Practice, pirates and coast guards: the grand narrative of Somali piracy.** *Third World Quarterly* 34(10). [link](https://doi.org/10.1080/01436597.2013.851896) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Uses pirates' own interviews to show how they justify piracy as "coast guarding" against foreign fishing and dumping, which helps explain why it lasted. Reliability: A well-known maritime-security professor in a peer-reviewed journal, but the study is interpretive and covers Somalia only.
- **Kontorovich, E. and Art, S. (2010). An empirical examination of universal jurisdiction for piracy.** *American Journal of International Law* 104. [link](https://scholarlycommons.law.northwestern.edu/facultyworkingpapers/38) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Using piracy data for 1998–2009, finds that at most 1.47% of clearly punishable piracies were ever prosecuted internationally, so law on paper rarely meant enforcement. Reliability: A Northwestern law professor in a top journal, but pages and DOI are unconfirmed and the data end in 2009.
- **Sun, R., Abouarghoub, W., Demir, E. and Potter, A. (2025/26). Geopolitical disruptions and maritime transitions: environmental and economic costs of rerouting.** *Transportation Research Part A*. [link](https://orca.cardiff.ac.uk/181932) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Cape detours on three Eurasian routes raise emissions by at least 46% and fleet costs by at least 51%, and add 20–34 days per round trip. Reliability: Cardiff Business School authors in a strong journal, but volume and DOI are unconfirmed and results are modelled.
- **Yang, D., Yue, X. and Yap, W.Y. (2025). Geospatial resilience of shipping alliances: navigating the Red Sea crisis.** *Journal of Transport Geography* 126. [link](https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/jotrge/v126y2025ics0966692325001450.html) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Shipping alliances skipped more ports, especially Red Sea ones, to keep their schedules reliable; no headline figure was seen. Reliability: PolyU researchers in a strong journal, confirmed in search, but its focus on carrier scheduling is narrow.
- **Arellanes Arellanes, J. (2025). Los hutíes en el entramado geopolítico de Medio Oriente: la Crisis del Mar Rojo como disrupción de la seguridad marítima internacional.** *Revista Mexicana de Ciencias Políticas y Sociales* 70(255). [link](https://www.revistas.unam.mx/index.php/rmcpys/article/view/93957) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Treats the Houthis as a "hybrid actor", not a simple Iranian proxy, using over 18,000 ACLED events (2017–2025) to show how cheap weapons and Bab el-Mandeb let them disrupt shipping. Reliability: A peer-reviewed UNAM journal; seen in search summaries only.
- **Rai, A. (2025). Turbulent waters in the Red Sea: understanding maritime shipping business impacts and implications.** *Journal of Transportation Security* 18(1). [link](https://doi.org/10.1007/s12198-025-00299-7) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Reviews the business effects of the attacks: rerouting, longer voyages, more fuel and higher war-risk premiums; snippets mention over 80% of container ships diverting. Reliability: A peer-reviewed review by an author whose affiliation is unconfirmed; it adds no new data and the figures are not clearly from this paper.
- **[Authors to check] (2024). The costs of maritime supply chain disruptions: the case of the Suez Canal blockage by the "Ever Given" megaship.** *International Journal of Production Economics*. [link](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0925527324003219) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Measures the supply-chain costs of the six-day accidental Suez blockage in 2021, a useful control case with no hostile actor. Reliability: A top operations journal, but the authors are unknown, only a snippet was seen, and a six-day event is a poor match in scale.
- **[Author to check] (2009). Coalition strategy and the pirates of the Gulf of Aden and the Red Sea.** *Comparative Strategy*. [link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/01495930903025250) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Analyses how naval coalitions were organised against Somali piracy at Bab el-Mandeb, an early comparison for Prosperity Guardian and Aspides; no figure seen. Reliability: A US defence-oriented peer-reviewed journal; the author is unknown and it was written before outcomes were known.
- **[Author to check] (2015). Learning from piracy: future challenges of maritime security governance.** *Global Affairs*. [link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/23340460.2015.960170) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Draws lessons from counter-piracy off Somalia, praising loose coordination such as the Contact Group on Piracy, built on memoranda rather than treaties. Reliability: A peer-reviewed journal, but the author is unconfirmed (possibly Bueger) and the content comes from snippets.
- **Germond, B. and Smith, M.E. (2009). Re-thinking European security interests and the ESDP: explaining the EU's anti-piracy operation.** *Contemporary Security Policy* 30(3). [link](https://eprints.lancs.ac.uk/id/eprint/40789/) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Explains why the EU launched Operation Atalanta off Somalia in 2008; no figure seen. Reliability: Lancaster and Hawaii scholars in a peer-reviewed journal, but only the repository record was seen and the compiler said it was not checked enough to cite.
- **[Author to check] (n.d.). US and UK military strikes in Yemen and the jus ad bellum.** *International and Comparative Law Quarterly*. [link](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/international-and-comparative-law-quarterly/article/us-and-uk-military-strikes-in-yemen-and-the-jus-ad-bellum/720306CC8E7C900346AC9A9AA3A39EF1) — **Grade A6**. Summary: Assesses whether the 2024 US–UK strikes on Yemen were lawful uses of force; no figure seen. Reliability: A high-prestige law journal that would balance the US view, but only the title was seen, so its content cannot be judged.
- **AJIL editors (2024). The United States and partners use force against the Houthis to protect freedom of navigation in the Red Sea and the Gulf of Aden.** *American Journal of International Law* 118(2). [link](https://doi.org/10.1017/ajil.2024.19) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Records the January 2024 US–UK strikes on the Houthis and the legal reasons given: Article 51 self-defence and freedom of navigation. Reliability: Written by the leading US law journal's editors, not blind-refereed; it records the US position rather than judging it.
- **CEPII (2016). Trade costs and the Suez and Panama Canals, Working Paper 2016-29.** CEPII. [link](https://www.cepii.fr/PDF_PUB/wp/2016/wp2016-29.pdf) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Estimates a much smaller effect of distance on trade (elasticity about −0.15) than Feyrer, so the size of the 1967–75 trade shock is contested. Reliability: A French government-funded economics centre; a working paper, not peer-reviewed, so check for a later journal version.
- **Wang, Z., Fang, Z., Yu, J., Hu, X. and Gong, J. (2024). Red Sea crisis impacts on maritime shipping networks.** *Heliyon* 10(22). [link](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.heliyon.2024.e40384) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Ship-tracking data show fewer and slower ships after the crisis began, with many sailing past the Red Sea; the network changed shape but stayed resilient. Reliability: Chinese authors in a high-volume open-access journal with lighter review; the DOI was seen only once.
- **Rodríguez-Díaz, E., Alcaide, J.I. and García-Llave, R. (2024). Challenges and security risks in the Red Sea: impact of Houthi attacks on maritime traffic.** *Journal of Marine Science and Engineering* 12(11). [link](https://doi.org/10.3390/jmse12111900) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Traffic data (19 November 2023–5 February 2024) show sharp falls in the Gulf of Aden and Suez and higher freight rates; the authors urge more cooperation. Reliability: University of Cádiz authors in a fast-review MDPI journal, arguing for cooperation, the very idea being tested.
- **Dunley, R. (2024). The end of the age of transoceanic navies?** *RUSI Journal* 169(4). [link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/03071847.2024.2391354) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Uses the Red Sea to ask whether navies can still protect shipping far from home against cheap drones and missiles; warships coped tactically, but cost and endurance are the problem. Reliability: A UNSW naval historian in a UK defence think-tank journal; the abstract is more pessimistic than the compiler's summary, so read it first.
- **[Authors to check] (2025). China's Red Sea gambit: a paradigm shift of NSAGs and the maritime strategic dilemma in the Middle East.** *Cogent Arts & Humanities*. [link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/23311983.2025.2495475) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Covers China's position in the crisis and the rise of armed non-state groups like the Houthis as sea powers; no figure seen. Reliability: A pay-to-publish mega-journal outside its field, with unknown authors, so use it only as support.
- **Ferhan, O. (2025). Environmental impacts of the Houthis' attacks against commercial shipping in the Red Sea.** *Frontiers in Political Science* 7. [link](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/political-science/articles/10.3389/fpos.2025.1711643/full) — **Grade B4**. Summary: Finds rerouting around Africa raised shipping emissions by about 30–35%. Reliability: A high-volume open-access journal using secondary research; the compiler's figures (40% Suez income fall, Aspides escorts) do not match the abstract, so trace them to official sources.
- **[Authors to check] (n.d.). Growth volatility and trade: evidence from the 1967–1975 closure of the Suez Canal, MPRA Paper 39040.** RePEc/MPRA. [link](https://ideas.repec.org/p/pra/mprapa/39040.html) — **Grade C3**. Summary: One of the few studies of how the eight-year closure affected countries' economic growth and its ups and downs; no figure seen. Reliability: An unrefereed archive where anyone can upload, with unknown authors, so treat it as a lead only.
- **[Author to check] (2025). [Article on the Houthi attacks and commercial and environmental maritime safety].** *DEHUKAM Journal of the Sea and Maritime Law*. [link](https://dergipark.org.tr/en/pub/dehukamder/article/1678223) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Looks at the Houthi attacks from the angle of commercial and environmental safety at sea and argues for a coordinated international response. Reliability: A small peer-reviewed Turkish journal; the article and its unusual DOI could not be confirmed.
- **[Author to check] (2024). The Red Sea crisis: implications of the Houthi attack on maritime trade and global security.** *IJHESS* 4(1). [link](https://ijhess.com/index.php/ijhess/article/view/1135) — **Grade D3**. Summary: A general overview of how the attacks affected trade and security; no new figure. Reliability: A little-known open-access journal whose peer review cannot be relied on, possibly predatory, so leave it out.
- **Li, X. and Xu, [first name to check] (2026). Assessing the port resilience under the Red Sea crisis.** *Journal of Transport Geography* 130. [link](https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/jotrge/v130y2026ics0966692325003655.html) — **Grade F6**. Summary: Said to measure how well ports withstood and recovered from the Red Sea disruption; the abstract was not seen. Reliability: An exact-title search found no such paper and the DOI was seen once, so drop it unless confirmed.
- **[Author to check] (n.d.). La crisis actual en el Mar Rojo y sus implicancias en el comercio marítimo internacional.** *Revista AMELICA*. [link](https://portal.amelica.org/ameli/journal/615/6154859006/html/) — **Grade F6**. Summary: A Latin American open-access article on the Red Sea crisis and world shipping; no figure seen. Reliability: Author, date and review status are all unknown, so its content cannot be judged.

### Reports, think tanks and official documents

- **IMF (2024). Red Sea attacks disrupt global trade.** *IMF Blog* (Chart of the Week), 7 March. [link](https://www.imf.org/Blogs/Articles/2024/03/07/Red-Sea-Attacks-Disrupt-Global-Trade) — **Grade A1**. Summary: PortWatch data show Suez trade volume down about 50% year on year in January–February 2024 and Cape traffic up about 74%, adding 10+ days to deliveries. Reliability: IMF staff using original data, confirmed by UNCTAD; a short blog covering two months only, and the authors still need adding.
- **ECB (2024). Staff macroeconomic projections, March 2024 (Box 3) and Economic Bulletin 2/2024.** European Central Bank. [link](https://www.ecb.europa.eu/press/projections/html/ecb.projections202403_ecbstaff~f2f2d34d5a.en.html) — **Grade A2**. Summary: In an escalation scenario, euro-area inflation would be only about 0.25 points higher in 2024–25, because shipping is a small share of costs. Reliability: Official central-bank analysis, but a model scenario for the euro area only, and the ECB has a reason to call shocks "contained".
- **International Transport Forum (OECD) (2024). The Red Sea Crisis: Impacts on Global Shipping and the Case for International Co-operation.** ITF-OECD. [link](https://www.itf-oecd.org/sites/default/files/repositories/red-sea-crisis-impacts-global-shipping.pdf) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Rerouting a median container ship via the Cape adds about US$272 per 40-ft container into Europe, against up to US$160 via Suez. Reliability: An intergovernmental OECD body, but it argues one side by design, as its subtitle makes "the case for" cooperation.
- **World Bank (2025). The Deepening Red Sea Shipping Crisis.** World Bank. [link](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099253002102539789/pdf/IDU10b8b59671dbc814cfc19c4a1299ff54854ba.pdf) — **Grade A2**. Summary: If the crisis ended by May 2025 rather than October 2025, shipping trade would be about 6% higher in Red Sea neighbours and about 5% higher in the EU. Reliability: World Bank staff brief, not peer-reviewed, and these are scenarios rather than outcomes.
- **CSIS (2024). The Global Economic Consequences of the Attacks on Red Sea Shipping Lanes.** Center for Strategic and International Studies. [link](https://www.csis.org/analysis/global-economic-consequences-attacks-red-sea-shipping-lanes) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Counts about 40 Houthi attacks by early 2024 and puts the Cape detour at up to an extra $1 million per trip; US–UK strikes hit about 60 targets. Reliability: A major US think tank partly funded by the US government, defence firms and Gulf states; an early snapshot later overtaken.
- **CSIS (2026). The Houthis Enter the Iran War and Everything Gets Worse.** Center for Strategic and International Studies. [link](https://www.csis.org/analysis/houthis-enter-iran-war-and-everything-gets-worse) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Argues a Houthi attack at Bab el-Mandeb would wreck Saudi Arabia's Hormuz workaround, because the East–West pipeline to Yanbu is near capacity. Reliability: A major US think tank with Gulf donors, arguing from scenarios; trade-press reports back up its pipeline point.
- **ECFR (2026). The strait and narrow: Yemen, the Red Sea and Europe's path forward.** European Council on Foreign Relations. [link](https://ecfr.eu/article/the-strait-and-narrow-yemen-the-red-sea-and-europes-path-forward/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Says Europe should add ships, minesweeping and counter-drone cover to Aspides and Atalanta, which related ECFR work calls "fragmented and under-resourced". Reliability: A pro-EU think tank funded by foundations and governments; it is advocacy, so it has a reason to stress under-resourcing.
- **Arteaga, F., Escribano, G. and Urbasos, I. (2024). Crisis del mar Rojo: inseguridad marítima y energética, ARI 77/2024.** Real Instituto Elcano. [link](https://www.realinstitutoelcano.org/analisis/crisis-del-mar-rojo-inseguridad-maritima-y-energetica/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Finds the Houthi threat squeezed shipping logistics but had a limited effect on oil and gas markets; about 70% of tankers using the Red Sea diverted to the Cape. Reliability: Spain's leading foreign-policy think tank, part-funded by government and energy firms such as Repsol; the tanker share is unverified.
- **ACLED (2025). Regional power struggles fuel simmering tensions across Red Sea.** ACLED. [link](https://acleddata.com/report/regional-power-struggles-fuel-simmering-tensions-across-red-sea) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The year-end review found 84% fewer Houthi attacks on Red Sea shipping in 2025 than in 2024. Reliability: A widely used conflict-event dataset with a transparent method, partly government-funded; coding rules shape the counts and it was not cross-checked.
- **Kiel Institute (2024). Kiel Trade Indicator releases, December 2023–February 2024.** Kiel Institute for the World Economy. [link](https://www.kielinstitut.de/media/news/cargo-volume-in-the-red-sea-collapses/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: World trade fell 1.3% from November to December 2023 and Red Sea container volume was about 70% below expected; by February 2024 Kiel expected no bigger global damage. Reliability: A top German economic institute's own nowcast data, which may be revised later; press-release format.
- **VoxEU/CEPR (2024). Sailing through storms: the fallout of Red Sea disruptions for global trade and inflation.** *VoxEU* column. [link](https://cepr.org/voxeu/columns/sailing-through-storms-fallout-red-sea-disruptions-global-trade-and-inflation) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Effects on trade and inflation were muted overall, but in a long disruption euro-area export growth would be 1.6 points lower in 2024. Reliability: A research-based economists' column, not refereed, and the authors still need naming.
- **Carson, J., Rondos, A., Stigant, S. and Woldemariam, M. (2024). The Red Sea Crisis Goes Beyond the Houthis.** *Foreign Affairs*, 19 July. [link](https://www.foreignaffairs.com/somalia/red-sea-crisis-goes-beyond-houthis) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Argues instability across the Horn of Africa (Sudan, Somalia, Ethiopia–Eritrea) also threatens Red Sea shipping and calls for US-led diplomacy, not just naval force. Reliability: Former US and EU officials and academics in a top journal; it advocates a policy, and credentials are from background knowledge.
- **Escribano, G. and Urbasos, I. (2024). [Article on Red Sea instability and energy-transition supply chains].** *Política Exterior*, April. [link](https://www.politicaexterior.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/04/16Escribano.pdf) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Red Sea instability hits not only oil and gas but also supply chains for clean-energy goods such as solar panels and batteries from Asia; no figure seen. Reliability: Elcano experts in an independent Madrid policy magazine; the exact title was not shown.
- **Romero Junquera, A. (2025). La crisis del mar Rojo. Análisis desde una dimensión marítima, Documento de Análisis 16/2025.** IEEE (Spanish Ministry of Defence). [link](https://www.defensa.gob.es/documents/2073105/2392118/la_crisis_del_mar_rojo_2025_dieeea16.pdf/d1fb085c-1426-775e-76d9-c5f0ca9e51a2?t=1740480237934) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A naval view of the crisis covering attacks, rerouting and the naval missions, with information up to 30 September 2024. Reliability: Written by a naval officer for a defence-ministry institute, so it speaks from inside government; seen as a summary only.
- **Romero Junquera, A. and Castro Torres, J.I. (2024). ¿Qué está pasando en el Mar Rojo? Causas y consecuencias de esta nueva crisis, Documento Informativo 01/2024.** IEEE. [link](https://www.ieee.es/Galerias/fichero/docs_informativos/2024/DIEEEI01_2024_ABEROM_MarRojo.pdf) — **Grade B2**. Summary: An early-2024 explainer on the causes of the attacks, the rerouting and the international response; no figure seen. Reliability: A Spanish defence-ministry briefing, quick and official in tone; snippet only.
- **Aguilera Raga, A. (2020). [On the Bab el-Mandeb strait], Documento de Opinión 19/2020.** IEEE. [link](https://www.ieee.es/Galerias/fichero/docs_opinion/2020/DIEEEO19_2020ANAAGU_BabMandeb.pdf) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A pre-crisis look at Bab el-Mandeb's strategic weight, useful as a "before" baseline; no strait-specific figure seen. Reliability: An opinion paper from a Spanish defence-ministry institute; the exact title was not shown.
- **US Naval Institute (2024). Lessons from the Red Sea: Considerations for Naval Strategy in the 21st Century.** *Proceedings*, May. [link](https://www.usni.org/magazines/proceedings/2024/may/lessons-red-sea-considerations-naval-strategy-21st-century) — **Grade B2** (graded here). Summary: Notes that Aspides stayed purely defensive under different rules from the US mission, and that Bab el-Mandeb traffic fell about 50% between November 2023 and February 2024. Reliability: A US naval professional journal with editorial review and a pro-navy audience; seen in a snippet, but the traffic fall matches IMF data.
- **Atlantic Council and Energy Intelligence (2024). Will US-UK strikes against the Houthis halt their Red Sea aggression?** Atlantic Council / *Energy Intelligence*. [link](https://www.atlanticcouncil.org/content-series/fastthinking/will-us-uk-strikes-against-the-houthis-halt-their-red-sea-aggression/) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Experts said the strikes were "unlikely to immediately halt" attacks, while Energy Intelligence warned the US risked being "trapped" in escalation. Reliability: A pro-NATO US think tank part-funded by governments and defence firms, plus oil-industry press; quick forecasts made before outcomes were known.
- **ELIAMEP (2024). [Insight on the Houthi attacks and the law of armed conflict].** ELIAMEP (Greece). [link](https://www.eliamep.gr/en/?p=29466) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Classifies the situation as a non-international armed conflict between states and a non-state group, the Houthis; no figure. Reliability: A Greek foreign-policy think tank (Greece commands Aspides, so a possible national interest); author not named.
- **Torregrosa Ramos, N. (2024). Ansar Allah, un movimiento insurgente en fase de ascenso, Documento de Análisis 27/2024.** IEEE. [link](https://www.ieee.es/Galerias/fichero/docs_analisis/2024/DIEEEA27_2024_NATTOR_Movimiento.pdf) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Profiles the Houthis and suggests Aspides and Prosperity Guardian could restore stability, a prediction later events tested. Reliability: A Spanish defence-ministry analysis seen only in summary; useful as an early forecast, not as evidence.
- **Cánovas Sánchez, B. (2024). Incertidumbre en la navegación por el Mar Rojo.** *Boletín IEEE* 34. [link](https://dialnet.unirioja.es/servlet/articulo?codigo=9674053) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Discusses the uncertainty the Houthi attacks created for Red Sea navigation, freight and insurance; no figure seen. Reliability: An article in the defence institute's bulletin indexed in Dialnet; content seen as a summary only.
- **IEEE (2011). El tratamiento jurídico de la piratería marítima en el ordenamiento español, Documento Marco 02/2011.** IEEE. [link](https://www.ieee.es/Galerias/fichero/docs_marco/2011/DIEEEM02-2011TratamientoJuridicoPirateria.pdf) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Explains how Spanish law treats piracy, which matters for prosecuting pirates caught by Atalanta off Somalia; no figure. Reliability: A Spanish defence-ministry legal document with no named author; pre-dates later reforms.
- **SPRI (2024). Situación en el mar Rojo y perspectivas.** Basque Government business agency, 7 February. [link](https://www.spri.eus/archivos/2024/02/pdf/situacion-en-el-mar-rojo-y-perspectivas_2024-02-07-3.pdf) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Early-2024 guidance telling Basque exporters how to cope with Red Sea disruption; no figure seen. Reliability: A primary regional-government briefing that shows how Spanish firms were advised, not an independent analysis.
- **Foreign Affairs (n.d.). Don't Bomb the Houthis.** *Foreign Affairs*. [link](https://foreignaffairs.com/yemen/dont-bomb-houthis) — **Grade B6** (graded here). Summary: An essay arguing against air strikes on the Houthis; author and figures not seen. Reliability: A respected policy journal, but only the URL and title were seen, so the argument cannot be judged.
- **Foreign Affairs (n.d.). How Washington Emboldened the Houthis.** *Foreign Affairs*. [link](https://www.foreignaffairs.com/middle-east/how-washington-emboldened-houthis) — **Grade B6** (graded here). Summary: An essay blaming US policy for strengthening the Houthis; author and figures not seen. Reliability: A respected policy journal, but only the title was seen, so its content cannot be judged.
- **Tallis, J. (2025). How the Biden Administration Won Tactically but Failed Strategically in the Red Sea.** *War on the Rocks*, 2 April. [link](https://warontherocks.com/2025/04/how-the-biden-administration-won-tactically-but-failed-strategically-in-the-red-sea/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The US Navy won "nearly flawless" fights against Houthi missiles and drones, but Prosperity Guardian failed to bring commercial traffic back, as few partners joined and carriers kept using the Cape. Reliability: A US naval analyst writing to persuade policy readers; an argument, not data.
- **Washington Institute for Near East Policy (2025). Houthi Shipping Attacks: Patterns and Expectations for 2025.** WINEP. [link](https://washingtoninstitute.org/policy-analysis/houthi-shipping-attacks-patterns-and-expectations-2025) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Charts the pattern of attacks, including the 19 January 2025 pause on non-Israeli ships and the later restart. Reliability: A US think tank founded with AIPAC backing and close to Israeli and US security views, with undisclosed donors; check its counts against ACLED.
- **Human Rights Watch (2025). Yemen: Houthis' attacks on cargo ships apparent war crimes.** HRW, 23 July. [link](https://www.hrw.org/news/2025/07/23/yemen-houthis-attacks-on-cargo-ships-apparent-war-crimes) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Found no evidence that the *Magic Seas* or *Eternity C* were military targets and said the Houthis did not meet the rules for a lawful blockade. Reliability: A campaigning human-rights NGO; its findings match news reports of the July 2025 sinkings.
- **The Conversation (2026). The Mecca Alliance Meets the Red Sea: Saudi Arabia's New Defense Pact Faces Its First Major Test.** *The Conversation*. [link](https://theconversation.com/the-mecca-alliance-meets-the-red-sea-saudi-arabias-new-defense-pact-faces-its-first-major-test-292975) — **Grade C2**. Summary: A Gulf-led coalition could protect the waters Saudi Arabia depends on, but the new "Mecca" pact has no joint command, which limits it against the Houthi blockade. Reliability: Academic commentary for general readers by an unnamed author; consistent with EFE reports on the Saudi alliance.
- **Lieber Institute, West Point (2024). Houthi operations in the Red Sea and LOAC?** Lieber Institute blog. [link](https://lieber.westpoint.edu/?p=24676) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Asks whether and how the law of armed conflict applies to the Houthi attacks; no figure. Reliability: A US military-academy law blog with an unnamed author; it raises questions rather than giving firm answers.
- **CIMSEC (2024–25). The Crucial Role of Public/Private Partnerships in the Red Sea Crisis.** CIMSEC. [link](https://cimsec.org/the-crucial-role-of-public-private-partnerships-in-the-red-sea-crisis/) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Argues that sharing information and routing advice between navies, shipping firms and insurers matters as much as warships; no figure seen. Reliability: A pro-navy online forum; the author and date were not shown.
- **Revista General de Marina (2024). [Article on Djibouti and Atalanta], March 2024, part 3.** Spanish Navy. [link](https://armada.defensa.gob.es/archivo/rgm/2024/03/rgmmar2024_Parte03.pdf) — **Grade C6**. Summary: Probably describes Spain's Atalanta deployment, which uses a patrol aircraft based in Djibouti; no figure seen. Reliability: The Spanish Navy's own journal, not peer-reviewed, and the title and author are unverified, so open the PDF first.
- **War on the Rocks (n.d.). Houthi Attacks in the Red Sea: A New Twist on the Jeune École?** *War on the Rocks*. [link](https://warontherocks.com/houthi-attacks-in-the-red-sea-a-new-twist-on-the-jeune-ecole/) — **Grade C6** (graded here). Summary: Compares Houthi tactics to the 19th-century "Jeune École" idea of weak navies using cheap weapons against strong ones; no figure seen. Reliability: An expert defence blog, but only the title was seen, so the argument cannot be judged.

### Primary sources

- **UN Security Council (2024). Resolution 2722 (2024), S/RES/2722.** United Nations, 10 January. [link](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4033392/files/S_RES_2722_%282024%29-EN.pdf) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Condemns at least two dozen Houthi attacks since 19 November 2023 and backs states' right to defend ships; it passed 11–0 with four abstentions (Algeria, China, Mozambique, Russia). Reliability: The original UN text, but its wording reflects great-power bargaining, it legitimises the sponsors' own response and has no enforcement power.
- **Council of the EU (2024–26). Decisions (CFSP) 2024/583, 2024/632, 2025/334 and 2026/439 on EUNAVFOR ASPIDES.** *EUR-Lex*. [link](https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/dec/2024/583/oj/eng) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The legal mandate for a defensive-only EU mission, extended in 2026 to 28 February 2027 with about €15 million in common costs (down from €17 million). Reliability: Original EU legal acts; they show what Aspides may do, not whether it worked, and the budget figure needs checking in the text.
- **Council of the EU (2024). Decision (CFSP) 2024/3186 amending Decision 2008/851/CFSP (Operation ATALANTA).** *EUR-Lex*, 16 December. [link](https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/dec/2024/3186/oj) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Extends the Spanish-led, Rota-based Atalanta to 28 February 2027 and widens its area to parts of the Red Sea, with closer links to Aspides. Reliability: An original EU legal text; it shows the mandate only, not results.
- **Lloyd's Market Association Joint War Committee (2026). JWLA-034 (Saudi Arabia).** LMA, 29 July. [link](https://lmalloyds.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/06/JWLA-034-Saudi-Arabia.pdf) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Extends the Red Sea high-risk listed area northwards along Saudi Arabia's coast; Reuters reports Jeddah and Yanbu premiums then rose from 0.25% to 1% of hull value. Reliability: An original insurance circular from underwriters with a commercial interest; the URL folder (2025/06) does not match the date, so check the PDF.
- **Ministerio de Asuntos Exteriores (2024). Condena de los ataques hutíes en el Mar Rojo.** Spanish Foreign Ministry press release, 6 February. [link](https://www.exteriores.gob.es/es/Comunicacion/Comunicados/Paginas/2024_COMUNICADOS/20240206_COMU004.aspx) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Spain condemns the Houthi attacks and backs international efforts for freedom of navigation, without sending ships to the US coalition.
