Summary: Looks at the multilateral arrangements for the straits, probably including the Cooperative Mechanism between coastal and user states. Reliability: Peer-reviewed journal, but only the title was seen and the content is a guess, so read the abstract first.
- **[Authors and year to check]. How did maritime piracy affect trade in Southeast Asia?** *Journal of East Asian Studies* (Cambridge). [link](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-east-asian-studies/article/how-did-maritime-piracy-affect-trade-in-southeast-asia/E45B5C7651110CC17D89B54316955C34) — **Grade A6**. Summary: Measures whether piracy actually cut trade in Southeast Asia, which shows whether Malacca piracy was a serious disruption or mainly a nuisance. Reliability: Respected Cambridge journal, but only the title was seen, so the author, year and findings are unknown.
- **[Author to check] (2018). Pirates of the South China Seas.** *Asian Affairs*, 49(1). [link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/03068374.2018.1416010) — **Grade A6**. Summary: Background history of piracy in the South China Sea and Malacca region, linking the 2000s peak to recent patterns. Reliability: Journal of the Royal Society for Asian Affairs; author unknown, paywalled, and the summary is general (title-level only).
- **Galgano, F.A. (2023). Hostis humani generis: pirates and global maritime commerce.** *Research in Globalization*, 8. [link](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2590051X23000783) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Using IMB reports, estimates a fall of nearly 75% in pirate activity in the Malacca and Singapore Straits and nearby Indonesian waters, credited to joint patrols and information sharing. Reliability: A Villanova geographer in a mid-tier open-access journal; other studies (Marine Policy 2026, Heliyon 2023) challenge its credit to cooperation.
- **[Authors to check] (2023). Assessment of global shipping risk caused by maritime piracy.** *Heliyon*. [link](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2405844023081963) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Maps piracy risk worldwide; one snippet says that after 2007 Malacca anti-piracy measures worked but pushed piracy elsewhere in Southeast Asia (moved, not removed). Reliability: Mega-journal with lighter review; authors unknown and the displacement point rests on one snippet.
- **Fernández, M. (2017). Piratería marítima en el Estrecho de Malaca, Somalia y Nigeria: causas, consecuencias y respuestas.** Thesis, Universidad Complutense de Madrid. [link](https://eprints.ucm.es/id/eprint/45559/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Spanish-language thesis comparing piracy off Indonesia/Malacca, Somalia and Nigeria, useful for setting Malacca's regional model against Somalia's naval one. Reliability: University thesis (level not stated), seen only as a repository record.
- **[Author and year to check]. Security in the Straits of Malacca.** *The Asia-Pacific Journal: Japan Focus*. [link](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/asia-pacific-journal/article/security-in-the-straits-of-malacca/43AB4ED844F0214995F93C67B5FEF272) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Credits both coordinated patrols and better rule of law on land in coastal areas for the fall in piracy. Reliability: Cambridge-hosted journal mixing refereed and editor-reviewed pieces; author and year unknown and it may be old.
- **García Sánchez, P. (2017). La piratería en el estrecho de Malaca.** Grupo de Estudios de Historia Militar (GEHM). [link](https://gehm.es/biblio/La_pirateria_en_el_estrecho_de_Malaca_Pablo_Garcia_gehm.pdf) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Short Spanish study of the strait's geography and piracy history; it gives the strait as about 800 km long and 2.8 km wide at its narrowest. Reliability: Written by a military-history study group and not peer-reviewed, so use it for background only.

### Reports, data and official documents

- **ReCAAP ISC (2026). Piracy and Armed Robbery against Ships in Asia: Annual Report 2025 (and press release).** ReCAAP Information Sharing Centre. [link](https://recaap.org/resources/ck/files/reports/annual/ReCAAP%20ISC%20Annual%20Report%202025.pdf) — **Grade A1**. Summary: 132 incidents in Asia in 2025 (+23%). The straits had 108, the most since 2007 (+74%), but 107 were in the Singapore Strait, none were in the worst category, and cases fell after Indonesian arrests. Reliability: Original data from the 21-state body, which wants to show its framework works.
- **ReCAAP ISC (2025). Incident Alert 01/2025: sea robbery incidents in the Singapore Strait.** ReCAAP ISC. [link](https://www.recaap.org/resources/ck/files/alerts/2025/ReCAAP%20ISC%20IA%2001-2025%20Sea%20robbery%20Incidents%20in%20the%20Singapore%20Strait%20(3%20Feb%202025).pdf) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Warned on 3 February 2025 that robberies in the eastbound lane had nearly tripled, with 11 incidents since 1 January. Reliability: Primary warning to shipping that shows how fast the information-sharing system works, though it covers only five weeks.
- **ReCAAP member states (2004). Regional Cooperation Agreement on Combating Piracy and Armed Robbery against Ships in Asia.** NUS Centre for International Law treaty database. [link](https://cil.nus.edu.sg/databasecil/2004-regional-cooperation-agreement-on-combating-piracy-and-armed-robbery-against-ships-in-asia-3/) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Asia's first government-to-government anti-piracy treaty (signed November 2004, in force 4 September 2006), which set up the Information Sharing Centre. Reliability: Primary legal text, but its main limit is that Malaysia and Indonesia, the two biggest coastal states, are not parties.
- **ICC International Maritime Bureau (2001). Piracy attacks rise to alarming new levels, ICC report reveals.** ICC press release. [link](https://iccwbo.org/news-publications/news/piracy-attacks-rise-to-alarming-new-levels-icc-report-reveals/) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Announced the 2000 world record: 469 attacks (+57%) and 72 seafarers killed; the Malacca Strait had 75 attacks, up from 2 in 1999. Reliability: Original data from a shipowner-backed watchdog that wants the threat noticed; IMB and ReCAAP count differently, so don't mix them.
- **ICC International Maritime Bureau (2026). Piracy and Armed Robbery Against Ships: Report for 2025.** ICC-IMB. [link](https://iccwbo.org/news-publications/report/global-maritime-piracy-and-armed-robbery-increased-in-2025/) — **Grade A2**. Summary: 137 incidents worldwide in 2025. The Singapore Straits had 80 (58% of the world total), guns were reported 27 times, and cases fell after Indonesian police caught two gangs in July. Reliability: Industry-funded watchdog data, seen via a media release; its count (80) differs from ReCAAP's (108).
- **Ministry of Defence, Singapore (2005). Joint news release: launch of Eyes in the Sky (EiS) initiative.** National Archives of Singapore. [link](https://www.nas.gov.sg/archivesonline/data/pdfdoc/20050913984.htm) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Malaysia, Indonesia, Singapore and Thailand launched joint air patrols on 13 September 2005. Each state flies up to two patrols a week, with an officer from each country on board. Reliability: Official four-state release, solid on what governments did but silent on whether it worked.
- **Ministry of Defence, Singapore (2008). Thailand joins Malacca Straits Patrols.** National Archives of Singapore. [link](https://www.nas.gov.sg/archivesonline/data/pdfdoc/20080918989.htm) — **Grade B1**. Summary: On 18 September 2008 Thailand signed the patrols' revised rules and became the fourth full member, after being an observer from 2005. Reliability: Primary government release, reliable for dates but says nothing about effectiveness.
- **Ministry of Defence, Singapore (2016). Malacca Straits Patrol: Member States commemorate 10 years of cooperation.** MINDEF news release. [link](https://www.mindef.gov.sg/news-and-events/latest-releases/2016apr21-news-releases-01782/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Singapore credits the Malacca Straits Patrol with getting Lloyd's insurers to drop the strait's war-risk listing in August 2006. Reliability: A self-promoting government look back written 10 years later, so use it as the official claim, not proof of cause.
- **Ministry of Defence / Republic of Singapore Navy (2025). Reply to media query on increase in piracy in Singapore waters.** MINDEF. [link](https://www.mindef.gov.sg/news-and-events/latest-releases/13oct25-mq/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The navy said nothing happened in Singapore's own waters in 2025, and Singapore Strait robberies fell from 14 in July to 2 in August and 1 in September. Reliability: A government defending its record, and counting only its own territorial waters narrows the numbers.
- **RSIS (2005–2015). Commentaries CO05057 "Malacca Straits: a war risk?", Ho (2009) and CO15082 "Expanding maritime patrols in Southeast Asia".** RSIS, Nanyang Technological University. [link](https://rsis.edu.sg/rsis-publication/rsis/718-malacca-straits-a-war-risk/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Joshua Ho credits the Malacca Straits Patrols with cutting piracy but warns there is "no guarantee" it won't return; CO05057 dates from the 2005 war-risk listing. Reliability: Respected Singapore think tank close to the government, so it may over-credit the patrols.
- **RSIS / IDSS (2004). Operationalising the Regional Maritime Security Initiative.** IDSS Commentaries. [link](https://rsis.edu.sg/rsis-publication/idss/614-operationalising-the-regional/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Explains that the RMSI grew from US Admiral Fargo's March 2004 plan for special forces on fast boats, and that the region saw outside patrols as breaking transit rules. Reliability: Written at the time by a Singapore think tank; Singapore was most open to US help, so it may play down opposition.
- **Thư Nguyễn Hoàng Anh (2023). Sea robbery in the Singapore Strait.** *The Interpreter* (Lowy Institute). [link](https://www.lowyinstitute.org/the-interpreter/sea-robbery-singapore-strait) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Using an August 2023 ReCAAP report, describes rising petty theft from ships in the Singapore Strait, showing gaps in coastal-state enforcement despite patrols. Reliability: Blog of an Australian think tank by a Vietnamese researcher, written before the 2025 surge.
- **CIMSEC (c. 2014). It's the economy: exploring Indonesia's piracy problem.** Center for International Maritime Security. [link](https://cimsec.org/economy-stupid-exploring-indonesias-piracy-problem/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Argues piracy off Indonesia is mainly an economic problem force cannot fix: Indonesian incidents rose from 15 in 2009 to 106 in 2013 while joint patrols ran. Reliability: Naval-affairs forum, not peer-reviewed; the author and date were not seen and the figures are unchecked.
- **East Asia Forum (2024). Insurers and shippers combat piracy in Southeast Asia.** East Asia Forum (ANU). [link](https://eastasiaforum.org/2024/05/17/insurers-and-shippers-combat-piracy-in-southeast-asia/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Armed robbery around the straits rose after 2015 while ransom piracy fell from 2007 to 2022, and insurers and shipowners now increasingly lead the response. Reliability: University-hosted expert commentary rather than data; authors not recorded and only a snippet was seen.
- **Pacific Forum CSIS (2004). PacNet 29: U.S. RMSI contravenes UN Convention on the Law of the Sea.** Pacific Forum. [link](https://pacforum.org/publications/pacnet-29-u-s-rmsi-contravenes-un-convention-on-the-law-of-the-sea/) — **Grade C3**. Summary: A Malaysian-view argument that US patrols under the RMSI would be illegal without the coastal states' consent; it presents the June 2004 Indonesia–Malaysia patrol deal as a response. Reliability: Short opinion piece pushing a national legal case, useful for 2004 attitudes rather than facts.

### News and trade press

- **TradeWinds (2026). Piracy in key Malacca Strait waterway hits 19-year record.** TradeWinds. [link](https://www.tradewindsnews.com/casualties/piracy-in-key-malacca-strait-waterway-hits-19-year-record/2-1-1925306) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Reports ReCAAP's 2025 data: 108 incidents, a 19-year high, with bulk carriers the most common target. Reliability: Respected, paywalled shipping paper repeating ReCAAP; the headline says "Malacca" though nearly all cases were in the Singapore Strait.
- **TradeWinds (2026). Asia ship robberies sink to seven-year low after Singapore Strait turnaround.** TradeWinds. [link](https://www.tradewindsnews.com/casualties/asia-ship-robberies-sink-to-seven-year-low-after-singapore-strait-turnaround/2-1-2016417) — **Grade B1**. Summary: ReCAAP's first-half 2026 data put Asian robberies at a seven-year low (straits down 74%), credited to coordinated enforcement and shipowner care. Reliability: Independent trade report that matches Splash247 but is paywalled and adds no new data.
- **Lloyd's List (2025). Singapore Strait armed robbery wave continues into 2025.** Lloyd's List. [link](https://www.lloydslist.com/LL1152373/Singapore-Strait-armed-robbery-wave-continues-into-2025) — **Grade B2**. Summary: ReCAAP counted 11 incidents by 3 February 2025, after 27 in the last quarter of 2024 against 7 a year before; some robbers carried guns or knives. Reliability: Leading shipping paper, but paywalled and the date is unconfirmed.
- **Lloyd's List (2025). Crew injured in Singapore Strait tanker boarding amid surge in armed robberies.** Lloyd's List. [link](https://www.lloydslist.com/LL1152727/Crew-injured-in-Singapore-Strait-tanker-boarding-amid-surge-in-armed-robberies) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A crewman on the tanker *Basset* was hurt around 28 February 2025, which is rare; security firm EOS counted 62 incidents in 2024 and 20 by late February 2025. Reliability: Leading trade paper, but EOS sells protection, so it has a reason to stress the threat.
- **The Maritime Executive (2025). ReCAAP sounds alarm about tripling of robberies in Singapore Strait in 2025.** The Maritime Executive. [link](https://maritime-executive.com/article/recaap-sounds-alarm-about-tripling-of-robberies-in-singapore-strait-in-2025) — **Grade C1**. Summary: On 3 February 2025 ReCAAP reported 11 eastbound-lane robberies since 1 January against 4 a year earlier, with 4 involving guns or gun-like objects. Reliability: US trade press accurately reporting a ReCAAP alert, though a five-week window overstates the trend.
- **The Maritime Executive (2026). Piracy surged in 2025 around Singapore, but ReCAAP sees positive indicators.** The Maritime Executive. [link](https://maritime-executive.com/article/piracy-surged-in-2025-around-singapore-but-recaap-sees-positive-indicators) — **Grade C1**. Summary: 2025 was the worst year since 2007 with 108 incidents (+74%); about 87% came in January–July, then cases fell after Indonesian police arrested gangs. Reliability: Clear trade summary of ReCAAP's annual report, but second-hand, so cite ReCAAP itself.
- **SAFETY4SEA (2026). ReCAAP ISC Annual Report: a total 132 incidents reported during 2025.** SAFETY4SEA. [link](https://safety4sea.com/recaap-isc-annual-report-a-total-132-incidents-reported-during-2025/) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Sums up ReCAAP's 2025 figures: 132 incidents in Asia and 108 in the straits, 107 of them in the Singapore Strait rather than Malacca itself. Reliability: Trade summary of a ReCAAP press release that adds nothing beyond the original.
- **Splash247 (2026). Singapore Strait crackdown sends Asian sea robberies to seven-year low.** Splash247. [link](https://splash247.com/singapore-strait-crackdown-sends-asian-sea-robberies-to-seven-year-low/) — **Grade C1**. Summary: In January–June 2026 Asian incidents fell to 35 (−64%) and straits incidents to 21 (−74%), which ReCAAP credited to coastal-state enforcement and industry precautions. Reliability: Trade press confirmed by ReCAAP's half-year release; the credit comes from ReCAAP's own director.
- **inforMARE (2006). Lloyd's Joint War Committee removes the Malacca Strait from its listed areas.** inforMARE. [link](https://www.informare.it/news/review/2006/b090806uk.asp) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Insurers took the strait off their war-risk list from 7 August 2006, after about 14 months; ports in north-east Sumatra stayed listed. Reliability: Italian shipping press backed by a London P&I Club circular; the delisting is insurers' judgement, not proof that patrols caused it.
- **Business Insurance / Malaysiakini (2006). Strait of Malacca off high-risk list / Dropping of Malacca Strait "war risk" listing hailed.** Business Insurance and Malaysiakini. [link](https://www.businessinsurance.com/strait-of-malacca-off-high-risk-list/) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Lloyd's credited better security after Singapore and Malaysia stepped up naval and air patrols, and Malaysia's defence minister welcomed cheaper insurance for ships. Reliability: US insurance trade press plus an independent Malaysian outlet; the minister's praise is self-congratulatory and dates are unconfirmed.
- **The Maritime Executive (2005). [Insurance rates for Malacca Strait transits].** The Maritime Executive. [link](https://maritime-executive.com/article/2005-07-06insurance-rates-for-malacca-strait-tra) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Reports the insurance effect after Lloyd's listed the strait as a war-risk area in June 2005; other reports say this added about 1% of cargo value. Reliability: Trade press, but the headline is cut off and the 1% figure comes from other reports.
- **PortNews (2026). Piracy and sea robbery in Malacca-Singapore Straits hit 19-year high in 2025, ReCAAP says.** PortNews. [link](https://en.portnews.ru/news/386610/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: 108 incidents in the straits in 2025, up 74% from 62, mostly opportunistic night-time theft in which often nothing was taken. Reliability: Russian shipping trade press (English edition) with no state angle on this topic, repeating ReCAAP data second-hand.
- **SAFETY4SEA (2026). ReCAAP ISC Q3: 58% drop in Asian piracy and armed robbery incidents.** SAFETY4SEA. [link](https://safety4sea.com/recaap-isc-q3-58-drop-in-asian-piracy-and-armed-robbery-incidents/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Asian incidents in January–September 2026 fell to 50 from 120 (−58%), with 23 in the straits, while Philippine cases rose from 2 to 12, so the problem may be moving. Reliability: Trade press repeating ReCAAP; date unconfirmed.
- **Seatrade Maritime (2026). Malacca Strait vessel traffic at record levels in 2025.** Seatrade Maritime. [link](https://www.seatrade-maritime.com/tankers/malacca-strait-vessel-traffic-at-record-levels-in-2025) — **Grade C2**. Summary: A record 102,525 ships passed through the straits in 2025 (+8.7%), so the robberies did not reduce trade. Reliability: Trade press using Malaysian Marine Department data; the date is approximate.
- **SAFETY4SEA (2026). ReCAAP ISC: one incident of armed robbery 29 September – 5 October.** SAFETY4SEA. [link](https://safety4sea.com/recaap-isc-one-incidents-of-armed-robbery-29-september-5-october/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: In one week ReCAAP recorded just one incident in all of Asia, a bulk carrier boarded in the Singapore Strait with no one hurt and nothing stolen. Reliability: Trade summary of a weekly bulletin, and one week is not a trend.
- **StrategyPage (2005). Sea transportation: eye in the sky over the Straits of Malacca.** StrategyPage. [link](https://www.strategypage.com/htmw/htseamo/articles/20051217.aspx) — **Grade D3**. Summary: Patrol rules for Eyes in the Sky were only agreed on 6 December 2005, months after the September launch, showing a slow start. Reliability: Small US defence website with unsigned articles and unclear sourcing, so check elsewhere first.

### Regional press (Malaysia, Singapore, Indonesia and others)

- **The Star (2026). Seaborne robbery cases in the Straits of Malacca and Singapore highest since 2007.** The Star (Malaysia). [link](https://www.thestar.com.my/aseanplus/aseanplus-news/2026/01/10/seaborne-robbery-cases-in-the-straits-of-malacca-and-singapore-highest-since-2007) — **Grade C1**. Summary: ReCAAP's 2025 figures: 108 incidents, the most since 2007 and 74% more than 2024, but mostly petty theft and not a bigger threat to shipping. Reliability: Malaysian paper linked to a governing party, possibly reprinting the Straits Times; relies only on ReCAAP.
- **The Star (2026). Mohd Khaled: Malacca Strait remains secure despite global tensions.** The Star (Malaysia). [link](https://www.thestar.com.my/news/nation/2026/04/19/mohd-khaled-malacca-strait-remains-secure-despite-global-tensions) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Malaysia's defence minister said the strait stays secure despite the Iran war and the Hormuz closure. Reliability: Official reassurance meant to calm markets, confirmed as said, but not an independent risk check.
- **New Straits Times (2026). Malaysia rejects external control of Strait of Malacca.** New Straits Times (Malaysia). [link](https://www.nst.com.my/amp/news/nation/2026/04/1424560/malaysia-rejects-external-control-straits-malacca) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Malaysia's defence minister said any action on the strait must be agreed by the region, rejecting outside control after Indonesia floated transit fees. Reliability: Government position only, but confirmed by Singapore, Indonesian and Thai reports.
- **Malay Mail (2025). Piracy surges 83pc in Asian waters, with most attacks in Straits of Malacca and Singapore.** Malay Mail (Malaysia). [link](https://www.malaymail.com/news/singapore/2025/07/10/piracy-surges-83pc-in-asian-waters-with-most-attacks-in-straits-of-malacca-and-singapore/183499) — **Grade C1**. Summary: ReCAAP's half-year report: 80 incidents in the straits in January–June 2025 against 21 a year earlier; Asia-wide incidents up 83%. Reliability: Independent Malaysian outlet repeating ReCAAP data, confirmed by the Bangkok Post.
- **Malay Mail (2026). No parallel with Hormuz situation in Malacca Strait, says defence minister.** Malay Mail (Malaysia). [link](https://www.malaymail.com/news/malaysia/2026/04/19/no-parallel-with-hormuz-situation-in-malacca-strait-says-defence-minister/216858) — **Grade C1**. Summary: The defence minister said there is "no reason" for Malacca to face what happened at Hormuz. Reliability: Independent outlet reporting the official line, which The Star also confirms.
- **Malay Mail (2026). Could the Strait of Malacca be next? What Hormuz's closure means for the world's busiest chokepoint.** Malay Mail (Malaysia). [link](https://www.malaymail.com/news/malaysia/2026/04/23/could-the-strait-of-malacca-be-next-what-hormuzs-closure-means-for-the-worlds-busiest-chokepoint/217417) — **Grade C1**. Summary: About 23.2 million barrels of oil a day passed through Malacca in early 2025 (Hormuz: 20.9 million), with over 102,500 ships in 2025; crime rose to at least 104 incidents, then fell in 2026. Reliability: Probably Reuters-based; figures match other reports but come second-hand from EIA and ReCAAP.
- **Free Malaysia Today (2026). 75% increase in piracy, robbery cases in Straits of Melaka, S'pore.** Free Malaysia Today (Malaysia). [link](https://www.freemalaysiatoday.com/category/nation/2026/01/10/75-increase-in-piracy-robbery-cases-in-straits-of-melaka-spore) — **Grade C1**. Summary: ReCAAP said incidents rose almost 75% in 2025, mostly opportunistic theft that "does not indicate a corresponding increase in threat to maritime trade". Reliability: Independent outlet confirming The Star's report, but second-hand.
- **The Straits Times (2004). US plan to put Marines and special forces in the Malacca Straits.** The Straits Times (Singapore), via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/straitstimes20040405-1) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Reported the US plan, after Admiral Fargo's testimony, to put Marines and special forces on fast boats in the strait, and asked if the region would accept it. Reliability: Archive copy from 2004 linking to the whole day's paper, so find the exact article and headline.
- **The Straits Times (2004). Malaysia rules out direct foreign policing of the strait.** The Straits Times (Singapore), via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/straitstimes20040428-1) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Malaysia rejected foreign forces policing the strait: "The issue of security should not be an excuse to compromise on sovereignty." Reliability: Government-linked Singapore paper from the time; whole-day issue link, so check the exact article.
- **Today (2005). Four-nation air patrols (Eyes in the Sky) set to begin.** Today (Singapore), via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/today20050909-1) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Joint air patrols by four countries over the strait were about to begin, to fight piracy and terrorism. Reliability: State-owned Singapore paper from the time, confirmed by the MINDEF release; whole-day issue link.
- **The Business Times (2006). Relief as war-risk rating for Malacca Strait is scrapped.** The Business Times (Singapore), via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/biztimes20060809-1) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Insurers dropped the strait's war-risk rating in August 2006, about two years after MALSINDO began, and shipowners welcomed the end of extra premiums. Reliability: Widely confirmed report from the time, but the link opens the whole issue and premium levels are not given.
- **The Straits Times (2006). ReCAAP information-sharing centre to open by December.** The Straits Times (Singapore), via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/straitstimes20060421-1) — **Grade C1**. Summary: The ReCAAP Information Sharing Centre would open in Singapore by December 2006 as a network for 16 Asian nations. Reliability: Government-linked paper from the time, confirmed by later launch coverage; whole-day issue link.
- **Today (2006). ReCAAP Information Sharing Centre launched.** Today (Singapore), citing CNA, via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/today20061130-2) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Reported the launch of the Singapore-based centre set up under the ReCAAP agreement on 29 November 2006. Reliability: State-owned outlet, but the fact is uncontroversial; whole-day issue link.
- **The Straits Times (2026). "Strategically aligned": Singapore, Indonesia want Strait of Malacca open and free for all, say leaders.** The Straits Times via Asia News Network (Singapore). [link](https://asianews.network/strategically-aligned-singapore-indonesia-want-strait-of-malacca-open-and-free-for-all-say-leaders/) — **Grade C1**. Summary: PM Lawrence Wong and President Prabowo said the strait must "always remain open to all", naming robbery, piracy, accidents and pollution as threats. Reliability: Summit coverage by a paper close to Singapore's government, confirmed by Indonesian reports.
- **The Jakarta Post (2026). Hormuz crisis throws spotlight on the Malacca Strait.** The Jakarta Post (Indonesia). [link](https://www.thejakartapost.com/world/2026/04/24/hormuz-crisis-throws-spotlight-on-the-malacca-strait) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Malacca is the world's biggest oil chokepoint; over 102,500 ships used it in 2025, and crime rose to at least 104 incidents before falling in 2026. Reliability: Independent Indonesian paper running wire copy, so no local reporting; figures match Malay Mail.
- **The Jakarta Post (2026). US warship passes through Malacca Strait, TNI says.** The Jakarta Post (Indonesia). [link](https://www.thejakartapost.com/world/2026/04/20/us-warship-passes-through-malacca-strait-tni-says.html) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Indonesia's armed forces confirmed a US warship crossed the strait during the Iran war and said this followed international law. Reliability: Military-sourced but reported by two outlets (also Antara); little detail.
- **Jakarta Globe (2026). Indonesia, Singapore vow no Strait of Malacca blockade.** Jakarta Globe (Indonesia). [link](https://jakartaglobe.id/news/indonesia-singapore-vow-no-strait-of-malacca-blockade) — **Grade C1**. Summary: The two governments pledged never to block the strait, and Prabowo said it must stay free of pollution, accidents and piracy. Reliability: Conglomerate-owned paper reporting official pledges confirmed elsewhere; undated.
- **Jakarta Globe (2026). Indonesia denies Malacca Strait tax plan after finance minister's joke.** Jakarta Globe (Indonesia). [link](https://jakartaglobe.id/news/indonesia-denies-malacca-strait-tax-plan-after-finance-ministers-joke) — **Grade C1**. Summary: The government called the toll idea a joke; a second article sets out the UNCLOS legal problems and the risk of a shipping boycott. Reliability: Conglomerate-owned paper whose "joke" framing follows the government line; confirmed by other outlets.
- **Bangkok Post (2025). Ship piracy, robberies up 83% this year in SE Asia.** Bangkok Post (Thailand). [link](https://www.bangkokpost.com/business/general/3066820/ship-piracy-robberies-up-83-this-year-in-se-asia) — **Grade C1**. Summary: ReCAAP's first-half 2025 figures: 80 incidents in the straits against 21, and ReCAAP's director urged more patrols at hotspots. Reliability: Private Thai paper repeating ReCAAP data, confirmed by Malay Mail; date inferred.
- **The Nation (2026). Indonesia floats Malacca Strait levy, Singapore and Malaysia push back.** The Nation (Thailand). [link](https://www.nationthailand.com/news/asean/40065385) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Reports the toll row; Indonesia's finance minister later played the idea down because of legal limits. Reliability: Private Thai paper with a regional round-up based on Straits Times reporting; confirmed but adds little new.
- **The Star (2025). No pirates in Malaysian waters, only petty crooks, Dewan Rakyat told.** The Star (Malaysia). [link](https://www.thestar.com.my/news/nation/2025/10/28/no-pirates-in-malaysian-waters-only-petty-crooks-dewan-rakyat-told) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Malaysia's home minister told Parliament the country has no pirates, only small thieves, since legally "piracy" happens only on the high seas. Reliability: A minister defending Malaysia's image, reported without challenge by a party-linked paper; shows how definitions shape numbers.
- **The Star (2025). Anti-piracy watchdog logs 94 robberies on ships in Straits of Malacca and Singapore as of July 2025.** The Star (Malaysia). [link](https://www.thestar.com.my/news/nation/2025/08/06/anti-piracy-watchdog-logs-94-robberies-on-ships-in-straits-of-malacca-and-singapore-as-of-july-2025) — **Grade C2**. Summary: ReCAAP counted 94 incidents in the straits in the first seven months of 2025, about 85% of Asia's total, with more thefts of scrap metal from tugs and barges. Reliability: Straight data report, snippet only and not independently confirmed.
- **New Straits Times (2026). Hormuz crisis throws spotlight on world's largest "chokepoint": Strait of Malacca.** New Straits Times (Malaysia). [link](https://www.nst.com.my/opinion/columnists/2026/04/1424605/hormuz-crisis-throws-spotlight-worlds-largest-chokepoint-strait) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Argues the Hormuz closure forced Asian policymakers to ask how safe Malacca, the busiest trade waterway, really is. Reliability: Probably a Reuters analysis in the columns section (same headline ran elsewhere), so cite the wire if found.
- **New Straits Times (2026). Illegal crude transfers intensify in Strait of Malacca.** New Straits Times (Malaysia). [link](https://www.nst.com.my/news/nation/2026/04/1418744/illegal-crude-transfers-intensify-strait-malacca) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Illegal ship-to-ship oil transfers linked to the sanctioned "shadow fleet" grew in the strait during the Hormuz crisis, a new kind of threat. Reliability: Based on enforcement-agency sources; no figures were visible.
- **New Straits Times (2026). Evolving threats push RMAF to strengthen maritime watch.** New Straits Times (Malaysia). [link](https://www.nst.com.my/amp/news/nation/2026/09/1537211/evolving-threats-push-rmaf-strengthen-maritime-watch-watch) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Malaysia's air force is increasing sea surveillance, the modern successor to Eyes in the Sky; it says over 80% of Malaysia's trade uses the strait. Reliability: Positive military-sourced feature; the 80% figure is unchecked.
- **Malay Mail (2026). Asia's busiest trade route, the Straits of Malacca, draws scrutiny as security risks rise.** Malay Mail (Malaysia). [link](https://www.malaymail.com/news/malaysia/2026/04/18/asias-busiest-trade-route-the-straits-of-malacca-draws-scrutiny-as-security-risks-rise/216779) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Explains why the Hormuz crisis raised attention on Malacca, quoting Malaysia's foreign minister that joint patrols keep the waterway open. Reliability: Independent outlet, probably wire-based; snippet only.
- **Free Malaysia Today (2026). Hormuz blockade stirs tension over Straits of Malacca.** Free Malaysia Today (Malaysia). [link](https://www.freemalaysiatoday.com/category/nation/2026/04/17/hormuz-blockade-stirs-tension-over-straits-of-malacca) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The Hormuz blockades revived worries about Malacca, only about 2.7 km wide at its narrowest; one analyst saw "no clear and present danger", another called Malacca more critical. Reliability: Independent outlet balancing analysts' opinions, not data.
- **The Straits Times (2004). Malaysia and Indonesia to have "final say" on a US presence.** The Straits Times (Singapore), via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/straitstimes20040408-1) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Malaysian Foreign Minister Syed Hamid Albar said Malaysia and Indonesia would decide whether any US military presence was needed. Reliability: Report from the time, but a whole-day issue link and snippet only.
- **The Straits Times (2004). US ambassador: no plan to deploy troops.** The Straits Times (Singapore), via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/straitstimes20040421-1) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The US ambassador to Indonesia said the US had no plan to send troops to the strait, showing the US backing down within weeks. Reliability: Report from the time; whole-day issue link and the quote is unverified.
- **The Straits Times (2004). Singapore's calls for collective action meet "cool responses".** The Straits Times (Singapore), via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/straitstimes20040522-1) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Singapore kept urging Malaysia and Indonesia to act together against sea terrorism but got a cool reply, showing barriers before MALSINDO. Reliability: Singapore-government-linked view; whole-day issue link.
- **The Business Times (2005). Malaysia says the strait is safe for seafarers.** The Business Times (Singapore), via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/biztimes20050913-1) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Malaysia played down fears and called the strait safe while the Lloyd's war-risk listing was in force. Reliability: Business paper from the time; whole-day issue link and snippet only.
- **The Business Times (2006). Malacca Strait stays on Lloyd's Joint War Committee risk list.** The Business Times (Singapore), via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/biztimes20060120-1) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Insurers ignored months of lobbying by the coastal states and kept the strait on the war-risk list, showing premiums fell only slowly. Reliability: Report from the time; whole-day issue link, so check against Lloyd's records.
- **The Straits Times (2026). Indonesia, Malaysia, and Singapore building on efforts to keep Malacca Strait open and safe.** The Straits Times via Asia News Network (Singapore). [link](https://asianews.network/indonesia-malaysia-and-singapore-building-on-efforts-to-keep-malacca-strait-open-and-safe/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: With Hormuz traffic below a tenth of normal, "spillover anxiety" reached Southeast Asia; over 100,000 ships a year carry almost a quarter of sea trade through the straits. Reliability: Undated, pro-government framing and rounded figures.
- **The Straits Times (2024). 100 incidents of armed robbery against ships in Asia recorded in 2023.** The Straits Times via Asia News Network (Singapore). [link](https://asianews.network/100-incidents-of-armed-robbery-against-ships-in-asia-recorded-in-2023/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: 63 of Asia's 100 incidents in 2023 were in the straits, the start of the rise that peaked in 2025. Reliability: Data report repeating ReCAAP, with the date inferred.
- **Asia News Network (2026). No unilateral moves on Strait of Malacca, says Malaysia after Indonesia floats toll idea.** Asia News Network (regional). [link](https://asianews.network/no-unilateral-moves-on-strait-of-malacca-says-malaysia-after-indonesia-floats-toll-idea/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Malaysia said passage "is not a toll to be paid; it is a right", and Singapore said the three states have "a cooperative mechanism, not to collect tolls, but to keep it open". Reliability: Corroborated story, but the original paper is unclear and the Singapore quote comes from other coverage.
- **The Jakarta Post (2026). The Malacca Strait runs the world.** The Jakarta Post (Indonesia), opinion. [link](https://www.thejakartapost.com/opinion/2026/05/05/the-malacca-strait-runs-the-world.html) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Argues the strait is central to world trade, citing an estimate of about 22% of world sea trade. Reliability: Opinion piece in an independent paper; the figure was seen only in a snippet.
- **The Jakarta Post (2012). The need for cooperation in the Malacca Strait.** The Jakarta Post (Indonesia). [link](https://www.thejakartapost.com/news/2012/07/19/the-need-cooperation-malacca-strait.html) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Says the US RMSI failed because Malaysia and Indonesia rejected it, and calls the coastal states' 2004 arrangement the best example of cooperation. Reliability: One-sided Indonesian retrospective written years later.
- **Kompas (2026). Arus minyak di Selat Malaka turun tajam, apa dampaknya ke Indonesia? (Oil flow in the Malacca Strait drops sharply).** Kompas (Indonesia). [link](https://money.kompas.com/read/2026/08/19/145051526/arus-minyak-di-selat-malaka-turun-tajam-apa-dampaknya-ke-indonesia) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Using EIA data, oil through Malacca fell to about 16.6 million barrels a day in Q2 2026 from 23.2 million in early 2025, because less Gulf oil moved east. Reliability: Independent Indonesian paper, but the 16.6 figure has only one source, so check EIA.
- **Kompas (2026). Pejabat Iran singgung Selat Malaka bisa bernasib seperti Selat Hormuz (Iranian official hints Malacca could suffer Hormuz's fate).** Kompas (Indonesia). [link](https://internasional.kompas.com/read/2026/04/21/151130670/pejabat-iran-singgung-selat-malaka-bisa-bernasib-seperti-selat-hormuz) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Reports Iranian adviser Ali Akbar Velayati suggesting Malacca could face a fate like Hormuz. Reliability: Independent paper reporting one provocative remark, not confirmed elsewhere in the research.
- **Kompas (2026). Oman tawarkan mekanisme pengelolaan Selat Hormuz, tiru Selat Malaka (Oman offers a Hormuz mechanism copying the Malacca Strait).** Kompas (Indonesia). [link](https://www.kompas.com/global/read/2026/07/28/190000770/oman-tawarkan-mekanisme-pengelolaan-selat-hormuz-tiru-selat-malaka) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Oman proposed running Hormuz on the Malacca model (coastal-state cooperation plus voluntary payments from users), and Iran rejected it. Reliability: Independent paper, Indonesian-language and snippet only; check the Omani proposal in international wires.
- **Koran Jakarta (2025). Bakamla RI dan APMM Malaysia gelar rendezvous di Selat Malaka (Indonesian and Malaysian coastguards meet in the strait).** Koran Jakarta (Indonesia). [link](https://koran-jakarta.com/2025-09-20/bakamla-ri-dan-apmm-malaysia-gelar-rendezvous-di-selat-malaka) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The two coastguards met at sea on 19 September 2025 in their 32nd joint patrol, targeting smuggling, illegal fishing and piracy. Reliability: Press-release-style report; the paper's owner was not verified.
- **Bangkok Post (2026). Thailand touts $31 billion "Land Bridge" plan amid Hormuz crisis, courts Singapore.** Bangkok Post (Thailand). [link](https://www.bangkokpost.com/business/general/3244962/thailand-touts-31-billion-land-bridge-plan-amid-hormuz-crisis-courts-singapore) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Thailand revived a land bridge across the Kra Isthmus to bypass Malacca after Hormuz showed how fragile chokepoints are. Reliability: Private paper reporting government promotion; the plan was later reported scrapped.
- **Bangkok Post (c. 2013). Joint patrols to take on Malacca pirates.** Bangkok Post (Thailand). [link](https://www.bangkokpost.com/thailand/politics/384637/joint-patrols-to-take-on-malacca-pirates) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Thailand, Indonesia, Malaysia and Singapore signed an agreement at the 8th Malacca Straits Patrol committee meeting, chaired by a Thai officer. Reliability: Private Thai paper, but the date and details need checking.
- **Bernama (2026). Strait of Malacca must stay open for global trade, says Philippines.** Bernama (Malaysia), reporting the Philippines. [link](https://bernama.com/misc/rss/news.php?id=2553732) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The Philippines, a user state, said the strait must stay open for world trade. Reliability: Malaysian state agency reporting a foreign statement second-hand; headline only and undated.
- **VnExpress International (2026). Thailand to scrap $30B land bridge project on economic loss concerns.** VnExpress International (Vietnam). [link](https://e.vnexpress.net/news/business/economy/thailand-to-scrap-30b-land-bridge-project-on-economic-loss-concerns-5101502.html) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Thailand's finance minister said a new study found the Malacca-bypass land bridge would lose money overall, so the main alternative to the strait failed on cost. Reliability: Business news from a state-controlled press system; undated and not confirmed by a Thai source.
- **VOV (2026). Từ Hormuz đến Malacca: kịch bản "thu phí" liệu có tiếp diễn? (From Hormuz to Malacca: will tolls spread?).** Voice of Vietnam (Vietnam). [link](https://vov.vn/the-gioi/tu-hormuz-den-malacca-kich-ban-thu-phi-lieu-co-tiep-dien-post1313499.vov) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Asks whether Iran's Hormuz fees could spread to Malacca, citing EIA data that 29% of seaborne oil passed through Malacca in early 2025. Reliability: Vietnamese state broadcaster on a foreign topic; figure second-hand from EIA.
- **The Star (2025). Straits of Malacca secure, says MMEA DG amid piracy claims.** The Star (Malaysia). [link](https://www.thestar.com.my/news/nation/2025/02/20/straits-of-malacca-secure-says-mmea-dg-amid-piracy-claims) — **Grade C3**. Summary: The head of Malaysia's coastguard insisted the strait was secure in February 2025, just as ReCAAP showed incidents tripling. Reliability: Self-assessment by the agency responsible; only the headline and a short excerpt were seen.
- **The Star (2026). Malaysia, Indonesia, Singapore and Thailand must resist attempts to weaponise the Strait of Malacca, says expert.** The Star (Malaysia). [link](https://www.thestar.com.my/aseanplus/aseanplus-news/2026/04/26/malaysia-indonesia-singapore-and-thailand-must-resist-attempts-to-weaponise-the-strait-of-malacca-says-expert) — **Grade C3**. Summary: An expert urges the four patrol states to stop Malacca being "weaponised" like Hormuz and notes illegal ship-to-ship oil transfers. Reliability: Opinion from an expert whose name was not visible, so their expertise can't be checked.
- **Bernama (2026). Gulf tensions risk spillover into Strait of Malacca, ASEAN warned.** Bernama (Malaysia). [link](https://asean.bernama.com/news.php?id=2549795) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Warns that Gulf disruption could push 10–20% more ships through Southeast Asia and that chokepoints could be "weaponised". Reliability: Malaysian state news agency; the projection clashes with Kompas data showing oil flows fell.
- **Malaysiakini (c. 2005). Strait war-risk rating will affect economies.** Malaysiakini (Malaysia). [link](https://www.malaysiakini.com/news/54777) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Malaysia's reaction to the Lloyd's war-risk listing: the transport minister said higher premiums would hit consumers and make Malaysian ports less competitive. Reliability: Independent outlet from the time, but the quote and date come from search summaries; paywalled.
- **Malaysiakini (c. 2004–06). Najib: US will not interfere in Malacca Strait security.** Malaysiakini (Malaysia). [link](https://www.malaysiakini.com/news/51238) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Then-defence minister Najib Razak said the US would not interfere in the strait's security, the "sovereignty first" outcome of the RMSI row. Reliability: Undated report of a minister's claim about US intentions.
- **Malaysiakini (c. 2005–06). Less piracy in Malacca Straits.** Malaysiakini (Malaysia). [link](https://www.malaysiakini.com/news/43847) — **Grade C3**. Summary: IMB director Pottengal Mukundan linked the late-2005 drop to more Indonesian national patrols and warned piracy returns "when it goes off the agenda". Reliability: Independent outlet quoting IMB, but snippet only, undated, and attack counts differ between reports.
- **New Straits Times (2026). Importance of Strait of Malacca growing as war rages in Mideast.** New Straits Times (Malaysia), opinion. [link](https://www.nst.com.my/amp/opinion/columnists/2026/03/1392388/importance-strait-malacca-growing-war-rages-mideast) — **Grade C3**. Summary: A Malaysian view that the Hormuz war raises Malacca's strategic weight and the coastal states' responsibility. Reliability: Opinion column by an unnamed author in an establishment-leaning daily.
- **Jakarta Globe (n.d.). Indonesia fears piracy surge could turn regional waters into "new Somalia".** Jakarta Globe (Indonesia). [link](https://jakartaglobe.id/news/indonesia-fears-piracy-surge-turn-regional-waters-new-somalia) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Indonesian officials warned a piracy surge could make regional waters a "new Somalia", probably over Abu Sayyaf kidnappings in the Sulu Sea. Reliability: Undated and probably about the Sulu Sea, not Malacca, so check the page before using it.
- **Jakarta Globe (n.d.). Commentary: Indonesian joint naval patrols haven't halted pirates.** Jakarta Globe (Indonesia), opinion. [link](https://jakartaglobe.id/opinion/commentary-indonesian-joint-naval-patrols-havent-halted-pirates/) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Argues coordinated naval patrols have not been enough to stop piracy, a dissenting view on joint operations. Reliability: Opinion with unknown author and date.
- **Kompas (2026). Prabowo tambah alutsista baru, pengamat singgung potensi Selat Malaka di-Hormuz-kan (Analyst warns Malacca could be "Hormuz-ised").** Kompas (Indonesia). [link](https://nasional.kompas.com/read/2026/05/19/09510471/prabowo-tambah-alutsista-baru-pengamat-singgung-potensi-selat-malaka-di) — **Grade C3**. Summary: As Prabowo bought new weapons, an analyst warned Malacca could be "Hormuz-ised". Reliability: Independent paper reporting a speculative analyst view.
- **The Nation (c. mid-2010s). How Kra Canal can combat piracy in Straits of Malacca.** The Nation (Thailand), opinion. [link](https://www.nationthailand.com/perspective/30292851) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Argues MALSINDO failed to end piracy because of limits on cross-border pursuit and weak information sharing, claiming 41% of world pirate attacks in 1995–2013 were in Southeast Asia. Reliability: Strongly promotional piece for the Kra Canal; the 41% figure is unchecked.
- **The Star (2005). Malacca Strait among world's safest shipping lanes.** The Star (Malaysia). [link](https://www.thestar.com.my/news/nation/2005/07/11/malacca-strait-among-worlds-safest-shipping-lanes/) — **Grade C4**. Summary: Weeks after the Lloyd's war-risk listing in June 2005, Malaysia's transport minister called claims that the strait was dangerous unfounded. Reliability: A minister's claim that clashes with IMB attack data, so use it only to show Malaysia's framing.
- **The Star (2008). Eye in The Sky shows results.** The Star (Malaysia). [link](https://www.thestar.com.my/news/nation/2008/04/14/eye-in-the-sky-shows-results) — **Grade C4**. Summary: Malaysia's deputy air force chief said Eyes in the Sky worked, linking it to zero piracy in the strait in 2007. Reliability: Military self-praise; the "zero" claim clashes with IMB/ReCAAP counts and ignores the tsunami and Aceh peace deal.
- **The Straits Times (2004). Only one serious attack since MALSINDO patrols began.** The Straits Times (Singapore), via NewspaperSG. [link](https://eresources.nlb.gov.sg/newspapers/digitised/issue/straitstimes20040816-1) — **Grade C4**. Summary: Reported only one serious pirate attack since the three-nation patrols began on 20 July 2004. Reliability: A "success" claim after less than a month, contradicted by later reports of at least 20 sailors kidnapped since May.
- **The Jakarta Post (2026). Could the Strait of Malacca be the next global flashpoint?** The Jakarta Post (Indonesia), opinion. [link](https://www.thejakartapost.com/opinion/2026/04/30/could-the-strait-of-malacca-be-the-next-global-flashpoint) — **Grade C6**. Summary: An academic opinion piece asking whether Hormuz-style disruption could reach Malacca. Reliability: Only the headline was seen, so the author and argument are unknown; read it before citing.
- **The Jakarta Post (2026). How not to securitize the Malacca Strait.** The Jakarta Post (Indonesia), opinion. [link](https://www.thejakartapost.com/opinion/2026/04/28/how-not-to-securitize-the-malacca-strait.html) — **Grade C6**. Summary: Warns against over-militarising the strait in reaction to Hormuz. Reliability: Headline only, so the content cannot be judged until the page is read.
- **The Jakarta Post (2023). The pirates of the Strait.** The Jakarta Post (Indonesia), editorial. [link](https://www.thejakartapost.com/opinion/2023/05/04/the-pirates-of-the-strait.html) — **Grade C6**. Summary: An editorial on the return of robbery in the strait as incidents climbed in 2023, possibly rare Indonesian self-criticism. Reliability: Only the headline was seen, so the content is unknown.
- **The Jakarta Post (2023). Managing power conflict spillover in Straits of Malacca and Singapore.** The Jakarta Post (Indonesia), opinion. [link](https://www.thejakartapost.com/opinion/2023/11/06/managing-power-conflict-spillover-in-straits-of-malacca-and-singapore.html) — **Grade C6**. Summary: Discusses how great-power rivalry could spill into the straits. Reliability: Headline only, so read it before citing anything.
- **Antara (2026). Prabowo, Wong reaffirm open Malacca Strait under UNCLOS.** Antara (Indonesia). [link](https://en.antaranews.com/news/421707/prabowo-wong-reaffirm-open-malacca-strait-under-unclos) — **Grade D1**. Summary: The two leaders agreed to keep the straits open under UNCLOS, and Prabowo added pollution to piracy and accidents as threats. Reliability: Indonesian state agency writing about its own president; the meeting is confirmed, but treat the framing as Indonesia's.
- **Antara (2026). Indonesia rejects Malacca Strait tolls amid global tensions.** Antara (Indonesia). [link](https://en.antaranews.com/news/413549/indonesia-rejects-malacca-strait-tolls-amid-global-tensions) — **Grade D1**. Summary: After the finance minister floated a fee, ministers said Indonesia would not charge tolls: "We have ratified UNCLOS and will uphold the law we have signed." Reliability: State agency doing damage control that plays down the original idea; facts confirmed elsewhere.
- **Antara (2025). Indonesia, Malaysia wrap up coordinated Malacca Strait patrols.** Antara (Indonesia). [link](https://en.antaranews.com/news/391813/indonesia-malaysia-wrap-up-coordinated-malacca-strait-patrols) — **Grade D2**. Summary: The 2025 Indonesia–Malaysia patrol had four sessions watching the sea border, showing the patrols set up in 2004 still run. Reliability: State agency using navy sources, with no results data.
- **Antara (2024). Navy foils piracy attempt on MV African Halcyon.** Antara (Indonesia). [link](https://en.antaranews.com/news/307473/navy-foils-piracy-attempt-on-mv-african-halcyon) — **Grade D2**. Summary: Indonesia's navy stopped a robbery attempt on a ship anchored off Dumai in the strait on 29 February 2024. Reliability: State agency publicising a single navy success, self-reported.
- **Antara (c. 2011–12). Piracy rate in Malacca Strait down sharply: defense minister.** Antara (Indonesia). [link](https://en.antaranews.com/news/78803/piracy-rate-in-malacca-strait-down-sharply-defense-minister) — **Grade D2**. Summary: Indonesia's defence minister credited four-nation sea and air patrols, citing ReCAAP (35 incidents in 2005 to 0 in 2011) and IMB (38 in 2004 to 0). Reliability: State media relaying a minister; check the zero figures in the original reports.
- **Antara (c. 2009). US supports tripartite Malacca Straits security arrangements.** Antara (Indonesia). [link](https://en.antaranews.com/news/67092/us-supports-tripartite-malacca-straits-security-arrangements) — **Grade D3**. Summary: Reports the US backing the coastal states' own patrols instead of its own, and credits them with about a 70% fall in crime by 2009. Reliability: State agency framing the RMSI outcome as an Indonesian win; the 70% figure has one source and the date is unclear.

### World press

- **YaleGlobal Online (2004). Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore launch coordinated patrol of Malacca Strait.** YaleGlobal Online (wire reprint). [link](https://archive-yaleglobal.yale.edu/node/47391) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Reports the July 2004 launch of year-round MALSINDO patrols; each navy gave about 5–7 ships and could chase into neighbours' waters only with permission. Reliability: Wire story reprinted by Yale's news digest; the original agency is not named and ship numbers are from a snippet.
- **Voice of America (2005). Southeast Asian nations to begin air patrols over Malacca Strait.** VOA News, via GlobalSecurity.org. [link](https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/library/news/2005/09/mil-050908-voa01.htm) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore and Thailand agreed the "Eyes in the Sky" air patrols, each giving two aircraft. Reliability: US state-funded broadcaster reposted on a third-party site; facts confirmed by the MINDEF release.
- **Al Jazeera (2005). Piracy disappears in tsunami's wake.** Al Jazeera (also NBC News). [link](https://www.aljazeera.com/amp/news/2005/2/1/piracy-disappears-in-tsunamis-wake) — **Grade B2**. Summary: An IMB official said no attacks had happened in the strait since the December 2004 tsunami: "The tsunami seems to have really succeeded where the joint patrols could not." Reliability: Major broadcaster quoting one official weeks after the event, so a first impression.
- **Foreign Policy (c. 2005–06). Pirates of the Malacca Strait: Lloyd's curse.** Foreign Policy. [link](https://foreignpolicy.com/?p=13589) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Covers the row over the Lloyd's war-risk listing; coastal states said it was made without asking them and mixed up piracy, terrorism and robbery. Reliability: US policy magazine; date and author unconfirmed, snippet only.
- **The Diplomat (2015). Piracy in the Malacca Strait: can ASEAN respond?** The Diplomat. [link](https://thediplomat.com/2015/07/piracy-in-the-malacca-strait-can-asean-respond/) — **Grade C1**. Summary: MALSINDO patrols stay inside each country's waters and cannot chase suspects across borders, and Eyes in the Sky flew few flights with little ability to respond. Reliability: Asia-focused analysis magazine with well-documented points, but dated and the author is not recorded.
- **The Diplomat (2026). The Strait of Malacca: the Indo-Pacific's most critical chokepoint?** The Diplomat. [link](https://thediplomat.com/2026/07/the-strait-of-malacca-the-indo-pacifics-most-critical-chokepoint/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Presents the four-state Malacca patrols as a model of coastal-state-led cooperation backed by user states, with piracy far lower than in past decades. Reliability: Argument-led analysis by an unnamed author; snippet only.
- **The Diplomat (2026). Indonesia and Singapore pledge that Malacca Strait will remain "open to all".** The Diplomat. [link](https://thediplomat.com/2026/07/indonesia-and-singapore-pledge-that-malacca-strait-will-remain-open-to-all/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Prabowo pledged to work with Malaysia and Thailand to keep the strait open and safe from pollution, accidents, robbery and piracy, the opposite of Iran's approach at Hormuz. Reliability: Reports a political pledge, not results.
- **Panneerselvam, P. and Ramkumar, K.G. (2023). Piracy and armed robbery in Southeast Asia: the need for a fresh approach.** The Diplomat. [link](https://channel16.dryadglobal.com/piracy-and-armed-robbery-in-southeast-asia-the-need-for-a-fresh-approach) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Argues that despite MALSINDO and ReCAAP, robbery in the straits keeps rising, so the frameworks need better information sharing and enforcement on land. Reliability: Indian maritime-security scholars making an argument before the 2025 surge; seen via a repost.
- **Asia Times (2026). Thailand seizes on Hormuz fears to push land bridge dream.** Asia Times. [link](https://asiatimes.com/2026/05/thailand-seizes-on-hormuz-fears-to-push-land-bridge-dream/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Thailand markets the Kra land bridge as a Malacca bypass; the piece notes shipowners pay into a voluntary navigation-safety fund for the strait. Reliability: Hong Kong-based analysis repeating promotional Thai claims; the project may never be built.
- **Gulf News (c. 2006). Piracy declines sharply in Malacca Straits.** Gulf News. [link](https://gulfnews.com/business/piracy-declines-sharply-in-malacca-straits-1.224149) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Attacks fell sharply in 2005 (38 to 12, though other counts differ), helped by Indonesian patrols and possibly the tsunami and the Aceh peace deal. Reliability: Gulf paper reporting IMB data; snippet only, date unconfirmed and the counts conflict, so use the IMB report.
- **Taipei Times (2026). How Asia should deal with the "Malacca dilemma".** Taipei Times (Bloomberg Opinion reprint). [link](https://www.taipeitimes.com/News/editorials/archives/2026/04/25/2003856197) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Argues the Hormuz crisis shows Asia's exposure to chokepoints and that Malacca is the bigger strategic risk. Reliability: Opinion column reprint by an unnamed author, so cite Bloomberg Opinion as the original.


## Sources: theory, method, cross-case and Spanish perspectives

88 sources that cover more than one strait, the theory of maritime cooperation, the risk equation, the law of the sea, and Spain's position. Entries marked [Author to check] need the author confirmed on the publisher's page.

### Theory and maritime security

- **Bueger, C. and Edmunds, T. (2017). Beyond seablindness: a new agenda for maritime security studies.** International Affairs 93(6). [link](https://academic.oup.com/ia/article/93/6/1293/4111108) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Argues that politicians and scholars ignored the sea and sets out a research agenda built on lessons from counter-piracy, such as information sharing and capacity-building. It gives you the key vocabulary for your literature review. Reliability: A top, highly cited international relations journal, but it is theory rather than data.
- **Bueger, C. (2015). What is maritime security?** Marine Policy 53. [link](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.marpol.2014.12.005) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Shows that "maritime security" has no agreed meaning, which lets many actors work together without agreeing on everything but can hide political disagreement. Useful for your definitions section. Reliability: One of the most cited papers in the field, free to read.
- **Bueger, C. and Edmunds, T. (2024). Understanding Maritime Security.** Oxford University Press. [link](https://research-information.bris.ac.uk/en/publications/understanding-maritime-security/) — **Grade A2**. Summary: A short textbook that sorts sea threats into state conflict, terrorism and "blue crime" such as piracy, and explains how states cooperate against each. That threat-type split helps explain why cooperation worked against Malacca piracy but struggles against Iran or the Houthis. Reliability: Leading scholars with a top academic publisher.
- **Till, G. (2026). Seapower: A Guide for the Twenty-First Century (5th edn).** Routledge. [link](https://rsis.edu.sg/staff-publication/seapower-a-guide-for-the-twenty-first-century/) — **Grade A2**. Summary: The standard seapower textbook, updated for Ukraine, Gaza and the Indo-Pacific. It covers good order at sea and naval cooperation. Reliability: Written by a senior King's College London professor, though page counts differ between listings.
- **Bueger, C. (2013). Practice, pirates and coast guards: the grand narrative of Somali piracy.** Third World Quarterly 34(10). [link](https://doi.org/10.1080/01436597.2013.851896) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Uses pirates' own interviews to show they justified piracy as "coast guarding" against foreign fishing and dumping. This helps explain why piracy lasted despite naval patrols. Reliability: A peer-reviewed qualitative study, free to read.
- **Shortland, A. and Vothknecht, M. (2011). Combating "maritime terrorism" off the coast of Somalia.** European Journal of Political Economy 27(S1). [link](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ejpoleco.2011.03.004) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Judges the 2008–10 naval mission off Somalia, including EU Atalanta, a partial success, because attacks were pushed out of the guarded corridor into open ocean. A key counter-argument: cooperation can move a threat rather than remove it. Reliability: Peer-reviewed and mixes statistics with interviews.
- **Percy, S. and Shortland, A. (2013). The business of piracy in Somalia.** Journal of Strategic Studies 36(4). [link](https://doi.org/10.1080/01402390.2012.750242) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Argues that Somali piracy is a business run from land, so naval patrols alone cannot end it. A useful limit on the "cooperation works" argument. Reliability: Peer-reviewed in a leading strategic-studies journal.
- **Bensassi, S. and Martínez-Zarzoso, I. (2012). How costly is modern maritime piracy to the international community?** Review of International Economics 20(5). [link](https://doi.org/10.1111/roie.12000) — **Grade A2**. Summary: A trade model of Europe–Asia trade finds that hijackings cut exports by making deliveries less reliable, costing about US$24.5 billion a year. It shows piracy, not just war, has measurable trade costs. Reliability: Peer-reviewed, with a Spanish co-author and a free repository copy.
- **[Author to check] (2015). Learning from piracy: future challenges of maritime security governance.** Global Affairs. [link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/23340460.2015.960170) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Draws lessons from Somali counter-piracy for how sea security should be run. It highlights loose, informal groups like the Contact Group on Piracy as a model. Reliability: Peer-reviewed, but the author is unconfirmed and only snippets were seen.
- **[Author to check] (2013). Communities of security practice at work? The emerging African maritime security regime.** African Security. [link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/19392206.2013.853579) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Argues that the fight against piracy sharply increased maritime cooperation and created early "security communities". Good for the theory of why cooperation forms. Reliability: Peer-reviewed, but the author is unknown and it focuses on Africa.
- **[Author to check] (2019). Piracy studies coming of age: a window on the making of maritime intervention actors.** International Affairs 95(5). [link](https://academic.oup.com/ia/article/95/5/1037/5537420) — **Grade A3**. Summary: A review of piracy research showing how states and coalitions become "maritime intervention actors". A good anchor for your literature review. Reliability: A top journal, but the author still needs checking.
- **[Author to check] (2009). Coalition strategy and the pirates of the Gulf of Aden and the Red Sea.** Comparative Strategy. [link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/01495930903025250) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Analyses how naval coalitions were organised against Somali piracy. An early comparison for later coalitions at the same chokepoint, and for coalition theory generally. Reliability: Peer-reviewed but US-centred and written before outcomes were known.
- **[Authors to check] (2025). The economic impact of piracy: a critical assessment of maritime security and trade disruptions in the Gulf of Guinea.** Maritime Economics & Logistics. [link](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1057/s41278-025-00344-1) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Uses realism and critical theory to explain why responses to piracy are often fragmented and go-it-alone. A useful counter-example where cooperation was weak. Reliability: A leading niche journal, but authors unconfirmed and outside your three cases.
- **[Authors to check] (n.d.). Shipping network disruption, vulnerability and resilience amidst geopolitical tensions.** Maritime Business Review 10(3). [link](https://www.emerald.com/mabr/article/10/3/260/1270557/Shipping-network-disruption-vulnerability-and) — **Grade A6**. Summary: Studies how vulnerable shipping networks are, and how they recover, when politics disrupts them. It may support your theory of chokepoint vulnerability and resilience. Reliability: Peer-reviewed, but only the title was seen, so read the abstract first.
- **[Authors to check] (2026). Come sail your ships around me: wartime trade, distance, and the impact of the global shipping industry on the pacifying effect of trade.** Defence and Peace Economics. [link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/10242694.2026.2665844) — **Grade A6**. Summary: Asks how global shipping and rerouting change the idea that trade keeps the peace. Possible theory support. Reliability: A respected journal, but only the title was seen.
- **[Authors to check] (2023). Assessment of global shipping risk caused by maritime piracy.** Heliyon. [link](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2405844023081963) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Maps piracy risk worldwide and suggests that anti-piracy measures in one area pushed piracy elsewhere rather than ending it. Reliability: A large open-access journal with lighter review, and the displacement point comes from one snippet.
- **Chalk, P. (2008). The Maritime Dimension of International Security: Terrorism, Piracy, and Challenges for the United States.** RAND Corporation (MG-697). [link](https://library.mumbai.bristol.ac.uk/Record/doab-20.500.12854ir-114734) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Explains why piracy and sea terrorism rose after 2000, pointing to weak port security, poor coastal surveillance and busy chokepoints. It also reviews US-led security initiatives. Reliability: A free, well-regarded report, but RAND is US-government-linked.
- **Fishman, E. (2025). Chokepoints: American Power in the Age of Economic Warfare.** Portfolio/Penguin. [link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chokepoints) — **Grade C2**. Summary: About economic chokepoints (finance, technology, energy) used in sanctions on Iran, Russia and China, not mainly sea straits. Use it only for short context on why states use chokepoints as leverage. Reliability: A well-reviewed popular book by a former US sanctions official, so cite the publisher's page, not Wikipedia.

### Risk methodology (the equation)

- **ISO (2018). ISO 31000:2018 Risk management — Guidelines.** International Organization for Standardization. [link](https://www.iso.org/standard/65694.html) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The international standard that defines risk as the "effect of uncertainty on objectives", described through likelihood and consequences. It justifies "Risk = Likelihood × Severity". Reliability: The official global standard, though it says nothing about cooperation.
- **IEC (2019). IEC 31010:2019 Risk management — Risk assessment techniques (2nd edn).** International Electrotechnical Commission. [link](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=IEC+31010%3A2019+Risk+assessment+techniques) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The companion catalogue to ISO 31000, listing assessment methods including the likelihood/consequence matrix. It says the right method depends on purpose and data. Reliability: An official standard; the link is a search because no URL was recorded.
- **Kaplan, S. and Garrick, B.J. (1981). On the quantitative definition of risk.** Risk Analysis 1(1). [link](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=Kaplan+Garrick+1981+On+the+quantitative+definition+of+risk) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The classic paper defining risk as three questions: what can go wrong, how likely is it, and what are the consequences. It underpins every formula in your equation. Reliability: A foundational, very widely cited peer-reviewed paper; check the page range.
- **Cox, L.A. (2008a). What's wrong with risk matrices?** Risk Analysis 28(2). [link](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1539-6924.2008.01030.x) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Shows that risk matrices give very different risks the same score and that users can rate the same risk in opposite ways. A key warning to state in your evaluation. Reliability: A highly cited peer-reviewed paper in the leading risk journal.
- **National Research Council (2010). Review of the Department of Homeland Security's Approach to Risk Analysis.** National Academies Press. [link](https://doi.org/10.17226/12972) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Found splitting risk into threat, vulnerability and consequence "reasonable", but said simply multiplying them fails for terrorism because a clever enemy adapts. Relevant to adaptive threats like the Houthis. Reliability: An expert review by the US National Academies.
- **Marin-Ferrer, M., Vernaccini, L. and Poljansek, K. (2017). Index for Risk Management INFORM: Concept and Methodology, Version 2017.** EU Publications Office (EUR 28655 EN). [link](https://doi.org/10.2760/094023) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Explains the INFORM index, which combines hazard, vulnerability and "lack of coping capacity" with a geometric mean. It is the model for your cooperation-adjusted equation. Reliability: The official methodology of the EU Joint Research Centre.
- **IPCC (2014). Summary for policymakers, Climate Change 2014: Impacts, Adaptation, and Vulnerability.** Cambridge University Press. [link](https://www.ipcc.ch/report/ar5/wg2/) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Defines risk as hazards interacting with the exposure and vulnerability of systems, including lack of capacity to cope. Supports treating cooperation as coping capacity. Reliability: An authoritative, heavily reviewed UN scientific assessment.
- **UNGA (2016). Report of the open-ended intergovernmental expert working group on indicators and terminology relating to disaster risk reduction (A/71/644).** United Nations. [link](https://undocs.org/A/71/644) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The UN's official disaster-risk terms, treating risk as a function of hazard, exposure, vulnerability and capacity. Supports giving capacity its own place in the equation. Reliability: An official UN document agreed by governments.
- **IMO (2002). Guidelines for Formal Safety Assessment (FSA) for use in the IMO rule-making process (MSC/Circ.1023).** International Maritime Organization. [link](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=IMO+MSC%2FCirc.1023+Formal+Safety+Assessment+guidelines) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The original shipping risk method, using a log-scaled matrix where Risk Index = Frequency Index + Severity Index. Shows that adding log scores equals multiplying. Reliability: A primary IMO document, though the matrix detail was taken from secondary sources.
- **IMO (2018). Revised Guidelines for Formal Safety Assessment (MSC-MEPC.2/Circ.12/Rev.2).** International Maritime Organization. [link](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=MSC-MEPC.2%2FCirc.12%2FRev.2+Formal+Safety+Assessment) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The current IMO risk guidelines, defining risk as frequency combined with severity and requiring incident data to be checked for reliability. Reliability: A primary document from the UN shipping agency; the link is a search.
- **OECD/JRC (Nardo, M. et al.) (2008). Handbook on Constructing Composite Indicators: Methodology and User Guide.** OECD Publishing. [link](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=Handbook+on+Constructing+Composite+Indicators+OECD+2008) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The standard guide to building an index step by step: choosing data, normalising, weighting, combining and testing sensitivity. It guides how you build and test your score. Reliability: An official OECD and EU handbook, widely used.
- **Saisana, M., Saltelli, A. and Tarantola, S. (2005). Uncertainty and sensitivity analysis techniques as tools for the quality assessment of composite indicators.** Journal of the Royal Statistical Society: Series A 168(2). [link](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-985X.2005.00350.x) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Shows how to test whether an index's rankings survive changes in weights and methods. Backs up your sensitivity test. Reliability: A peer-reviewed paper in a top statistics journal.
- **Cox, L.A. (2008b). Some limitations of "Risk = Threat × Vulnerability × Consequence" for risk analysis of terrorist attacks.** Risk Analysis 28(6). [link](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=Cox+2008+limitations+Risk+%3D+Threat+%C3%97+Vulnerability+%C3%97+Consequence) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Lists four problems with multiplying threat, vulnerability and consequence, including ignoring links between them and subjective scores. Reliability: Peer-reviewed in the leading risk journal; details seen only in search results.
- **Willis, H.H. (2007). Guiding resource allocations based on terrorism risk.** Risk Analysis 27(3). [link](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1539-6924.2007.00909.x) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Extends terrorism risk scores to decisions about where to spend defence money and how much risk to accept. Reliability: Peer-reviewed, but written for US homeland security rather than shipping.
- **Pristrom, S., Yang, Z., Wang, J. and Yan, X. (2016). A novel flexible model for piracy and robbery assessment of merchant ship operations.** Reliability Engineering & System Safety 155. [link](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=Pristrom+2016+novel+flexible+model+for+piracy+and+robbery+assessment) — **Grade A2**. Summary: A probability model of a ship being hijacked off East Africa, including the security measures in place. It shows cooperation and protection act on likelihood. Reliability: Peer-reviewed engineering journal, but it needs ship-level data beyond an EPQ.
- **CISA (n.d.). Risk Analysis and Management for Critical Asset Protection (RAMCAP).** US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency. [link](https://www.cisa.gov/mts-resilience-resources/risk-analysis-and-management-critical-asset-protection-ramcap) — **Grade A2**. Summary: The US infrastructure risk method that multiplies threat, vulnerability and consequence. It is the source of the T × V × C formula. Reliability: An official US government page, though the formula detail is partly confirmed via other sources.
- **JRC DRMKC (n.d.). INFORM Risk: Methodology.** European Commission. [link](https://drmkc.jrc.ec.europa.eu/inform-index/INFORM-Risk/Methodology) — **Grade A2**. Summary: The web summary of INFORM's three dimensions and how they are combined. A quick companion to Marin-Ferrer et al. (2017). Reliability: An official EU page, undated, so cite the 2017 report for detail.
- **JRC COIN (2023). Step 6: Aggregation [training slides].** European Commission. [link](https://knowledge4policy.ec.europa.eu/sites/default/files/COIN_2023_Step%206.%20Aggregation.pdf) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Explains that an average lets one strong score cancel a weak one, while a geometric mean punishes unbalanced profiles. Supports your choice of geometric mean. Reliability: Official EU training material, not a formal publication.
- **George, A.L. and Bennett, A. (2005). Case Studies and Theory Development in the Social Sciences.** MIT Press. [link](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=George+Bennett+2005+Case+Studies+and+Theory+Development+in+the+Social+Sciences) — **Grade A2**. Summary: The standard guide to comparing case studies and to process tracing, which you use to separate the effect of cooperation from other causes. Reliability: A leading academic methods book, carried over from an earlier file without a fresh check.
- **Fan, H., Lu, J., Chang, Z. and Ji, Y. (2024). [Bayesian network-based TOPSIS framework for dynamic control of maritime piracy risk].** Maritime Policy & Management 51(7). [link](https://ideas.repec.org/a/taf/marpmg/v51y2024i7p1582-1601.html) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Links a data-driven probability model to a ranking method to compare anti-piracy strategies by how much they cut likelihood. Shows the advanced methods you chose not to use. Reliability: Peer-reviewed, but the exact title still needs copying from RePEc.
- **Willis, H.H., Morral, A.R., Kelly, T.K. and Medby, J.J. (2005). Estimating Terrorism Risk (MG-388).** RAND Corporation. [link](https://doi.org/10.7249/MG388) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Written for US homeland security funding, it recommends defining terrorism risk as expected yearly consequences. A source for the threat-vulnerability-consequence family of formulas. Reliability: A respected, widely cited think-tank study, though US-government-funded.
- **BIMCO, ICS, IGP&I Clubs, INTERTANKO and OCIMF (2018). BMP5: Best Management Practices to Deter Piracy and Enhance Maritime Security.** Witherby Publishing Group. [link](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Security/Documents/BMP5%20small.pdf) — **Grade B1**. Summary: The shipping industry's guide for the Red Sea, Gulf of Aden and Indian Ocean, requiring a threat assessment, then a ship risk assessment, then protective measures. Its order matches threat × vulnerability. Reliability: Primary industry guidance hosted by the IMO, though written by shipping bodies.
- **Arslanalp, S., Koepke, R. and Verschuur, J. (2021). Tracking Trade from Space: An Application to Pacific Island Countries (WP/21/225).** International Monetary Fund. [link](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=Arslanalp+Koepke+Verschuur+2021+Tracking+Trade+from+Space) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Explains how ship-tracking satellite data is turned into trade estimates. It is the method behind PortWatch, so it tells you what those numbers can and cannot show. Reliability: An IMF working paper, expert but not peer-reviewed.
- **ADRC (n.d.). Chapter 2, Section 2.1 [disaster risk].** Asian Disaster Reduction Center. [link](https://www.adrc.asia/publications/LWR/LWR_pdf/Chapter%202%20Section%202_1.pdf) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A teaching version of the formula Risk = Hazard × Vulnerability ÷ Capacity, which puts capacity below the line. Supports treating cooperation as capacity. Reliability: An intergovernmental centre, but undated teaching material.
- **Argus Media (n.d.). Explainer: war risk insurance and AWRP.** Argus Media. [link](https://www.argusmedia.com/en/news-and-insights/latest-market-news/2795769-explainer-war-risk-insurance-and-awrp) — **Grade B2**.
