Summary: Explains that extra war-risk premiums are quoted as a percentage of a ship's hull value and are required in "listed areas". Helps you use insurance as a likelihood measure. Reliability: A major commodity-pricing agency, but undated and only seen in search results.

### International law

- **United Nations (1982). UN Convention on the Law of the Sea (UNCLOS), Part III, Articles 34–45 (transit passage).** UN Division for Ocean Affairs. [link](https://www.un.org/Depts/los/convention_agreements/texts/unclos/part3.htm) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The legal rules for passing through straits, including the right of transit passage and Article 43, which says user and coastal states "should by agreement co-operate". The legal basis for all three straits. Reliability: The primary treaty text, though Iran signed but never ratified it.
- **Treves, T. (2009). Piracy, law of the sea, and use of force: developments off the coast of Somalia.** European Journal of International Law 20(2). [link](https://doi.org/10.1093/ejil/chp027) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Explains how UN Security Council resolutions let navies enter Somali waters, and why navies hesitated to detain pirates over human-rights worries. Useful for the "enforcement of laws" factor. Reliability: Written by a former judge of the international law-of-the-sea tribunal.
- **Guilfoyle, D. (2008). Piracy off Somalia: UN Security Council Resolution 1816 and IMO regional counter-piracy efforts.** International and Comparative Law Quarterly 57(3). [link](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/international-and-comparative-law-quarterly/article/abs/ii-piracy-off-somalia-un-security-council-resolution-1816-and-imo-regional-counterpiracy-efforts/A7C877DE295732AD8D439457F9AA4978) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Covers Resolution 1816, the IMO-led regional cooperation that became the Djibouti Code of Conduct, and the need for Somalia's consent. Good for the formal frameworks factor. Reliability: A leading law journal and a specialist author; paywalled.
- **Kontorovich, E. and Art, S. (2010). An empirical examination of universal jurisdiction for piracy.** American Journal of International Law 104. [link](https://scholarlycommons.law.northwestern.edu/facultyworkingpapers/38) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Using 1998–2009 data, it finds that at most 1.47% of clearly punishable piracies were prosecuted, even though any state may try pirates. Strong evidence that law on paper is not enforcement. Reliability: Peer-reviewed in the top US law journal; pages and DOI unconfirmed.
- **[Author to check] (n.d.). UNCLOS under fire: recalibrating maritime security governance.** International and Comparative Law Quarterly. [link](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/international-and-comparative-law-quarterly/article/unclos-under-fire-recalibrating-maritime-security-governance/CC79F3F984435EEFFF580C342A6B751D) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Argues the law of the sea leaves gaps; for example, UNCLOS piracy rules do not apply in territorial waters, where most Malacca attacks happen. Explains why coastal states control enforcement. Reliability: A high-prestige law journal, but the author and year still need checking.

### Cross-case reports and data

- **Verschuur, J., Lumma, J. and Hall, J.W. (2025). Systemic impacts of disruptions at maritime chokepoints.** Nature Communications 16. [link](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-65403-w) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Estimates that about US$192 billion of trade a year is at risk at 24 chokepoints, counting delays, rerouting, insurance and freight. It puts all three of your cases on one scale. Reliability: A top free journal and an Oxford/TU Delft team, though the figure is a model estimate made before the 2026 Hormuz closure.
- **EIA (2024, updated 2026). World Oil Transit Chokepoints.** US Energy Information Administration. [link](https://www.eia.gov/international/analysis/special-topics/World_Oil_Transit_Chokepoints) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The standard reference for oil volumes through each chokepoint, showing Malacca carries the most oil (about 23 million barrels a day) and Hormuz has few bypass routes. Reliability: Official US government data, free.
- **UNCTAD (2024). Review of Maritime Transport 2024: Navigating Maritime Chokepoints.** United Nations. [link](https://unctad.org/publication/review-maritime-transport-2024) — **Grade A1**. Summary: A whole yearly report on chokepoints: by mid-2024 Gulf of Aden tonnage was down 76% and Suez down 70%, with Cape of Good Hope arrivals up 89%. Poorer and island states suffered most. Reliability: An official UN report, free to use.
- **IMF and University of Oxford (2023–). PortWatch – Chokepoints and Disruptions.** IMF PortWatch. [link](https://portwatch.imf.org) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Free daily ship-transit counts and trade estimates for about 28 chokepoints, made from satellite ship-tracking data. Download it to make your own graphs. Reliability: Official IMF data, but it starts in 2019, arrives late and can miss "dark" ships.
- **Federal Reserve Bank of St Louis (ongoing). FRED: Crude Oil Prices: Brent – Europe (DCOILBRENTEU).** FRED, source US EIA. [link](https://fred.stlouisfed.org/series/DCOILBRENTEU) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Daily Brent oil prices since 1987, downloadable as a spreadsheet. Lets you compare oil prices across the Tanker War, the Red Sea crisis and 2026. Reliability: Official data; for years before 1987 you need annual figures from elsewhere.
- **Lloyd's Market Association (2026). Joint War Committee Listed Areas (incl. circular JWLA-033).** LMA. [link](https://lmalloyds.com/committee/joint-war-committee/) — **Grade A1**. Summary: The insurance market's list of high-risk sea areas, showing when each became "high risk" and needed extra war-risk premiums. Covers the Gulf, Red Sea and, historically, Malacca (listed 2005). Reliability: A primary insurance document, but verify the Malacca dates from a primary source.
- **Carrière-Swallow, Y., Deb, P., Furceri, D., Jiménez, D. and Ostry, J.D. (2023). Shipping costs and inflation.** Journal of International Money and Finance 130. [link](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jimonfin.2022.102771) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Finds that spikes in shipping costs push up import prices and inflation, and last longer than oil shocks. The key link from chokepoint disruption to higher prices in every case. Reliability: IMF economists in a strong, highly cited journal.
- **UNCTAD (2025). Review of Maritime Transport 2025.** United Nations. [link](https://unctad.org/osgstatement/launch-review-maritime-transport-2025) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Sea trade grew 2.2% in 2024, but shipping distances grew 5.9% because of Cape diversions, raising costs and emissions. Routes are moving away from traditional chokepoints. Reliability: An official UN report, though figures were seen via trade-press coverage.
- **ICC International Maritime Bureau (2026). Piracy and Armed Robbery Against Ships: Report for 1 January – 31 December 2025.** ICC-IMB. [link](https://iccwbo.org/news-publications/report/global-maritime-piracy-and-armed-robbery-increased-in-2025/) — **Grade A2**. Summary: Counts 137 piracy incidents worldwide in 2025, 80 of them in the Singapore Straits, and says Somali piracy stayed contained thanks to naval presence. Reliability: A respected industry data source, but it counts differently from ReCAAP.
- **Haralambides, H. (2024). The Red Sea crisis and chokepoints to trade and international shipping.** Maritime Economics & Logistics 26(3). [link](https://doi.org/10.1057/s41278-024-00296-y) — **Grade A6**. Summary: Places the Red Sea crisis alongside other chokepoints to world trade and shipping. Possibly useful for your general comparison. Reliability: A senior expert in a peer-reviewed journal, but only the record was seen, not the text.
- **Bailey, R. and Wellesley, L. (2017). Chokepoints and Vulnerabilities in Global Food Trade.** Chatham House. [link](https://www.chathamhouse.org/about-us/our-departments-and-programmes/environment-and-society-centre/chokepoints-and) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Maps 14 chokepoints and finds over half of traded grain passes through at least one, often with no real alternative route. Useful for exposure and trade concentration. Reliability: A top think tank, widely cited, though focused on food.
- **Drewry (weekly). World Container Index.** Drewry. [link](https://www.drewry.co.uk/supply-chain-advisors/supply-chain-expertise/world-container-index-assessed-by-drewry) — **Grade B2**. Summary: The weekly spot cost of shipping a 40-foot container on eight main routes. Your freight-rate measure, especially for the Red Sea; data start in 2011. Reliability: A respected commercial index, but the URL was not confirmed by search.
- **Ranjan, A. and Shuja, A. (2025). Circumventing the Middle East Chokepoints.** Institute of South Asian Studies, NUS. [link](https://www.isas.nus.edu.sg/books/circumventing-the-middle-east-chokepoints) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Studies how states build new ports such as Duqm, Gwadar and Chabahar to depend less on Hormuz and Bab el-Mandeb. Evidence for physical alternatives. Reliability: An academic think tank close to the Singapore government; only the summary was seen.
- **S&P Global Market Intelligence (2026). Maritime Chokepoints Tighten, Global Supply Chain Risk Rises.** S&P Global. [link](https://www.spglobal.com/market-intelligence/en/news-insights/research/2026/04/maritime-chokepoints-tighten-global-supply-chain-risk-rises) — **Grade B6**. Summary: Panama Canal traffic jumped and Malacca traffic grew during the 2026 Hormuz closure, tightening capacity elsewhere. Shows connected crises across chokepoints. Reliability: A major data firm, but no figures were visible in the snippet.

### Spain and Europe

- **Council of the European Union (2024). Council Decision (CFSP) 2024/3186 amending Decision 2008/851/CFSP (Operation Atalanta).** EUR-Lex. [link](https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/dec/2024/3186/oj) — **Grade A1**. Summary: Extends Spanish-led Atalanta, with its headquarters at Rota, to February 2027 and widens it to parts of the Red Sea, working more closely with Aspides. A direct example of EU information sharing. Reliability: A primary EU legal document, free.
- **Germond, B. and Smith, M.E. (2009). Re-thinking European security interests and the ESDP: explaining the EU's anti-piracy operation.** Contemporary Security Policy 30(3). [link](https://eprints.lancs.ac.uk/id/eprint/40789/) — **Grade A3**. Summary: Explains why the EU launched Operation Atalanta, its first naval mission. Useful background on European motives for cooperation. Reliability: Peer-reviewed, but only the repository record was checked and no DOI was confirmed.
- **Sanz Alisedo, G. (2022). Océanos y choke points, oportunidades y riesgos para el comercio marítimo global.** IEEE Documento de Análisis 68/2022. [link](https://www.ieee.es/Galerias/fichero/docs_analisis/2022/DIEEEA68_2022_GONSAN_Oceanos.pdf) — **Grade B1**. Summary: A Spanish overview of how world trade depends on straits and canals. It explains that war-risk insurance can cover piracy zones, using Lloyd's 2005 listing of Malacca. Reliability: A Spanish Defence Ministry think tank, and the 2005 listing is well documented.
- **Estado Mayor de la Defensa (2025). Operación Atalanta news pages.** EMAD, Spanish Ministry of Defence. [link](https://emad.defensa.gob.es/en/operaciones/operaciones-en-el-exterior/42-ATALANTA/Noticias/listado/250425-atalanta-visita-delegacion-europea-somali-fragata.html) — **Grade B1**. Summary: Spain runs Atalanta from Rota and contributes about 350 personnel, a frigate, special forces and a patrol aircraft in Djibouti. Spain is the only EU state in the mission without a break since 2008. Reliability: An official source on what Spain did, though self-promoting.
- **Ministerio de Hacienda / Merca2 / El Español (2026). Government anti-crisis package: VAT on fuel cut to 10%.** Hacienda press note 521. [link](https://www.hacienda.gob.es/es-es/prensa/noticias/noticiaspdf/521.pdf) — **Grade B1**. Summary: In March 2026 Spain cut fuel VAT from 21% to 10% in a package worth over €5 billion, then phased the help out from July. Shows how policy cushioned the price shock. Reliability: A primary government source backed by news reports.
- **Forbes España / Deia (Europa Press) (2026). Las importaciones de crudo en España caen un 12,7% en febrero y Oriente Medio cubre el 17% del suministro.** Forbes España. [link](https://forbes.es/economia/900842/las-importaciones-de-crudo-en-espana-caen-un-127-en-febrero-y-oriente-medio-cubre-el-17-del-suministro/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Official CORES data show the Middle East supplied 17% of Spain's crude in February 2026 but only 5.5% by May. Clear evidence of Spain switching suppliers. Reliability: News-agency copy of official data; check CORES for the originals.
- **Bolsamanía (Europa Press) / Vozpópuli / Ara (2026). Spanish CPI reports, March–September 2026.** Bolsamanía. [link](https://www.bolsamania.com/noticias_amp/economia/economia--amp-el-ipc-se-dispara-al-33-en-marzo-por-los-carburantes-pero-la-inflacion-subyacente-aguanta-en-el-27--22191310.html) — **Grade B2**. Summary: Spanish inflation rose from 3.3% in March 2026 to a flash 4.9% in September, driven by fuel. Your best Spain-specific price series. Reliability: News-agency copy of official INE data; confirm at ine.es.
- **Fernández, M. (2017). Piratería marítima en el Estrecho de Malaca, Somalia y Nigeria. Causas, consecuencias y respuestas.** Universidad Complutense de Madrid (thesis). [link](https://eprints.ucm.es/id/eprint/45559/) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A Spanish comparison of piracy off Malacca, Somalia and Nigeria to find common causes and which responses worked. Good for comparing regional and naval models. Reliability: An academic thesis, so not peer-reviewed like a journal article.
- **IEEE (2011). El tratamiento jurídico de la piratería marítima en el ordenamiento español.** IEEE Documento Marco 02/2011. [link](https://www.ieee.es/Galerias/fichero/docs_marco/2011/DIEEEM02-2011TratamientoJuridicoPirateria.pdf) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Explains how Spanish law handles the crime of piracy, useful for Atalanta prosecutions and the "enforcement of laws" factor. Reliability: A government think-tank paper, but the author was not shown and it is old.
- **Diari de Tarragona / Esdiario (2026). El Gobierno español estima que el 5% del petróleo y el 2% del GNL procede del estrecho de Ormuz.** Esdiario. [link](https://www.esdiario.com/economia/260306/181650/gobierno-espanol-estima-5-petroleo-2-gnl-procede-estrecho-ormuz.html) — **Grade C1**. Summary: Official CORES data show only about 5% of Spain's 2025 oil imports and 2% of its gas passed through Hormuz. Shows Spain's direct exposure was low. Reliability: Regional news, but the figure is confirmed by other sources.
- **Valencia Plaza / EFE (2024). El Puerto de València recibe el primer gran buque desviado por la situación en el mar Rojo.** Valencia Plaza. [link](https://valenciaplaza.com/valenciaplaza/el-puerto-de-valencia-recibe-el-primer-gran-buque-desviado-por-la-situacion-en-el-mar-rojo) — **Grade C1**. Summary: The first big ship rerouted round Africa reached Valencia, which became a new port of call, while local exporters faced delays and higher freight costs. Reliability: Regional news confirmed by an EFE wire version.
- **Universidad de Navarra (n.d.). Afección de la piratería a las rutas marítimas.** Universidad de Navarra. [link](https://unav.edu/documents/16800098/147587031/afeccion-pirateria-rutas-maritimas.pdf) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Explains how piracy affects sea lanes and notes that 2023 Malacca–Singapore incidents were armed robbery, not piracy in the legal sense. That legal distinction matters for your measures. Reliability: A university paper with no author or date shown.
- **Vivir Ediciones / EFE (2025). Atalanta commander Ignacio Villanueva on piracy trends.** Vivir Ediciones. [link](https://vivirediciones.es/?p=840236) — **Grade C2**. Summary: The Atalanta commander reported 46 pirate attacks in 2024 and a fall in 2025. Useful for incident numbers. Reliability: A repost of EFE wire copy, so check the original.
- **Motorpasión / Coches.com / Motor.es (2026). Spanish petrol and diesel prices during the Hormuz shock.** Motor.es. [link](https://www.motor.es/noticias/gasolina-diesel-espana-shock-ormuz-calma-precios-mayo-2026114105.html) — **Grade C2**. Summary: In March 2026 petrol averaged €1.775 a litre and diesel €1.901, as Brent rose from US$67 to over US$126. Shows the shock at Spanish pumps. Reliability: Motoring press; prefer official price data for figures.
- **Euronews en español / Merca2 (2026). El precio del diésel se dispara en Europa aunque en España sigue siendo de los más baratos.** Euronews. [link](https://es.euronews.com/2026/09/13/el-precio-del-diesel-se-dispara-en-europa-aunque-en-espana-sigue-siendo-de-los-mas-baratos) — **Grade C2**. Summary: By September 2026 Spain had the EU's second-cheapest diesel at €1.795 a litre, against about €2.56 in Germany. Reliability: Mainstream news, but compare with EU Weekly Oil Bulletin figures.
- **The Objective (2023). El puerto de Algeciras se convierte en punto estratégico tras los ataques en el mar Rojo.** The Objective. [link](https://theobjective.com/espana/andalucia/2023-12-24/puerto-algeciras-convierte-punto-estrategico-ataques-mar-rojo/) — **Grade C2**. Summary: As big shipping lines switched to the Cape route, Algeciras gained strategic importance. Shows the disruption redistributing traffic towards Spain. Reliability: National news with a political slant; snippet only.
- **Peyroteo Portela Guedes, H. (2009; 2016). La vuelta de la piratería marítima; La piratería marítima y el drama de los marinos.** Revista General de Marina. [link](https://bibliotecavirtual.defensa.gob.es/BVMDefensa/bibliodef/en/consulta/registro.do?id=303805) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Spanish Navy journal articles on the return of piracy and its human cost to sailors. Shows the navy's own view of anti-piracy cooperation. Reliability: A professional journal, not peer-reviewed, and the pages were not opened.
- **Energías Renovables (2026). La guerra de Ormuz eleva el coste de las importaciones de petróleo de España por encima de los 20.000 millones de euros.** Energías Renovables. [link](https://www.energias-renovables.com/boletines/boletinenergiasrenovables-20260727) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Spain's oil import bill passed €20 billion with Brent near US$100. A rough severity figure for Spain. Reliability: Pro-renewables trade press with an interest in criticising oil dependence.
- **Escudo Digital (2025). España asume el mando de Atalanta con la fragata Numancia tras cinco ataques piratas en diez días.** Escudo Digital. [link](https://www.escudodigital.com/defensa/espana-asume-el-mando-de-atalanta-con-la-fragata-numancia-tras-cinco-ataques-piratas-en-diez-dias.html) — **Grade C4**. Summary: Spain took command of Atalanta after a burst of pirate attacks, which it linked to ships rerouting because of the Houthis. An example of connected crises. Reliability: The date and commander's name conflict with other sources, so check EMAD.
- **Revista General de Marina (2024). [Article on Djibouti and Atalanta], March 2024, part 3.** Armada Española. [link](https://armada.defensa.gob.es/archivo/rgm/2024/03/rgmmar2024_Parte03.pdf) — **Grade C6**. Summary: A Spanish Navy journal piece that appears to describe Spain's Atalanta patrol aircraft based in Djibouti. Reliability: Title and author unverified and content unseen, so open the PDF first.
- **Moncloa.com (2026). El control de Trump sobre el estrecho de Ormuz amenaza el suministro de petróleo a España (and related pieces).** Moncloa.com. [link](https://www.moncloa.com/2026/07/14/estrecho-ormuz-espana-trump-control-3399206/) — **Grade D3**. Summary: A series on Hormuz and Spain, claiming Gulf states supplied over 28% of Spain's crude in 2025 by origin. Reliability: Despite its name it is not the government's website, so prefer CORES and INE data.

### Analysis and opinion

- **Council on Foreign Relations (2026). Conflict-Driven Chokepoint Disruptions (Crisis Response Playbook no. 4).** CFR. [link](https://www.cfr.org/reports/conflict-driven-chokepoint-disruptions) — **Grade B2**. Summary: A playbook for how governments should respond when war closes a chokepoint. It says oil-reserve releases were "insufficient" for a long Hormuz closure and that bypass pipelines take "years, maybe decades". Reliability: A top US think tank with a US policy angle; check the PDF for authors.
- **Kardon, I. [attributed] (2026). How America Lost Command of the Commons.** Foreign Affairs. [link](https://www.foreignaffairs.com/united-states/how-america-lost-command-commons) — **Grade B3**. Summary: Argues the US can no longer keep sea lanes open on its own, using the Red Sea and Hormuz crises as evidence. Read with the Indian reply from VIF. Reliability: A major journal, but the byline comes from a secondary source.
- **The Conversation (2026). How three colonial powers gained and then lost control over the Straits of Hormuz and Bab el-Mandeb.** The Conversation. [link](https://theconversation.com/how-three-colonial-powers-gained-and-then-lost-control-over-the-straits-of-hormuz-and-bab-el-mandeb-292255) — **Grade C2**. Summary: Historical background on how outside powers controlled these straits. Useful context for the non-Western view that foreign navies have long dominated these waters. Reliability: Written by academics for the public, but it is a short explainer.
- **Vivekananda International Foundation (2026). Has America Lost Command of the Commons.** VIF India. [link](https://vifindia.org/category-articles/21085) — **Grade C3**. Summary: An Indian reply to Kardon on what weaker US sea control means for India and Indian Ocean security. Adds a non-Western view. Reliability: A New Delhi think tank close to India's government.
- **Hernandez Diaz, D., Di Fiori, L. and Devesa, T. (2026). Energy Security in an Age of Disruption.** Project Syndicate. [link](https://www.project-syndicate.org/commentary/how-countries-and-companies-can-prepare-for-the-next-energy-shock-by-diego-hernandez-diaz-et-al-2026-09) — **Grade C3**. Summary: Advice on preparing for the next chokepoint shock, arguing that stockpiles and rerouting, not reopening the strait, did most to soften the 2026 crisis. Reliability: An opinion piece whose authors' credentials were not shown.

## Limitations and what to check before citing

Every source here was confirmed to exist, but almost none could be opened in full, so check each figure on the original page before you cite it.

1. **Search snippets only.** The research environment blocked direct access to web pages, so summaries and figures come from search-result text. Credibility grades are capped at 2 unless several independent sources agree. Items marked [BK] or "to check" rely on background knowledge and need checking first.
2. **Missing outlets.** The NYT, Guardian, BBC, FT, El País, The Economist and some Indian papers were blocked from search, so other papers from the same countries stand in for them.
3. **The scores are judgements.** The 1–5 bands, the equal weights and the process-tracing scores are reasoned choices, not measurements. The verdicts survive most changes, with one exception: counting national patrols as cooperation makes cooperation the main reason at Malacca.
4. **Thin historical data.** There is no daily ship-tracking data before 2019, no 1967 war-risk premium, and no freight index for the Tanker War, so some periods use fewer indicators.
5. **Likelihood and severity overlap.** Insurance premiums rise with both the chance and the cost of an attack.
6. **The K term assumes cooperation helps.** It shows the best case for cooperation. The process-tracing table, not the equation, judges whether cooperation caused a change.
7. **The 2026 crises are still going on.** The Hormuz and Red Sea duration scores may rise.
8. **Dates and counts that conflict between sources:** the Hormuz re-closures (April, late June and July 2026); Malacca attacks in 2004 (37 or 38); the Eternity C deaths (3 or 4); and the end of the 1974 Suez clearance (15 or 20 December).
